Theme 1: Unveiling
Plasmodium vivax
dynamics: transmission, genomics & surveillance
T1-O1 A study of low-density malaria parasite infection in asymptomatic individuals in rural Bikaner
Sanjay K. Kochar, Mohammed Rizwan, Niranjana Ranga, Vinay Mehra, Abhishek Binani, Abhishek Kawatra
Department of medicine, Sardar Patel Medical College, Bikaner, Rajasthan, India
Correspondence:
Sanjay K. Kochar (drskkochar@gmail.com, drskkochar @rediffmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O1
Background:
Rapid diagnostic tests (RDTs) are widely used in malaria control programs for their ease of use and ability to provide quick results. However, their sensitivity may be limited, particularly in detecting low-level or asymptomatic infections. Examination of the peripheral smears for detection of malaria is gold standard but expertise of microscopist plays important role, and may be responsible for missing presence of malaria parasite in peripheral smear. Asymptomatic carriers, though not exhibiting symptoms may be acting as reservoirs within the community and can still contribute to malaria transmission.
Materials and methods:
In this study, we aimed to detect
Plasmodium species
in asymptomatic individuals who tested negative for malaria using a RDT and examination of peripheral smear. A total of 131 RDT-negative samples were collected from asymptomatic individuals from selected villages of Bikaner district. Each sample was carefully processed to ensure the quality of DNA for downstream analysis.
We employed real-time Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR) to enhance the detection sensitivity for low-level infections. DNA was extracted from each sample using a standardized extraction protocol. Following extraction, real-time PCR targeting
Plasmodium species
specific genes was performed to identify the presence of
Plasmodium
DNA in each sample. The PCR reactions were conducted in triplicate to ensure accuracy, and appropriate positive and negative controls were included in each run.
Results:
In this study, we analysed 131 asymptomatic, RDT and peripheral smear negative samples to investigate the presence of
Plasmodium species
. Our objective was to determine if
Plasmodium species
could be detected in asymptomatic individuals despite negative RDT and peripheral smear examination results. Out of the 131 samples, three were found to be positive for
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) and negative for
Plasmodium falciparum
(
P. falciparum
). This finding suggests that asymptomatic individuals may harbour low-level infections that are not detected by standard diagnostic test, highlighting a potential gap in malaria detection and the importance of further research in surveillance of asymptomatic malaria.
Conclusion:
Our study revealed that, among 131 asymptomatic and RDT/peripheral smear negative, three samples tested positive for
Plasmodium vivax
. This indicates that asymptomatic individuals can carry low density malaria infections undetected by routine testing, pausing potential challenges to malaria control and elimination efforts. These findings underscore the need for more sensitive diagnostic tools and reinforce the importance of monitoring asymptomatic carriers to prevent further transmission.
T1-O2 Hotspot districts with persistent
Plasmodium vivax
malaria pose a formidable challenge to malaria elimination in India
Amala R

International Centre for Genetic Engineering and Biotechnology, New Delhi, India

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O2
Background:
Malaria continues to pose a significant public health challenge in India, with an approximately 95% of its population residing in malaria-endemic regions.
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
or
Pv
) and
Plasmodium falciparum
(
P. falciparum
) have historically been the predominant species, with
P
.
vivax
contributing between 30 and 55% of malaria cases over the past two decades. This study aims to delineate districts with a high burden of
P. vivax
malaria, based on malariometric indices pertaining to 24 years (2000–2023). Furthermore, it evaluates the effectiveness of the 14-day primaquine (PQ-14) regimen and the distribution of long-lasting insecticide-impregnated nets (LLINs) in controlling
P
.
vivax
malaria.
Materials and methods:
We systematically ranked all districts based on malariometric indices (number of
P. vivax
& Slide Positivity Rate for
Pv
) using national malaria control programme data from 2000 to 2023. Districts that consistently ranked in the top 5 percentile for both indices for at least 12 years (≥ 50%) were classified as ‘Hotspot districts. The contribution of these identified hotspot districts to overall, state and national malaria burden was subsequently assessed. Also, the impacts of PQ-14 and LLIN interventions were analyzed using Interrupted Time Series (ITS) analysis.
Results:
The analysis identified 11 districts consistently ranked within the top 5 percentiles for over 50% of the years, which include Kolkata Municipal Corporation (West Bengal); Sonbhadra, Mirzapur, Allahabad (Uttar Pradesh); Latehar, Garhwa, Palamu, Chatra, Giridih, Hazaribagh (Jharkhand) and Nuapada (Odisha). The districts experienced a variable decline in
P. vivax
cases following implementation of the PQ—14 regimens started in 2007, with the country achieving an average annual reduction of 57 cases per 1000
Pv
cases. Additionally, LLINs resulted in an average reduction of 43 cases per 1000
Pv
cases annually, significantly higher than pre-LLINs average reduction of 14 cases per 1000
Pv
cases. Overall, these interventions established a substantial and sustained impact on reducing
P. vivax
burden, with the exception of Kolkata Municipal Corporation, which accounted for 91.5%, 76.8%, and 18.5% contribution to the district, state and nation
vivax
burden in 2023.
Conclusion:
Although
P. falciparum
has contributed largely to the overall burden,
P. vivax
malaria poses a significant challenge at district-level. This study identified 11 districts and municipal corporations across the states of West Bengal, Uttar Pradesh, Jharkhand and Odisha that require prioritization for
P. vivax
malaria control through intensified vector control, ensuring complete and directly observed treatments, routine hospital surveillance, etc. complementing the country’s broader elimination strategy.
T1-O3 Characterising the

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O3
Background:
Provision of radical cure in Southeast Asia for
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) malaria has decreased
P. vivax
caseloads dramatically by preventing relapses. As the hypnozoite reservoir is depleted transmission also has decreased. The effect of hypnozoite reduction on
P. vivax
relapse has not been quantified. In northwest Thailand where malaria control measures increased, we performed an observational rolling cohort study to describe the epidemiology and measure the incidence of
P. vivax
malaria while concomitant therapeutic studies assessed treatment efficacy.
Materials and methods:
Healthy non-pregnant G6PD normal volunteers with a history of symptomatic
P. vivax
infection, and without receiving radical cure, were recruited. Primaquine was given daily for 14-days (0.5 mg base/kg/day). Participants were followed at 1 and 2 months, then every 2 months until they developed symptomatic or asymptomatic
P. vivax
malaria. Visits for febrile illnesses were encouraged between follow up visits. Censored participants were replaced, matched by age group, sex, and recruitment site to maintain a cohort of 200 participants.
Results:
From March–April 2010 200 healthy participants were enrolled. Over the next 4 years, 92 individuals developed
P. vivax
malaria after primaquine. Assuming most relapses occur early, 28 of the 92 were within 6 months of enrollment. The overall annual incidence of
P. vivax
malaria infection decreased from 0.19 in 2010 to 0.09 infections per person per year in 2014. Post primaquine radical cure incidence of
P. vivax
cases were 25% of predicted by probabilistic modeling, which assumed a constant hypnozoite burden.
Conclusion:
Declining
P. vivax
transmission decreases hypnozoite burden and increases radical cure efficacy in previously vivax infected individuals suggesting higher doses of 8-aminoquinolines may not be needed as malaria caseloads approach elimination.
Acknowledgements:
The authors extend their appreciation to the Shoklo Malaria Research Unit (SMRU) teams for their commitment and diligence in running this study.
Trial registration-
NCT01076868
.
T1-O4 Microhaplotypes informing the origin of
Plasmodium vivax
recurrence from a therapeutic efficacy study in Papua, Indonesia
Edwin Sutanto

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O4
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) recurrent infection can occur due to recrudescence, reinfection, or relapse from reactivation of dormant hypnozoites. Polymerase chain reaction (PCR) and microsatellite genotyping have been used to classify relatedness of recurrent infections; however, the resolution of markers limits the confidence of results. Thus, we tested a newly developed microhaplotype assay that provides high-resolution data in a cost-effective manner [1]. We applied this assay to samples collected from a therapeutic efficacy study in Papua, an area of high malaria transmission in Indonesia, to inform the efficacy of antimalarial treatment in the study area.
Materials and methods:
The study was conducted in Timika, Papua, where participants were followed for 42 days from day of enrolment. Participants with uncomplicated malaria were treated with dihydroartemisinin-piperaquine (DHP). Venous blood was collected at day of enrolment (baseline) and at the time of recurrence. DNA was extracted from venous blood samples, then 93
P. vivax
microhaplotype markers were sequenced using Illumina MiSeq. The reads were processed and quality controlled using a malaria amplicon pipeline (
https://github.com/broadinstitute/malaria-amplicon-pipeline
). Markers with at least 10 × coverage were considered to be successfully genotyped. From the high-quality data, MOIRE was used to estimate the multiplicity of infection (MOI) in the population, then Dcifer was used to estimate between-infection relatedness by identity-by-descent (IBD). The IBD estimates were visualized using network plots at various IBD thresholds. Visual confirmation of allele proportions between baseline and recurrence cases was done by rainbow plots. All data analyses were done in R.
Results:
The study enrolled 78 participants, with 79 samples identified as
P. vivax
mono-infection. Two baseline-recurrence pairs were identified with
P. vivax
recurrences occurring on Day 21 and 28. Sequencing all of the samples generated almost 20 million reads, with a median of 227,300 reads per sample (range: 17,954–611,932 reads). After filtering and quality control, the median reads per sample was 212,682 (range: 16,834 reads to 564,052 reads) and the median number of markers that were successfully genotyped per sample was 90 markers (range: 86–92 markers). The median MOI in the population was 1 (range: 1–10). The median IBD between-infection in the population was 0%, with rare cases of relatedness between pairs. The IBD between baseline and recurrence pairs at Day 21 and 28 were 0.5% and 96.8%, respectively.
Conclusion:
We found a recurrent case with close to 100% IBD, indicating the possibility of a relapse from a homologous hypnozoite, and another recurrence case with almost 0% IBD, likely to be a reinfection or a relapse from a heterologous hypnozoite. This hypothesis is supported through a visual confirmation of the shared alleles on the suspected relapse cases sharing many common alleles (Fig. 1a), while the suspected reinfection only shared a few alleles (Fig. 1b). The presence of these cases prompted further investigation on possible hypnozoidal drug failure. Overall, our clinical trial and molecular analysis highlights the potential of microhaplotypes to resolve the possible cause of recurring
P. vivax
cases.
Fig. 1
Rainbow plots illustrate alleles shared between baseline and recurrence samples with a) close to 100% IBD and b) almost 0% IBD. The bars row-wise represent the 93 microhaplotype markers, colors in each bar represent proportion of the allele frequencies per marker
Acknowledgement:
The study was funded by the Medical Research Council UK, the Menzies Small Grant awarded to Steven Kho, the Australian NHMRC Ideas Grant (2019153), the Bill and Melinda Gates Foundation (INV-043618) to Sarah Auburn, and Exeins Health Initiative.
References:
Kleinecke M, Rumaseb A, Sutanto E, Trimarsanto H, Hoon KS, Osborne A, et al. Microhaplotype deep sequencing assays to capture
Plasmodium vivax
infection lineages. medRxiv; 2024.
T1-O5 The trend in
Plasmodium vivax
malaria in Boudh district, Odisha (India) and malaria elimination challenges
Pradhan Madan Mohan

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O5
Background:
Malaria poses worldwide public health problem, despite drastic reduction in morbidity and mortality over the decades. Of the five
Plasmodium spp
,
Plasmodium vivax
(
Pv
) accounts to 51% of reported malaria cases in South-East Asia Region (World Malaria Report 2023). As the life cycle of
Pv
is very complex, the epidemiological trend of
Pv
malaria must be kept under strict vigilance for malaria elimination by 2030. We attempt to describe the recent trend in
Pv
malaria and related challenges in
Plasmodium falciparum
(
Pf
) dominated Boudh district of Odisha state in India.
Materials and methods:
This study is based on the analysis of secondary malariometric data available with the routine malaria surveillance system of Boudh, a historically
Pf
malaria endemic district. The latest estimated population of the district is 0.53 million with population growth rate of 17.82%. We analysed year and month wise malaria data for last 10 years (2014 to 2024), sex wise malaria cases for last 3 years (2022 to 2024) and routine malaria data one DAMaN Sub-centre for last 2 year (2023 and 2024) of using software; Excel and GraphPad 5.01, 2007. We calculated the malaria epidemiological indicators:
Annual Blood Examination Rate (ABER),
Annual Parasite Incidence (API),
Pv
and
Pf
percentage. We analysed the malaria cases among male and female population using last 3 years surveillance data. We interpreted the analysis for
Pv
trend, sex wise malaria variations and Pv transmission seasonality of last 3 years.
Result:
Between 2014 and 2024, routine malaria surveillance indicator (ABER) ranged from 12 to 22. API declined from 15 in 2014 to 0.3 in 2019. After 2019 malaria cases increased with fluctuations (0.6 to 1.32) till 2022. API increased from 1.07 in 2023 to 6.2 in 2024. During this period,
Pv
percentage ranged between 19.85 in 2014 to 26.22 in 2023 which almost doubled to 53% in 2024 (P = 0.00022). Malaria among males is higher (59.23%) than females as per the last 3 years’ average data. Moreover, in a
Pf
dominated Sub-centre under DAMaN-Mass Screening and Treatment,
Pv
cases increased from zero in 2017 to 58% in 2024. The overall rise in malaria cases is 5.8 times higher in 2024 (2964 cases) than that of 2023 (511 cases). Further, in 2024, malaria transmission started peaking up in May instead of July coinciding with the increased number of
Pv
cases.
Conclusion:
Unprecedented rise in
Pv
cases in
Pf
endemic Boudh district with early
Pv
transmission is a matter of concern. As per the current guidelines,
Pv malaria
cases are treated with 3 days Chloroquine along with a low dose of Primaquine for 14 days. The poor compliance to Primaquine might be one reason for
Pv
case rise. Further, climate factors like temperature, rainfall patterns and humidity are known to have significant impact on malaria transmission. Studies on these aspects may help to explain the sudden rise in
Pv
infection in a
Pf
dominant area which would help in developing appropriate malaria elimination strategy.
Keyword:
DAMaN—Durgam Anchalare Malaria Nirakaran, meaning-malaria eradication in hard-to-reach areas.
T1-O6 Characteristics of imported
Plasmodium vivax
malaria in Sri Lanka during the first eleven years post-elimination
Pubudu U. Chulasiri

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O6
Background:
Sri Lanka has been a malaria-free country since the disease was eliminated in 2012 owing to a rigorous programme to prevent the re-establishment of malaria. Extensive travel between Sri Lanka and other malaria endemic countries in the southeast Asian region, particularly India, and Africa led to a high rate of importation of malaria, particularly of
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) infections from India. This study reveals particular characteristics of imported
P. vivax
malaria which relate to its biological features that have major implications on strategies to prevent the re-establishment of malaria in Sri Lanka.
Materials and methods:
A detailed history was obtained from all microscopically confirmed imported malaria cases registered in the National Malaria Database maintained at the Anti Malaria Campaign (AMC), Sri Lanka from 2013 to 2023. Data relating to travel and information on treatment seeking behaviour of patients which included the duration between return from travel and onset of clinical manifestations and between onset of illness to a malaria diagnosis were recorded. Blood parasites stages and densities were monitored. Patients diagnosed with
P. vivax
malaria were treated radically in accordance with the national malaria treatment guidelines with the recommended dose of chloroquine and primaquine. After cure patients were followed up on days 1, 2, 3, 7, 14, 28 and thereafter once a month for 12 months to monitor relapses. Data on imported malaria cases diagnosed with
P. vivax
and for comparison
Plasmodium falciparum
(
P. falciparum
) were extracted from this database for analysis.
Results:
A total of 532 imported malaria patients were reported from 2013 to 2023. 215 (40.5%) of these infections were
P. vivax,
and the rest were
P. falciparum
(48.1%),
P. ovale
(9.4%),
P. malariae
(1.9%) and
P. knowlesi
(0.19%). The proportion of imported
vivax
malaria infections declined steadily from 54.7% in 2013 to 6.45% in 2023. 140 (65%) of all
P. vivax
infections were acquired in India and 15% in Africa. Of infections originating in India 88 (63%) were in Sri Lankans and 34% in Indian nationals.
The period between patients’ arrival in Sri Lanka and onset of illness was 16 days, and between onset of illness and diagnosis 6 days in
P. vivax
malaria, contrasting with
P. falciparum
in which they were 3 and 4 days respectively. At diagnosis gametocytes were present in 89% of
P. vivax
infections and in only 19% of
P. falciparum
infections. All
vivax
patients recovered with treatment but three relapsed.
Conclusion:
India’s declining malaria burden likely contributed to the reduced proportion of imported
P. vivax
cases in Sri Lanka, possibly reflecting decreased travel between the two countries, though this is not evident. Some
P. vivax
cases may have been relapses, accounting for delayed illness onset after arrival compared to
P. falciparum
. The higher prevalence of infective gametocytes in
P. vivax
suggests a greater risk of re-establishment than
P. falciparum
.
T1-O7 Temporal dynamics of
P. vivax
symptomatic, asymptomatic and submicroscopic infections in Dhalai District of Tripura along with molecular surveillance for drug resistance

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O7
Background:
While there is improvement in the malaria situation in many parts of India, Tripura, a small State in Northeast India is facing specific challenges, with outbreaks and increasing trends despite intensive control measures. Interestingly, recently it experienced a shift in species composition with an increase in both absolute number and proportion of
P. vivax
, where
P. falciparum
has been reported as the dominant species (90–98%) for last 15 years. Our study reported a high hidden burden of submicroscopic and asymptomatic
P. vivax
and mixed cases in 2021, in
P. falciparum
predominant areas of Dhalai District, Tripura prompting this detailed study.
Materials and methods:
We conducted active surveillance in 4 high malaria-endemic villages of Dhalai, in addition to the data generated by active surveillance done by the Accredited Social Health Activists (ASHAs). Passive surveillance data at the Subcentre, Primary health centre (PHC) and the health camp were collected, cross-checked to remove duplication and subdivided into corresponding villages to calculate the village-wise data. The samples of 10 mass surveys conducted during 2019–22 in the area, were subjected to microscopy and Polymerase chain reaction (PCR) to asses asymptomatic and submicroscopic infections. Ensemble modelling is underway to determine the significant covariates influencing the species.
P. vivax
mdr-1 and crt-o genes, suspected drug resistance markers were studied too.
Results:
We witnessed an increasing trend of symptomatic
P. vivax
cases by using RDT in all these SCs in Dhalai, with a sharp increase in the number of cases in the other two malaria-endemic Districts of Tripura and for the entire State as well from a particular point of time. There was a huge asymptomatic, submicroscopic burden as well, where a distinct temporal variation pattern was observed. Our study showed a considerable number of cases which totally remains undetected by routine active and passive surveillance and even by mass surveillance if only Rapid diagnostic test (RDT) is used. Important and distinct mutation patterns have been found for Pvmdr-1 and PvCRT genes. A similar situation has been observed by analyzing Mizoram State-level data as well.
Conclusion:
This study from NE India displays the importance of conducting cross-sectional surveillance based on molecular methods. The increase in the clinical cases of
P. vivax
could be sustained by the asymptomatic
P. vivax
infections observed in the community or the autochthonous rise of drug-resistant parasites may be the cause of this. Despite India's low level of chloroquine resistance, reports of CQ resistance have been made in a number of regions, except for NE-India. However, alternative possibilities are pointed out by our molecular drug surveillance data. The increase in
P. vivax
cases is concerning for malaria elimination.
T1-O8 What drives
P. vivax
transmission: the role of parasite, human host, and antimalarial factors?
Fitsum G. Tadesse
1,2
, Wakweya Chali
1,3
, Legesse Alamerie Ejigu
1,2
, Teun Bousema
3
,
Chris Drakeley
2
1
Armauer Hansen Research Institute, Addis Ababa, Ethiopia;
2
London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine, London, United Kingdom;
3
Radboud University Medical Centre, Nijmegen, The Netherlands
Correspondence:
Fitsum G. Tadesse (fitsum.girma@ahri.gov.et)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O8
Background:
In
P. vivax
, gametocyte production directly follows the asexual parasite dynamics. The rapid generation of gametocytes contributes to the high infectivity of clinical
P. vivax
patients, even before symptoms arise. Despite gametocytemia being an important predictor of transmissibility to mosquitoes, other regulators such as human host, parasite, mosquito, drug and environmental factors may play important roles. The contribution of these factors is relatively well documented in
P. falciparum
, whilst evidence in
P. vivax
limited. In multisite studies in Ethiopia, we evaluated the transmission modulation role parasite (density and co-infection), sexual stage immunity, and schizonticides administered with or without primaquine play.
Materials and methods:
Using blood from 409 patients fed to 49,080
An. arabiensis
mosquitoes, we examined the role of parasite factors on the transmissibility of
P. vivax
to mosquitoes in direct membrane feeding assays (DMFAs). Examined in these feeds were also the relative transmissibility of
P. vivax
when it is a single infecting species vs mixed with
P. falciparum
. The transmission reducing role of sexual stage immunity was evaluated in separate paired 199 serum replacement feeds (SRMFA) using 31,840 mosquitoes. Antibody responses against a panel of
P. vivax
gametocyte antigens were quantified using ELISA. Additionally, the transmission blocking effect of chloroquine and an ACT (Pyramax) administered with or without primaquine were evaluated in 60 DMFAs, before and after treatment (days 1–3). The transmission blocking effect of chloroquine was also evaluated shortly after treatment (8 and 24 h).
Results:
Most patients were infectious (63.8%, 261/409) at presentation without screening for
P. vivax
gametocytemia. Parasitemia (ρ = 0.39, p < 0.001) and gametocytemia (ρ = 0.50, p < 0.001) were the most important factors that determined infectivity. More feeds were infectious when naïve sera were used (82.9%, 165/199; mean oocyst density 38, 1–321) than patient’s own sera (76.4%, 152/199; 19, 1–272; p < 0.001). Transmission reducing activity was observed in 20.1% (34/165) of feeds; the strain specificity is being evaluated in heterologous feeds at the moment with quantification of sexual stage immunity. Following treatment, transmission declined sharply on day 1 in both chloroquine (47%, 7/15) and Pyramax (13%, 2/15) arms with only 27% (4/15) patients in the CQ + PQ arm remaining infectious whilst no infection was observed in the PA + PQ arm on day 2. Signs of declining transmissibility was observed shortly (8 h) after chloroquine (40%, 206/522 infected mosquitoes; mean oocyst density 34) compared to before treatment (52%, 247/480; 57).
Conclusion:
Our observations support the notion that
P. vivax
sexual stages are more efficiently transmitted to mosquitoes before treatment is initiated. Considering the high infectivity of
P. vivax
to mosquitoes and the potential contribution of relapsing infections to the infectious reservoir, strategies that radically cure
P. vivax
might have an additional role of reducing transmission. Combined with enhanced case management to intercept early transmission and vector control to prevent new infections hypnozoitocides will play complementary role. ACTs have a relatively faster effect in reducing transmissibility which supports universal treatment for the two species in sympatric settings.
Acknowledgements:
The authors acknowledged that Fitsum G Tadesse will present this abstract on behalf of a study group.
Trial registration:
The Trial registration is Pan African Clinical Trials Registry (PACTR), PACTR202110520435408.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O9
Background:
With two neighboring countries recently certified malaria-free, Honduras is aiming for malaria elimination. The large decrease in local cases from over 30,000 in 2000 to under 400 in 2019, reversed in the last years to reach 3660 in 2022. To accelerate progress towards elimination, Honduras needs to strengthen its malaria elimination strategies, particularly against
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
). This analysis uses mathematical modeling to quantify the impact of intervention approaches and answer two questions: Is
P. vivax
elimination feasible within 5 years or a later target? What is the most appropriate combination of interventions to eliminate
P. vivax
in this timeframe?
Materials and methods:
The epidemiological impact on malaria transmission of health interventions targeting
P. vivax
malaria, namely routine case management, reactive drug administration (RDA), mass drug administration (MDA), tafenoquine (TQ) introduction and reduction in case importation from Nicaragua, is analyzed with a stochastic compartmental model for
P. vivax
transmission. Vector control interventions and ecological factors are assumed to be maintained at their 2022 level. Probabilities of interrupting
P. vivax
transmission is evaluated in the simulations for the ten most endemic foci of the Gracias a Dios department which make up more than 85% of the country’s
P. vivax
burden. Each focus is assumed free of transmission when local cases are not acquired during 1 year.
Results:
Increasing access to care (i.e. increasing seeking behaviour, timely diagnosis and quality treatment at points of care) has the greatest impact in case reduction, decreasing the number of reported cases by 83% on average in 5 years. In comparison, cases decrease by 40% when adherence to primaquine is increased and to 28% when tafenoquine is introduced. RDA and reducing delays to treatment have a smaller effect on case reduction than the other interventions (respectively 21% and 8% reduction on average in reported cases after 5 years).
Under the scenario of increased case management (i.e. increase in access to care, and increase in PQ adherence), transmission interruption is reachable only if importation from abroad is reduced by 90%. In this scenario, the probabilities of transmission interruption reach about 50% in most foci of the department of Gracias a Dios after 10 years.
Conclusion:
Interruption of malaria transmission in most foci is feasible in the next 10 years if efforts are concentrated on increasing all aspects of malaria case management and reducing malaria importation from abroad. In the most endemic focus of Kauquira, additional interventions are needed to raise the probability of elimination above 50%. This work highlights that health system strengthening is key to reach malaria elimination, although timelines should not be underestimated and imported infections play an important role in reseeding infections. These results can be combined with cost estimates to evaluate the cost-effectiveness of each strategy.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O10
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) is transmitted in tropical areas worldwide, and is the primary cause of malaria morbidity in South America. In addition,
P. vivax
is an emerging threat in areas where
Plasmodium falciparum
(
P. falciparum
) is in decline. It is more complicated to treat and control than
P. falciparum
because the hypnozoite stage in the liver causes relapse infections. High-throughput genotyping methods in
P. falciparum
that rely on targeted sequencing approaches have been widely deployed for genomic surveillance of malaria, which can detect population structure, as well as the geographic distribution of resistance to antimalarial agents and rapid diagnostic tests. Despite the proliferation of these tools for
P. falciparum
, fewer exist for
P. vivax
.
Materials and methods:
We leveraged global variation data from publicly available genome sequences to design four molecular inversion probe (MIP) genotyping panels that target: 1. geographically differentiating SNPs, 2. rare neutral SNPs, 3. common neutral SNPs, and 4. genes of known biological interest, i.e. erythrocyte invasion ligands that are potential blood-stage vaccine targets, and antimalarial resistance orthologs. We then deployed these MIP panels on 866
P. vivax
infections collected in the Peruvian Amazon.
Results:
Of these 866 samples, 685 were successfully genotyped and sequenced. Within these 685 samples, we identified clonal transmission networks (defined as having identity by descent [IBD] > 0.99) that are geographically and temporally connected. Most of these 685 samples were monoclonal. In addition, we detected copy number variation in Duffy binding protein (
Pvdbp
) and multiple reticulocyte binding proteins (
Pvrbps
). We also identified mutations in antimalarial resistance orthologs, as well as balancing selection in 13 of the potential blood-stage target genes, which include apical membrane antigen 1 (
Pvama1
),
Pvdbp
, erythrocyte binding protein (
Pvebp
), merozoite surface protein 1 (
Pvmsp1
), five
Pvrbps
, and three tryptophan-rich antigens (
Pvtrags
).
Conclusion:
Our study demonstrates that MIPs are capable of simultaneously characterizing multiple aspects of genomic epidemiology in
P. vivax
. These data are of interest to malaria control programs as well as researchers, and include complexity of infection estimates, transmission network inference, mutation frequencies in antimalarial resistance orthologs, and balancing selection in potential blood-stage vaccine targets that may hinder the development and deployment of such vaccines. This set of four MIP panels are the broadest genotyping tool available for
P. vivax
, and their success in a Peruvian context suggests that they are ready to be successfully deployed in other areas where
P. vivax
transmission occurs.
T1-O11 A novel isothermal-based recombinase polymerase amplification (RPA) assay for rapid detection of
Plasmodium vivax
Vachan M.M., Panchami Bhat, Anoopkrishna Rai, Shruthi P., Praveen Rai, Rama Adiga
Department of Molecular Genetics & Cancer, Nitte (Deemed to be University), Nitte University Centre for Science Education & Research (NUCSER), Mangalore, Karnataka, India

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-O11
Background:
Malaria, caused by
Plasmodium
parasites, presents a significant global health challenge, with
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) being a major contributor in endemic regions.
P. vivax
invades red blood cells via its merozoite form, which replicates within the host. Merozoite surface protein-1 (MSP1) is the most abundant GPI-anchored surface protein and is conserved across malaria species, highlighting its potential as a diagnostic target. Current diagnostic methods, including time-consuming microscopy and Polymerase chain reaction (PCR) assays with high equipment demands, are limited in resource-constrained settings. Although rapid diagnostic tests are affordable, they lack the sensitivity to detect low parasite densities. Therefore, there is an urgent need for a novel diagnostic technique that enhances specificity, sensitivity, while being simple and affordable and ideal in a resource limited setting. The study aims to develop a Recombinase polymerase amplification assay for specific diagnosis of
P. vivax
.
Materials and methods:
Sample collection
and
ethical clearance
. After the initial detection using Giemsa stained microscopic and nested PCR (Snounou PCR) detection of the
Plasmodium
species.
Assay development
; MSP1 region amplification primers were designed using sequences of MSP1 from various
P. vivax
strains. These nest primers targeted all strains/haplotypes of
P. vivax
. The 5' region of msp1 was amplified using a degenerative forward primer, nest forward primer and a reverse primer to generate 882 bp product and a 368 bp product using the TwistAmp® Basic kit manufactured by TwistDx, UK.
Assay optimization;
The primers were optimized for suitable annealing temperature, amplification conditions, incubation time (for RPA), the specificity and the sensitivity were calculated.
Assay validation;
RPA Assay test was conducted on 34 clinical samples collected in 2017–19 for the clinical validation.
Results:
The genomic DNA samples of malaria were used for amplification after the analysis using microscopy and conventional diagnostic PCR [1]. The MSP1 gene was successfully amplified by PCR for having a band size of 882 bp and 616 bp indicating that the specified Nest 1 and Nest2 primers were effective in amplifying the target gene. Later the designed primer for RPA successfully amplified the target gene with size of 368 bp. Among the different temperatures 40 °C and incubation times of 30 min showed bright band, which indicates this condition is ideal for conducting an RPA assay for Msp1 gene of
P. vivax
., Clinical validation revealed the sensitivity 82.61% and specificity of 27.27% with 95% CI.
Conclusion:
In this study, RPA assay was developed to target the merozoite surface protein Msp1 of
P. vivax
. RPA, first described in 2006, allows rapid amplification at 37–42 °C and is particularly suited for field and point-of-care diagnostics due to its high specificity and sensitivity. The assay achieved optimal performance at 37 °C for 20 min using the TwistAmp® Basic kit from TwistDx in the UK. The isothermal amplification technology can overcome limitations for nucleic acid testing since it is easier to use in resource constrained environments. Cost analysis has not been done since the method is in the research and development stage.
References:
Rai A, Rai P, Teerthanath S, Kotian A, Karunasagar I, Adiga R. Diagnosis, clinical and molecular delineation of human
Plasmodium
Species from Mangaluru, Southwest India. Journal of Pure & Applied Microbiology. 2023; 17(1): 532

Background:
The 263 million cases of malaria reported in the WHO World Malaria Report 2024 include
Plasmodium vivax
(
Pv
) cases, which are widely distributed in the Americas (72.1%), South-East Asia (48.2%), Western Pacific (28.9%), and Eastern Mediterranean (35.2%) areas in 2023. Due to its less severity than falciparum malaria and inability to maintain in-vitro culture, it is challenging to identify new drug targets. Nevertheless, its invasion mechanism
to human reticulocytes through PvRBP2
b, a protein and a potential vaccine candidate is known that interacts with reticulocyte transferrin Receptor-1 (CD71/TfR1) [1]. There is limited evidence on antigenic variations of PvRBP2b in African isolates, but apparently no data on Indian field isolates. This study aimed to map the precise pattern of genetic polymorphism of
PvRBP2b
in field isolates from different geographical regions of India.
Materials and methods:
A total of 22 microscopy-confirmed
Pv
mono-infection samples were collected on Whatman filter paper from different states of India. Genomic DNA was isolated and PvRBP2b-TfR1 interacting region (1436 bp) was amplified by designing the specific primers followed by sequencing to identify the polymorphism [2]. The patient’s sequencing data was aligned against reference sequence to check polymorphisms using MAFFT program.
Results:
Non-synonymous polymorphisms were observed in 91% (20/22) of the samples with one or more mutations in the PvRBP2b-TfR1 interacting region. This is the first report to identify four novel mutations N349K (45%; 10/22), E389K (4.5%; 1/22), K412N (82%; 18/22) and Q413K (4.5%; 1/22) in Indian clinical isolates. We also found mutations K288T, K363E, G382R and D366L/V/D, which have previously been reported in African isolates.
Conclusion:
High frequency of polymorphism in the PvRBP2b-TfR1 interacting region would challenge the efforts in developing PvRBP2b-based vaccine.
Trial registration:
Applied, under review.
References:
Gruszczyk J, Huang RK, Chan LJ, Menant S, Hong C, Murphy JM, et al. Cryo-EM structure of an essential
Plasmodium
vivax invasion complex. Nature. 2018;559(7712):135–9.

Plasmodium

Malaria Journal

T1-P2
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) is the most geographically widespread human malarial parasite, as over one-third of the world’s population is currently at risk of its infection. The state of Punjab in India which has qualified for malaria elimination under National Framework for Malaria Elimination in India (2016–30), is a
P. vivax
dominant state, and the high mobility of labour migration in the transmission season of malaria from the adjoining states is a major challenge in the task of achieving malaria elimination. With the launch of this programme, the state has accelerated malaria control efforts and intensified surveillance to keep track of the shifts in epidemiological trends in the state. In this study, the trends in proportion of imported vs. indigenous malaria cases in Punjab were analysed.
Materials and methods:
The occurrence of indigenous and mobile and migratory population (MMP) malaria in the state were recorded from 2017–2024. MMP cases were defined as those people who move frequently from their permanent residence state, either seasonally, periodically or continuously. The
Plasmodium
species, year- and district-wise distribution and trends, travel history and home state of the patients were descriptively analysed.
Results:
From 2017–2024, a total of 3290 malaria cases were recorded.
P. vivax
was observed to be the predominant species. Overall, the number of indigenous cases were observed to decrease gradually since 2017, while the MMP malaria ranged from 31–89% of the total malaria cases of the state. Year wise, the share of MMP malaria was 33.04% (263/796) in 2017, 39.9% (249/624) in 2018, 31.1% (355/1140) in 2019, 38.5% (42/109) in 2020, 39.4% (28/71) in 2021, 35.3% (42/119) in 2022, 41.3% (31/75) in 2023, and 89.33% (318/356) in 2024. The majority of the MMP cases were reported from rural as compared to the urban areas, while most were from the state of Uttar Pradesh.
Conclusion:
In last few years, a substantial proportion of malaria recorded in Punjab is due to MMP population. Out of 23 districts, the indigenous cases were majorly reported from the districts of Ludhiana, Mansa, Amritsar, Hoshiarpur and Sri Muktsar Sahib, while the imported cases were mainly reported from the districts of Hoshiarpur, SAS Nagar, Patiala, and Ludhiana. Although the incidence of indigenous malaria in Punjab has drastically decreased with intensive malaria control efforts, tackling MMP malaria is a major challenge in the state to achieve the targets of malaria elimination.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P3
Background:
Low density
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) infections are often undetectable by currently available diagnostic tools and sustain transmission, threatening elimination goals. Here we present a systematic review and meta-analysis quantifying the prevalence and densities of submicroscopic infections across different transmission settings to inform the integration of improved vivax diagnostics in malaria elimination programs.
Materials and methods:
A systematic review and meta-analysis of population-based cross-sectional studies measuring the prevalence of
P. vivax
infections by rapid diagnostic test (RDT) and/or light microscopy (LM) and polymerase chain reaction (PCR) was conducted. A search of PubMed, Web of Science and EMBASE was conducted for studies reporting the prevalence of
P. vivax
infections by RDT/LM and PCR. Data on parasite densities measured by PCR in light microscopy detectable and submicroscopic
P. vivax
infections were also collected. Pooled estimates of submicroscopic infections and parasite densities were calculated with a random-effects model. Heterogeneity was assessed using the Cochrane Q test and inconsistency index with stratification by transmission setting, defined as low (< 10%), medium (10–20%) and high
P. vivax
prevalence (> 20%).
Results:
In the review, 7439 studies assessed, 118 were included with 170,692 participants in total. Of the included studies, 27 also presented quantitative PCR (qPCR) density data from 90,328 participants. The proportion of submicroscopic infections varied significantly depending on endemic setting, 78.0%, 69.2% and 61.8% in low, medium and high transmission settings, respectively. Linear regression (coefficient − 0.75 [95% CI − 1.23 to − 0.28], p = 0.02), suggested a 0.75 percentage point decrease of submicroscopic prevalence for each percentage point increase in PCR prevalence.
Due to differences in method and reporting units, pooled analyses of parasite densities were performed in subsets, with an overall parasite density of 20.1 parasites/µL (range 0.6–213.3) across 19 studies, and 3.5 gene copies/µL (range 0.04–6.1) across 8 studies. There was a significant association between mean parasite density and malaria endemicity; 18.2, 46.0 and 192.2 parasites/µL in low, medium and high transmission settings, respectively.
Conclusion:
The proportion of submicroscopic
P. vivax
infections was found to be higher in low transmission settings. Detection and treatment of submicroscopic
P. vivax
infections is therefore necessary to accelerate towards elimination. The lack of reporting standards for PCR-derived parasite density results restricts the comparison between studies.

Malaria Journal
2025,

T1-P4
Background:
Imported malaria is a major burden in low-endemic settings. Rapid and accurate identification of the country of infection is crucial for appropriate treatment strategies and outbreak monitoring. Distinguishing autochthonous from imported cases is highly challenging for
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) as this species can form dormant liver stages that reactivate weeks to months after the initial infection. Molecular assays for a 33-SNP geographic barcode have been established to classify
P. vivax
geographic origin, but these utilise the Illumina platform, which may be constrained in turnaround time [1,2]. This study aimed to develop MinION assays for the 33-SNP barcode, for more rapid determination of the country of infection.
Materials and methods:
Building upon the previously established Illumina-based assays, we developed a single-plex assay for the MinION sequencer. We evaluated the limit-of-detection and specificity of the MinION assays using a diverse panel of 192 P. vivax patient samples from 9 countries. ​Allele calls​​ derived from the MinION assay were compared against the genomic data derived from the ​Malaria Genomic Epidemiology Network (MalariaGEN​)​
P. vivax
community project Pv4. The vivaxGEN-GEO software was used for country prediction.
Results:
The MinION assay demonstrated a high sensitivity and specificity in all countries assessed. ​​​Ten-fold ​​​​​​s​​erial dilution with parasitemia ranging from ​177,777 parasites/μl down to less than 1 parasite/μl were assayed to assess the assay’s limit of detection (LoD). The assay is able to ​​consistently genotype all 33 SNPs​ at ​70.4 p/µl, parasites/ul. ​Under 70.4 p/µl up to 15 SNPs (45%) had missing data, reducing the confidence in predicted geographic origin. Comparison between MinION amplicon sequencing, Illumina amplicon sequencing, and genomic data revealed a high concordance. Starting with a patient blood sample, we were able to conduct the laboratory assays and data analysis within 10 h in ideal conditions.
Conclusion:
The simplicity, portability, and cost-effectiveness of our MinION-based geographic barcode make it well-suited for resource-limited settings. Its potential impact lies in facilitating targeted interventions for imported
P. vivax
cases. The short turnaround aligns with strategies such as the “1–3-7” approach used in China for case reporting, investigation, and response. Further optimization and validation on larger patient cohorts are necessary to establish its practical utility in the field.
References:
Trimarsanto H, Amato R, Pearson RD, Sutanto E, Noviyanti R, Trianty L, et al. A molecular barcode and web-based data analysis tool to identify imported
Plasmodium vivax
malaria. Commun Biol. 2022;5(1):1411.
Kattenberg JH, Nguyen HV, Nguyen HL, Sauve E, Nguyen NTH, Chopo-Pizarro A, et al. Novel highly-multiplexed AmpliSeq targeted assay for
Plasmodium vivax
genetic surveillance use cases at multiple geographical scales. Front Cell Infect Microbiol. 2022; 12:953187.

Malaria Journal

T1-P5
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
is the predominant species causing malaria in South Asia. South Asian countries like Nepal, Bangladesh, and Bhutan have significantly reduced
P. vivax
malaria cases in the past decade. However, cross-border spread remains a challenge in some areas. Parasite genetics has the potential to inform on these dynamics. Previously, there was no genetic data on
P. vivax
in Nepal and only low-resolution data from a few markers in Bhutan and Bangladesh, leaving critical knowledge gaps on transmission in these regions. We used a new 98-marker
P. vivax
microhaplotype genotyping assay [1] to generate high-resolution data on the relatedness between infections in Bhutan, Bangladesh and Nepal, with comparison to high endemicity countries in the region like Afghanistan, India and Pakistan.
Materials and methods:
Patient samples were collected from a clinical trial in Sudurpashchim Province, Nepal, in 2021–2023 (ClinicalTrials.gov ID
NCT04079621
), a therapeutic efficacy survey in Bhutan in 2013–2015 [2], and a cross-sectional survey in the Chittagong Hill Tracts, Bangladesh, in 2014–2015 [3]. For comparative analyses, amplicon sequencing data from a previous study [1] were derived from a clinical trial in Afghanistan [4]. Genetic data from India and Pakistan were obtained from open-access whole-genome sequencing data [5].
Amplicon sequencing was performed at the vivaxGEN microhaplotype panel [1]. Variant calling on amplicon sequencing data was performed using the vivaxGEN-MHAPS GitHub pipeline [6]. Population genetic analyses included determining the effective multiplicity of infection (eMOI) using
MOIRE
[7] and measuring relatedness as identity by descent (IBD) using
Dcifer
[8].
Results:
Genotyping data were successfully generated for Bhutan (n = 30) and Bangladesh (n = 41) samples. Only 3 cases were successfully amplified from Nepal. Sufficient data were available from Afghanistan (n = 158), India (n = 30), and Pakistan (n = 30) for comparative analyses. High marker diversity was observed in all populations (excluding Nepal).
Within-host diversity analysis revealed that the lowest eMOI was in Bhutan and Bangladesh, suggesting low superinfection relative to Afghanistan. Relatedness (IBD) analyses revealed three infection clusters in the dataset: Bangladesh, Bhutan (partial), and high-endemic countries. Bhutan had two sub-populations: one aligned with the high-endemicity cluster comprising cases from Indian nationals, reflecting importations, and one mainly comprising cases from Bhutanese nationals.
The three Nepal cases split into two sub-populations comparable to Bhutan, potentially reflecting local and imported groups. Bangladesh was less connected to the large reservoir, but the primary endemic zones were geographically distant. Although not reaching significance (p > 0.05), the Bhutanese local and Bangladesh populations had high IBD compared to the high-endemic populations. This trend may indicate increased inbreeding in the low-endemic settings, but larger sample sizes are needed to confirm this.
Conclusion:
Our study provides the first report on the genetic diversity and transmission dynamics of
P. vivax
in South Asian countries. We highlight the risks of cross-border infection spread through comparative analyses with neighbouring countries. Our findings show that microhaplotype genotyping is more effective than previous microsatellite genotyping, offering valuable genetic insights into the differentiation between local and imported cases, and emphasizing its importance in detecting cross-border importations.

T1-P6 Assessment of primaquine compliance in
Plasmodium vivax
malaria patients in two districts of Odisha State, India
Anju Viswan K
1
, Dolly Choudhury
1
, Srikanth S
2
, Shriram A N
2
, Kannan T
2
, Rohit Sharma
2
, Manju Rahi
2
1
ICMR-Vector Control Research Centre Koraput Field Station, Odisha, India;
2
ICMR-Vector Control Research Centre, Puducherry, India
Correspondence:
Manju Rahi (drmanjurahi@gmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P6
Background:
Malaria remains a significant public health challenge in India, especially in rural and tribal regions, with prevalent infections from
Plasmodium falciparum
(
P. falciparum
) and
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
). In 2023,
P. vivax
accounted for 20% of the 41,971 malaria cases in Odisha. Full adherence to the 14-day primaquine regimen is essential for effectively controlling
vivax
malaria. However, socio-cultural factors, accessibility, and other issues often lead to poor treatment compliance, with no scientific data currently available on this topic. This study is the first in India to evaluate patient adherence to the primaquine regimen, identify barriers, and monitor malaria recurrence over 6 months.
Materials and methods:
A cross-sectional study utilized a structured questionnaire to evaluate adherence to the chloroquine and primaquine regimen among patients with
P. vivax
and mixed malaria infections (
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
) in the Koraput and Malkangiri districts of Odisha. Pregnant women and individuals who did not complete the full 14-day primaquine course due to reasons like non-availability or mixed infection (treated with ACT) were excluded. A sample size of 100
P. vivax
cases per site was calculated, assuming 50% non-compliance with primaquine among those confirmed with
P. vivax
malaria via RDT or microscopy. Participants were enrolled after providing consent or assent, and socio-demographic factors, clinical features, and malaria history at individual and family levels were recorded at enrolment. Follow-up assessments occurred on day 7 (through personal visit or phone) and day 14 (via direct interview) to evaluate medication compliance, confirmed by pill counts or blister pack checks.
Results:
Out of 206
P. vivax
malaria patients, 197 were confirmed as eligible, with both districts reporting similar case numbers. Overall adherence to the 14-day primaquine regimen was 56.9%, higher in Malkangiri (64.3%) than in Koraput (49.5%) (p = 0.0361). Non-compliance largely stemmed from symptom relief leading to medication discontinuation, particularly in Koraput (76.0%) compared to Malkangiri (37.1%) (p < 0.001). Other challenges included forgetfulness and avoidance, especially in Malkangiri (45.7%). Lower compliance was linked to being under 13 years, inconsistent use of long-lasting insecticidal nets (LLINs), and out-of-pocket treatment costs. Conversely, adherence to the 3-day chloroquine regimen was notably higher at 97.3%, likely due to its shorter duration. Patients completed the 14-day primaquine treatment as diagnosed at health facilities, despite irregular drug availability. ASHA workers provided monitoring and support in Lamtaput and Pottangi blocks to ensure compliance, while patients in other areas often turned to private clinics after 2 days of persistent fever.
Conclusion:
Among subjects who received a 14-day primaquine course, only 56.9% completed it, revealing significant treatment adherence gaps. Key barriers included symptom relief, forgetfulness, and financial issues. The study highlights the need for targeted interventions like real-time monitoring, follow-up systems, and IEC and SBCC campaigns to improve adherence in malaria-endemic areas. Incomplete treatment for
P. vivax
can lead to relapses, prolonged parasitemia, higher healthcare costs, and potential drug resistance, complicating malaria elimination efforts. Research from Indonesia, Thailand, Myanmar, Vietnam, and Cambodia shows that socio-cultural factors, healthcare infrastructure, and community-based interventions can enhance adherence, especially in rural regions. India's National Strategic Plan for Malaria Elimination recommends follow-ups on days 7 to 14 for Pv cases in low-risk areas to evaluate parasitemia clearance and compliance with radical cure. Although supervised primaquine administration may increase costs, it ensures complete treatment, reduces relapse rates, and supports the country's malaria elimination goals. Improving primaquine adherence is essential to advancing malaria elimination efforts in Koraput and Malkangiri where more than 40% of
vivax
malaria patients did not complete 14-day course of primaquine. Focused interventions addressing the identified barriers can enhance treatment compliance and accelerate progress toward malaria control and elimination in these high-risk areas.
T1-P7 Molecular analysis on drug-resistant genes of malaria caused by

, Rai P

2

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P7
Background:
The emergence of antimalarial drug-resistance in
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) is a global concern for malaria eradication. Key single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) associated with resistance include
pvmdr1
variations M908L, T958M, Y976F, F979S, M980V, F1076L,
pvdhfr
variations N50I, S58R, S117N, I173L, and
pvdhps
variations A383G and A553G. These SNPs effect drug efficacy, particularly first-line drugs like chloroquine, primaquine, and sulfadoxine-pyrimethamine (SP). Identifying and characterizing these molecular markers is essential for understanding resistance mechanisms and guiding treatment strategies. This study aims to elucidate the prevalence of these SNPs in clinical isolates from Mangaluru, providing insights into the drug-resistant polymorphisms in
P. vivax
.
Materials and methods:
This study collected blood samples from individuals with uncomplicated
P. vivax
malaria with ethical clearance. Samples were screened with QBC initially and confirmed using microscopy within 2 h, meanwhile, total genomic DNA was extracted employing the QIAamp DNA blood micro kit, followed by nPCR targeting gene 18S rDNA using published primers [1]. For drug-resistance determination of
P. vivax
, partial sequences of
mdr
, and
dhfr
were targeted using existing primers, and for
dhps
common primers were designed in the study to cover sets of primers of
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
that exist [2,3]. Amplified regions were sent for Sequencing (Eurofins Genomics India Pvt Ltd) ensuring all purity measures. Obtained sequences were processed using bioinformatics tools such as PolyPeakParser, BLAST (NCBI), and Multiple Sequence Alignment (MEGA-X). Sequence harboring SNPs were compared with reference sequences, existing variation reports, and employed statistics before depositing onto the GeneBank.
Results:
A total of 100 uncomplicated
P. vivax
samples confirmed by microscopy and PCR were screened for drug resistance genes. Sequencing revealed an equal prevalence of wild-type and the N273K mutation in
pvdhfr
. Analysis of 16 isolates identified mutations in
pvmdr1
: T898A, F979V, and M980V in two isolates each, F1076L in 15 isolates, and M908L and T958M in all 16. Additionally, sequencing of
pvdhps
in 26 samples showed nearly equal proportions of mutant and wild-type alleles, with 42.3% exhibiting the A553G mutation. In 25 isolates,
pvdhps
sequencing revealed tandem repeat variations, classifying them into eight haplotypes. The repeat pattern E/-|G/D|E/D|A/G/S followed by KLTN resulted in haplotypes A to H. Haplotype A is similar to the Sal 1 strain but contains a #GEA deletion, while types B, D, E, and F display a distinct EDDA# unit followed by variable GDA# or DDA# repeats.
Conclusion:
This study highlights the key mutations and unique tandem repeats in
P. vivax
genes, such as
pvmdr1
,
dhfr
, and
dhps
, are associated with resistance to common treatments like chloroquine and sulfadoxine-pyrimethamine (SP). The prevalence of mutations, particularly in
pvmdr1
(M908L, M980V, F1076L), suggests rapid genetic evolution and chloroquine resistance. Notably, mutations linked to SP resistance like A553G were found in
P. vivax
, despite SP being discontinued in the region, raising concerns about indirect exposure. Continuous surveillance and research are essential to understand these resistance dynamics and formulate antimalarial strategies and policies.

Authors grateful to Nitte University Centre for Science Education & Research and Nitte (Deemed to be University) for providing a Research environment and lab infrastructure for carrying out the study and for K.S. Hegde Charitable Hospital for providing blood samples.
This study was approved by the ethical committee of Nitte University,
vide
NU/CEC/ICMR-05/2015, NU/CEC/2018/0183. Consent from these patients who had attended Justice K.S Hegde Charitable Hospital, Mangalore was obtained per the ethical guidelines.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P8
Background:
Globally, efforts to control and eliminate
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) have been hampered by our limited understanding of the unique reservoirs these parasites can survive in and spread from, namely their ability to stay dormant and undetected in the liver. Dormant liver stage parasites can reactive anywhere from weeks to months after the initial infection and can cause recurrent episodes of malaria, referred to as relapses [1]. These hidden relapse infections can maintain transmission in low-endemic regions and diminish elimination efforts. Ethiopia’s transmission profile also presents a unique set of challenges due to the sympatric transmission of
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
.
Materials and methods:
Most genetic surveillance tools are unable to distinguish between
P. vivax
infection relapses, reinfections with a new parasite, or recrudescence due to treatment failure. Recent evidence suggests that combining tools aimed at characterising genetic relatedness through identity-by-descent (IBD) metrics, alongside time-to-event data, could be used to distinguish homologous relapses from recrudescence events on an individual level. Microhaplotypes comprise between 3 and 10 high diversity single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) and offer a cost-effective and high throughput alternative to performing WGS given their ability to capture IBD between infection pairs [2]. To assess the versatility of this concept, we analysed
P. vivax
infections from 142 independent individuals with 2 or more recurrent infections, with some individuals having up to 6 recurrences. These samples were collected within the framework of previously described randomised clinical trials with 4 treatment arms in Ethiopia.
Results:
Here we present a use case for microhaplotype-based IBD (93 marker panel) and time-to-event data to assess parasite persistence over multiple time points in Ethiopia. Within these clinical trials, two arms contained primaquine (PQ) as a radical cure treatment for dormant liver stage parasites. As anticipated, preliminary analysis revealed that the two non-PQ arms of the clinical trials exhibited a higher frequency of highly related pairs (IBD >  = 0.2375) than the two PQ arms, with a greater proportion of pairs with low IBD (IBD < 0.2375) observed in recurrence events after day 120. Within independent individuals, we identified highly related parasites (IBD > 0.475) persisting in individuals up to 242 days after their initially detected infection and clonal parasites (IBD > 0.95) up to 295 days (R-software
hmmIBD
) [3]. We also identified unrelated parasites across multiple time points within these same individuals, indicating concurrent recurrence and relapse events (N = 58 individuals).
Conclusion:
Using microhaplotype sequencing and time-to-event data, we were able to identify and observe the ability of highly related parasites to persist over time within individuals, despite repeated treatment for current infections, and probabilistically classify these infection types. We did not identify infections that appeared to indicate the failure of high-dose PQ, where failure is defined as either relapse or recurrence. These results highlight the versatility of this methodology to not only distinguish between infections at the individual level, furthering our understanding of
P. vivax
infection biology, but also monitor treatment efficacy and rate of relapse at the population level. This assay also offers a cost-effective way to implement genetic surveillance into national malaria control programmes.
References:
Popovici J, Pierce-Friedrich L, Kim S, Bin S, Run V, Lek D, Hee KHD, Lee Soon-UL, Cannon MV, Serre D, Menard D. Recrudescence, reinfection, or relapse? A more rigorous framework to assess chloroquine efficacy for
Plasmodium vivax
malaria. J Infect Dis. 2019; 219: 315–322.

Plasmodium vivax

Background:
Anopheles baimaii
and
An. minimus
have been traditionally identified as the principal vectors responsible for the transmission of human malaria especially
Plasmodium falciparum,
which used to account for > 90% of infections in the northeastern region of India, particularly in the state of Tripura but recent studies reported increasing transmission by
P. vivax
as well
.
Except primary vectors, secondary vectors are crucial for maintaining the low transmission levels needed to sustain malaria prevalence in humans. Long back,
An. maculatus
was found as a vector in some Southeast Asian countries including India and was also incriminated as a vector in Assam and Meghalaya. Still, cross-sectional studies in the past few decades failed to establish it as a vector [1]. Therefore, a detailed longitudinal study was undertaken to determine the vector status of
An. maculatus
group in Tripura.
Materials and methods:
The study was conducted in 9 villages within the Ambassa and Ganganagar blocks of Dhalai district, Tripura during 2019–2022.
Anopheles
collected between dusk to dawn using CDC light traps and hand collection were morphologically examined for Maculatus group members followed by molecular identification by Allele-specific PCR for species identification. Mitochondrial Cytochrome Oxidase 1 (CO1) gene was enhanced and profiled for molecular analysis. CO1-based multiplex PCR was done for blood meal assay. Nested quantitative PCR for cytochrome b was employed for parasite detection.
Results:
Although the density of
An. maculatus
was initially lower than the known primary vectors
An. baimaii
and
An. minimus
, after 2019
An. maculatus
maintained its density and expanded in other villages when the primary vector densities had dropped. It was also present in the months when the primary vector densities were either absent or very low, thereby pointing out its possible role in maintaining transmission in the area. Molecular identification revealed that out of 229 samples, the majority were
An. maculatus
s.s., with very few
An. dravidicus
. 3 new haplotypes of
An. maculatus
and 1 new haplotype of
An. dravidicus
were detected from our collection.
Pf
,
Pv
and mixed infections (
Pf
+
Pv
), were found with high positivity levels. In blood meal assay, while there were more cattle blood-fed than human ones, several were found with both human and cattle blood, indicative of opportunistic and nervous blood feeding.
Conclusion:
In our knowledge, this is the first report of
An. maculatus s.s.
as a vector in Tripura and
An. dravidicus
as malaria vector. With low density, and low to moderate anthropophilicity but high
Plasmodium
positivity rate,
An. maculatus
s.s. can be deemed to have a supplementary vectorial role for both
P. falciparum
and
P. vivax
in Tripura. Considering the same, there is an urgent need to study the detailed bionomics of this species to plan appropriate vector control strategies and an effective approach to eradicate malaria in the state.

T1-P10 Characterizing the CDGSH type NEET proteins as Fe-S binding proteins in
Plasmodium
Divya Malik
1
, B. Vani, Sanjay K. Kochar
2
, Shilpi Garg
1
1
Department of Biological Sciences, Molecular Parasitology and Vector Biology laboratory, Birla Institute of Technology and Sciences, Pilani, Rajasthan, India;
2
Department of Medicine, Sardar Patel Medical College, Bikaner, Rajasthan, India
Correspondence:
Shilpi Garg- shilpi@pilani.bits-pilani.ac.in
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P10
Background:
Malaria, caused by
Plasmodium
, remains a severe global health threat. Iron plays a crucial role in the parasite's life cycle, particularly within mitochondria, where it is used in free form or as [Fe-S] clusters for various metabolic processes. Regulating mitochondrial iron homeostasis is essential as its disruption can lead to iron and ROS accumulation, resulting in cell death. However, the molecular players regulating this balance remain unknown. Recent research highlights a novel group of Fe-S proteins, NEET proteins encoded by CISD genes, implicated in iron or Fe/S transfer and Fe distribution in plant cells.
Materials and methods:
In this study, we have identified three putative CISD gene orthologs in
Plasmodium
species and characterized their molecular structures. NEET proteins were amplified from patient's blood samples (clinical isolates), cloned into the pRSETA expression vector, and expressed in
E. coli
as an expression system. The recombinant proteins were purified using Ni–NTA columns and utilized to raise antibodies in mice for immune-localization studies. In vitro chemical reconstitution of [Fe-S] clusters under anaerobic conditions and UV–Vis spectroscopy was used to study the [Fe-S] clusters on NEET proteins.
Results:
Malaria parasites encode NEET proteins with conserved CDGSH domain across species.
Plasmodium vivax
was found to encode all three members of NEET proteins. This family contains a conserved CDGSH type domain with unique 3Cys-1His coordinated clusters with MiNT protein as an exception containing two CDGSH domains. The biochemical in vitro reconstitution revealed the presence of characteristic [2Fe-2S] clusters on NEET proteins under anaerobic conditions, and these clusters were found to be redox active. The clusters were highly unstable, and the stability of these clusters was dependent on temperature and pH. The MD simulation study confirmed that the change in the temperature and pH affects the folding state of the protein.
Conclusion:
The novel group of Fe-S binding proteins in
Plasmodium
are characterized by their unique 3Cys-1His coordination, a property different from other 2Fe-2S cluster binding proteins such as the Rieske protein group. The presence of two CDGSH Fe-S binding domains in one of the NEET proteins is a striking feature that can be further explored. The domain is highly conserved, providing this protein with a conserved iron-related function in mitochondria, an area largely unexplored in
Plasmodium
. The NEET functions are dependent on the Fe-S cluster's lability, so the drugs that target the 2Fe2S clusters of NEET proteins could be used as promising antimalarial drugs and can be exploited for antimalarial development.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P11
Background:
Peru, Colombia, and Honduras have set a goal to eliminate malaria by 2030. However, geographic connectivity and drug resistance pose significant challenges to eradication efforts.
Materials and methods:
In this study, we performed high-throughput sequencing on
Plasmodium vivax
samples collected between 2010 and 2019 from the Peruvian Amazon Basin (n = 147), the Colombian Andes (n = 47) and across Honduras (n = 23). Sequencing data was analyzed to assess the multiplicity of infections, population genetics, transmission dynamics, and putative drug resistance markers associated with sulfadoxine-pyrimethamine (SP) resistance (DHFR 57, 58, 61, and 117, and DHPS 380, 382, 383, 385, and 553).
Results:
Our results showed that Colombia samples presented a higher rate of multiplicity of infection (14.5%) than Peru (8.2%) and Honduras (0%). Also, population structure analysis revealed seven distinct clusters, with 23.5% of samples exhibiting evidence of admixture (threshold probability < 0.9). Peru presented two exclusive clusters, whereas Colombia and Honduras presented one each. The remaining three clusters showed recombination of Peru/Honduras, Peru/Colombia, or Peru/Honduras/Colombia, with significant genetic differentiation among populations based on AMOVA (Phi-samples-total = 0.18,
p
= 0.001). In terms of drug resistance, nine DHFR/DHPS haplotypes were identified. One wild-type haplotype (FSTS + ESAYA) grouped all samples from Honduras and a fraction from Peru (1.4%), three haplotypes accounted for all Colombian samples and a fraction from Peru (46.2%), and five haplotypes were specific for Peru (52.3%). Additionally, the DHFR: FRTN haplotype was present in all Colombian samples. This combination was also present in 46.2% of the Peruvian samples. These two haplotypes, when combined with the DHFR: FKTN (50.3%) haplotype, account for over 96.5% of the circulating haplotypes in Peru.
Conclusion:
In conclusion, our data indicates a consistent flow of
P. vivax
populations between the study areas. The distribution of DHFR variants reflects the historical use of SP as a fingerprint in each country: Colombia, which utilized SP for a longer duration, exhibits strong fixation of DHFR variants; Peru demonstrates moderate fixation; and Honduras, which never employed SP, maintains the wild-type DHFR. In this regard, the increasing trends of human migration in the region underscore the need for continuous surveillance and genotyping of circulating malaria strains across Central and South America to sustain progress towards malaria elimination.

Malaria Journal

T1-P12
Background:
Persistence of asymptomatic and sub-patent malaria is documented in various parts of India including Odisha. However, its burden and contribution to dynamics of malaria transmission is poorly understood. Asymptomatic infection being a significant reservoir for malaria transmission, early and precise identification of malaria is essential for effective management and control. The objective of the study was to assess the point prevalence of asymptomatic malaria in endemic villages of Odisha and to evaluate the performance of rapid diagnostic test (RDT) in comparison to nested PCR (nPCR).
Materials and methods:
A cross-sectional mass screening was conducted during June–August, 2024 in selected hot-spot villages of Kalahandi, Odisha. All blood samples collected from consented participants were preliminarily screened for malaria using RDT kit on the spot, whereas PCR diagnosis was conducted by nPCR [1] in all RDT positive samples and certain randomly selected RDT negative samples for confirmation of malaria species as well as to validate the accuracy of RDT test.
Results:
Of the 700 samples screened for malaria using RDT, 216 (30.86) were found to be positive (
P. falciparum
: 157
P. vivax:
36 and mixed infections: 23); 14 of which were symptomatic and rest were asymptomatic. Genus-specific PCR results confirmed presence of malaria in 119 (of which 37 were determined in n-PCR as
P. falciparum
, 24 as
P. vivax
and 58 as mixed infections) of total RDT positive samples and 5 (of which 02 were
P. falciparum
, 01
P. vivax
and 02 mixed infections) out of 132 RDT negative samples. Use of PCR negative primary product as template in n-PCR revealed presence of 27
P. falciparum
, 04
P. vivax
and 35 mixed infections in RDT positive individuals, whereas an unexpected (N = 102) number of 29
P. falciparum
, 12
P. vivax
and 61 mixed infections were detected amongst RDT negative individuals (N = 127). The positive predictive value by RDT was found to be 63.36% and accuracy of malaria detection to be 60.34%. Although diagnosis of
vivax
mono infection by RDT and PCR had no difference, both
P. falciparum
and
P. vivax
were underrepresented in mixed infections by RDT.
Conclusion:
Although underestimation of
falciparum
infection by RDT can be anticipated to be due to previous documentation of HRP-2/3 deletion variants in these regions [2] and/or low parasitaemia, reasons for RDT failure in diagnosing
vivax
particularly in mixed infections is unknown. Current findings of poor diagnosis of asymptomatic malaria by RDT, detection failure in primary PCR but reappearance in n-PCR suggests requirement of improved/modified PCR diagnosis or use of highly sensitive molecular tool for screening of asymptomatic malaria in these regions. Further, screening of HRP2/3 deletions variants in all
falciparum
infections and quantification of parasite density for all malaria species causing asymptomatic infections is warranted to understand their transmission dynamics.
Acknowledgements:
We acknowledge MERA-India (ICMR) for funding of the research work, the State-Ethics Committee and medical administration of Kalahandi district, Odisha.
References:
Snounou G, Viriyakosol S, Jarra W, Thaithong S, Brown KN. Identification of the four human malaria parasite species in field samples by the polymerase chain reaction and detection of a high prevalence of mixed infections. Molecular and biochemical parasitology. 1993;58(2):283–92

T1-P13 High-altitude malaria transmission: persistence and local spread of
Plasmodium vivax
in the Ethiopian highlands
Gustavo da Silva
1
, Yalemwork Ewnetu
2
, Lise Carlier
3
, Wossenseged Lemma PhD
2
, Cristian Koepfli PhD
1
1
University of Notre Dame, Notre Dame, USA;
2
Gondar University, Gondar, Ethiopia;
3
Noul Inc, Yongin-si, South Korea
Correspondence:
Gustavo da Silva: gsilva2@nd.edu
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P13
Background:
High-altitude regions in Ethiopia, with altitudes exceeding 2600 m, challenge malaria elimination efforts due to cooler climates that reduce mosquito activity and limit parasite survival—yet transmission persists. Seasonal migration from lower altitudes to Gondar (2133 m), a major highland city, may introduce
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) clones to surrounding areas, including higher-elevation sites like Dabat (2600 m).
Materials and methods:
This study examines
P. vivax
transmission patterns across the Ethiopian highlands, focusing on allele flow between high-altitude regions, Gondar’s role as a transmission hub, and the influence of elevation gradients on gene flow. We sequenced 456 clinical
P. vivax
samples collected between 2019 and 2023 from eight sites in the Ethiopian highlands, spanning altitudes from 2100 to over 2900 m. Amplicon sequencing via a ddPCR-AmpSeq assay targeting 60 genomic regions provided fine-scale resolution of diversity and population structure. Identity-by-descent (IBD) analysis, combined with spatial and temporal data, enabled us to trace allele flow and detect distinct transmission pathways across sites.
Results:
Sites at higher altitudes (> 2600 m) exhibited distinct genetic profiles, including unique alleles that highlighted localized transmission. Gondar maintained the highest allelic richness (21.27), followed by nearby highland sites like Amba Giorgis (7.78), and other cities like Sanja (4.63). Strong genetic connections linked Gondar to surrounding areas, including 14 clonal pairs shared with higher-altitude sites, such as Dabat —where moderate genetic diversity H
e
0.44 was recorded. Gondar and Sanja accounted for over 40% of the observed connections, with alleles persisting for up to 92 days. These findings reinforce Gondar’s role as a regional reservoir and transmission hub.
Conclusion:
Our findings challenge assumptions about malaria’s limits in high-altitude environments, emphasizing the need for altitude-sensitive control measures. While Gondar’s genetic diversity and influence dominate transmission across elevations, high-altitude sites like Dabat exhibit resilience and may sustain localized transmission without relying solely on reintroductions. These results underscore the importance of considering elevation and migration patterns when designing malaria elimination strategies in Ethiopia and beyond.

Malaria Journal
