T1-P14
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
is the most predominant malaria species in Latin America and Southeast Asia. With several countries aiming for malaria elimination, it is crucial to prioritize effectiveness of national control programs by optimizing the utilization of available resources and strategically implementing necessary changes. To support this, there is a need for innovative approaches such as genomic surveillance tools that can investigate changes in transmission intensity, imported cases and sources of reintroduction, and can detect molecular markers associated with drug resistance.
Materials and methods:
Here we present a set of highly multiplexed sequencing assays (AmpliSeq) developed for targeted deep sequencing of
P. vivax
targeting specific genomic regions of interest. These assays combine phenotypic and population genetic markers to study parasite dynamics, which has been successfully piloted in several countries [1,2]. Samples from previously approved studies with written consent for future use for malaria research were included and secondary use for the purpose of genomic surveillance of malaria was approved through the Institutional Review Board of ITM Antwerp (reference 1417/20) [1,2].
We demonstrate genetic surveillance use cases, such as the origin of imported
P. vivax
cases in Belgium (n = 90). In addition, we investigated parasite connectivity and population structure in Vietnam (n = 261) and Peru (n = 230). Analysis of the population dynamics across the Amazon region is in progress (n = 520, including 267 additional samples from Peru, Brazil, Colombia and Honduras). Furthermore, these sample collections allow us to map haplotypes in potential drug resistance associated genes across the globe.
Results:
Global
P. vivax
populations cluster by geographic origin into four continental groups. In Peru, we observed differences between peri-urban, remote and border communities, with a heterogenous high diversity population in the Loreto region, and moderate to low diversity in remote communities. In one border community, a clonal cluster was identified, with distinct haplotypes in resistant genes, representing an introduction from Brazil based on the predicted origin. Identity-by-descent relatedness analysis in Vietnam indicated high parasite connectivity between coastal provinces, and a distinct population, with high levels of relatedness, in a remote highland province. Alleles contributing to the global separation include variants in
pvmdr2
,
pvdmt2
,
pvmdr1
,
pvdhps
and
pvdhfr,
with high variability in these genes whereas low variability was observed in
pvcrt
and
pvK13.
In travelers, the spatial specificity of the vivaxGEO-barcode performs well for between-country prediction of origin, while our dedicated barcodes have increased resolution for within-country spatio-temporal surveillance.
Conclusion:
Our approach performs well in geographically differentiating isolates at diverse levels, detecting variants in resistance markers, while demonstrating applicability to serve various use cases. It has been used in Southeast Asia, Latin America and Europe and can be adjusted to suit the needs of other settings. As molecular surveillance platforms transition to incorporation into NMCPs, simple standardized laboratory protocols feasible on benchtop sequencers and automated analysis are necessary to generate reproducible results. We prioritize putting this tool in the hands of researchers and control programs in endemic countries to increase ownership and ensure data usage for decision-making and policy.
References:
Kattenberg JH, Nguyen HV, Nguyen HL, Sauve E, Nguyen NTH, Chopo-Pizarro A, et al. Novel highly-multiplexed AmpliSeq targeted assay for
Plasmodium vivax
genetic surveillance use cases at multiple geographical scales. Front Cell Infect Microbiol. 2022; 12:953187.
Kattenberg JH, Cabrera-Sosa L, Figueroa-Ildefonso E, Mutsaers M, Monsieurs P, Guetens P, et al.
Plasmodium vivax
genomic surveillance in the Peruvian Amazon with Pv AmpliSeq assay. PLoS Negl Trop Dis. 2024;18(7): e0011879.
T1-P15 A new
Plasmodium vivax
reference genome for South American isolates
Katlijn De Meulenaere

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P15
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) accounts for the majority of the malaria cases in South America. While high-quality reference genomes are available for isolates from Papua Indonesia (PvP01) and Thailand (PvW1), a corresponding reference genome for South America was lacking. Given the substantial genetic diversity and geographical clustering observed in
P. vivax
, a reference genome specific to the South American population would be highly valuable. This study introduces a new high-quality assembly of a
P. vivax
isolate from the Peruvian Amazon, named PvPAM (
P. vivax
Peruvian AMazon) [1].
Materials and methods:
A blood sample was collected from a
P. vivax
patient in Iquitos (Peru) with 1.0% parasitaemia, and leukocyte depleted. After DNA extraction of 100µL of infected red blood cells, 9 ng of DNA was obtained. Using the Ultra-Low DNA Input protocol, a library was prepared for PacBio sequencing. Reads were de novo assembled using Canu, scaffolded onto PvP01 and PvW1, and polished with Illumina reads. To assess the quality of the obtained reference genome, publicly available whole genome sequencing (WGS) data from different continents were collected, aligned against PvPAM, PvP01 and PvW1, and the quality of the alignment (SNPs, indels, large structural variants) was compared.
Results:
A highly-complete assembly was obtained, in which 6497 functional genes were detected. Telomeric ends were present in 17/28 chromosomal ends, and additional (sub)telomeric regions were identified in the remaining 12 unassigned contigs. Mapping of publicly available South American WGS data to PvPAM resulted in significantly fewer variants and truncated reads compared to the use of PvP01 or PvW1. To minimize the number of core genome variants in non-South American samples, PvW1 is most suited for Southeast Asian isolates, both PvPAM and PvW1 are suited for South Asian isolates, and PvPAM is recommended for South American as well as African isolates. Interestingly, when looking at subtelomeric variants, PvPAM results in the least variants for isolates from all continents, indicating high quality of the longer PvPAM subtelomeric regions. Furthermore, a remarkable variation in
vir
genes between PvPAM and PvP01 was revealed, together with the presence of MSP3.6 and 3.7 in PvPAM.
Conclusions:
Our findings demonstrate that the PvPAM reference genome more accurately represents South American
P. vivax
isolates in comparison to PvP01 and PvW1. In addition, PvPAM has a high level of completeness, and contains a similar number of annotated genes as PvP01 or PvW1. Therefore, the PvPAM genome is a valuable resource to improve future genomic analyses on
P. vivax
isolates from the South American continent.
References:
De Meulenaere K, Cuypers B, Gamboa D, Laukens K, Rosanas-Urgell A. A new
Plasmodium vivax
reference genome for South American isolates. BMC Genomics. 2023; 24: 606.

Malaria Journal

T1-P16
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
or
Pv
) is the most predominant malaria species in Latin America (LATAM), accounting for 72% of cases in 2022. Genomic surveillance tools are essential for national malaria control and elimination programs to understand transmission dynamics, connectivity between regions, and the spread of molecular resistance markers. Recently, we developed the
Pv
AmpliSeq v2 Peru assay, a novel highly multiplexed deep next-generation sequencing (NGS) tool that includes validated and candidate genes associated with antimalarial resistance, a global
P. vivax
SNP-barcode for prediction of origin, and a Peru-specific SNP-barcode for population genetics analysis [1]. Here, we aimed to investigate the transmission dynamics and parasite connectivity of
P. vivax
populations in the Amazon basin using our
Pv
AmpliSeq v2 Peru assay.
Materials and methods:
We analyzed the genomic data retrieved from
P. vivax
PCR-positive samples from 3 regions in Peru: Loreto (n = 254), Amazonas (n = 68), and Tumbes (n = 21), as well as samples from five other LATAM countries: Brazil (n = 100), Colombia (n = 18), Ecuador (n = 22), Venezuela (n = 31), and Honduras (n = 6). All samples used here came from previously approved studies with written consent for future use for malaria research and secondary use for the purpose of malaria genomic surveillance was approved through the Institutional Review Boards of UPCH (208510) and ITM Antwerp (reference 1417/20). Metrics of the genetic diversity, population structure, parasite connectivity, complexity of infection, and prediction of origin of infections were estimated.
Results:
Heterogeneous transmission patterns across LATAM were noted. In Peru, 3 population clusters per region were identified. Colombian parasites clustered with some populations present in Venezuela and Honduras, while the ones from Ecuador and Loreto were related. Low to moderate genetic diversity level was found (He: 0.15–0.40), where Honduras parasites exhibited the lowest diversity. Parasites from Peru, Ecuador, and Colombia showed low differentiation, although the Amazonas and Tumbes regions were the most different. Identity-by-descent relatedness analysis showed clonal populations in Peru, Venezuela, and Brazil and some connectivity among Peru, Ecuador, Brazil and Venezuela. Polyclonal infections in all countries were at moderate proportions (10–47.1%). Most parasites from Brazil, Colombia, and Loreto were predicted to originate in-country (predicted value: 60–100%). In contrast, parasites from Amazonas and Tumbes were predicted to have originated mainly from Colombia, while parasites from Ecuador and Venezuela were likely of Peruvian origin (94%).
Conclusion:
Our study showed high heterogeneity in
P. vivax
transmission dynamics, with moderate parasite connectivity among countries and complex population structure across LATAM. The
Pv
AmpliSeq v2 Peru assay is a valid malaria molecular surveillance tool in the region, and its implementation in surveillance systems should be encouraged. Broader implementation of this assay in Peru and other LATAM countries will help to reveal transmission routes, sources of introduction, and connectivity networks and can guide strategies in national programs, significantly contributing to malaria control and elimination for civilian and military populations.
References:
Kattenberg JH, Cabrera-Sosa L, Figueroa-Ildefonso E, Mutsaers M, Monsieurs P, Guetens P, et al.
Plasmodium vivax
genomic surveillance in the Peruvian Amazon with Pv AmpliSeq assay. PLoS Negl Trop Dis. 2024;18(7): e0011879
T1-P17 Assessing the performance of SNP barcode panels compared with microsatellites in investigating the population genetics of malaria parasites in the Peruvian Amazon

Malaria Journal
2025,

T1-P17
Background:
Malaria genomic surveillance can provide insights into transmission dynamics and guide national control programs. Microsatellites (MS) markers are used routinely to study
Plasmodium
population genetics [1]. Recently, new genomic surveillance platforms, such as AmpliSeq assays, have become available, allowing researchers to simultaneously analyze multiple targeted genes within a parasite's genome [2]. This study aims to compare the similarity of results, limitations, and advantages of each method, along with their applications across different scenarios. The findings offer a guideline for future studies to select the most appropriate method based on specific research questions and practical considerations, such as cost, available infrastructure, and feasibility.
Materials and methods:
Blood samples (n = 51) were collected from remote areas of the Peruvian Amazon. DNA extraction was followed by molecular diagnosis using real-time PCR. Using a 41-SNP barcode AmpliSeq assay, parasite DNA was sequenced and genomic data were analyzed according to best practices established by the MalariaGEN consortium. In addition, a previously developed panel of 16 MS markers was used to genotype the parasite DNA. For both SNP and MS panels, population genetic parameters, including expected heterozygosity (He) and fixation index (F
st
), were calculated. Population structure was examined using principal component analysis (PCA) and admixture analysis. The complexity of infection (COI) was assessed to determine the proportion of polyclonal infections.
Results:
The genetic diversity was similar across the subpopulations for both methods:
He
MS
= 0.68–0.78 (p = 0.23) and
He
SNP
= 0.36–0.38 (p = 0.80). Pairwise genetic differentiation (F
ST
) was also comparable: F
ST-MS
= 0.04–0.14 and F
ST-SNP
= 0.03 − 0.12 (p = 0.34–0.85). The population structure analyses revealed comparable clustering patterns between the AmpliSeq assay and the MS panel. However, the AmpliSeq assay demonstrated slightly greater precision, resulting in more clearly defined groupings of individuals compared to the broader clustering observed with the MS panel. PCA analysis confirmed patterns observed in the admixture analysis. AmpliSeq assay demonstrated higher costs compared to MS panel, with an estimated price of $183 per sample versus $49 for MS panel. However, personnel costs were significantly lower for AmpliSeq, at $5 per sample compared to $11–$19 for MS, mainly due to the faster turnaround time.
Conclusion:
The AmpliSeq assay provided results comparable to MS panel in population genetics analyses, including transmission intensity and population structure. Given their similarities, the choice between these methods should be guided by research objectives and practical factors, including expenses, resource availability.
References:
Gwarinda HB et al. Parasite genetic diversity reflects continued residual malaria transmission in Vhembe District, a hotspot in the Limpopo Province of South Africa. Malaria Journal. 2021; 20: 1–13.
Kattenberg JH et al. Malaria molecular surveillance in the Peruvian Amazon with a novel highly multiplexed
Plasmodium falciparum
AmpliSeq assay. Microbiology Spectrum. 2023; 11(2): 00960–22.
T1-P18 Understanding
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
prevalence trends in co-endemic regions on a global scale using a multi-species modelling framework approach
Richard J. Sheppard, Giovanni D. Charles, Nora Schmit, Shazia N. Ruybal-Pesántez, Thomas R. Brewer, Gina Cuomo-Dannenburg, Peter Winskill
MRC Centre for Global Infectious Disease Analysis, School of Public Health, Imperial College, London, UK
Correspondence:
Richard J. Sheppard (richard.sheppard11@imperial.ac.uk)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P18
Background:
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
are co-endemic across many regions of the world, but estimated prevalence trends vary substantially. Inherent biological differences between these parasite species lead to variations in dynamic transmission patterns and malaria intervention responses that are critical to understand for optimisation of control strategies in co-endemic regions. Transmission modelling can be used to explore and explain these trends and assess the differential intervention impact on both species, but few models exist that integrate both species for adequate dynamic simulation. We aim to understand and explain these prevalence trends using a multi-species modelling approach with flexible intervention capabilities.
Materials and methods:
We extend the capabilities of
malariasimulation
[1], a detailed and accessible open-source individual-based
P. falciparum
transmission model, to simulate
P. vivax
prevalence. We use the
P. vivax
model of White et al. (2018) [2] as a template, which includes
P. vivax
specific biology including hypnozoite infection, relapse and immunity dynamics. By conducting analyses of aligned simulation outputs for each parasite species, we modelled sub-national trends in
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
to investigate the extent to which Malaria Atlas Project prevalence estimates [3] for both species around the world can be replicated and explained. We investigated the impact of clinical treatment coverage, insecticide-treated net distributions, parasite misdiagnosis, masking of
P. vivax
in co-infection, within-host parasite competition, relapse rate and the infection preventative effect of Duffy negativity on
P. vivax
.
Results:
Our model broadly replicates estimates of regional co-endemic prevalence trends around the world (Fig. 1) and explains these with inherent parasite-specific biological characteristics captured in the model (e.g., hypnozoite reservoirs and relapse). We find that species-specific non-linear relationships between prevalence and mosquito density drive a higher prevalence of
P. falciparum
compared with
P. vivax
at high transmission intensity, while this relationship reverses at low transmission intensity, consistent with observed prevalence estimates. The modelled impact of malaria interventions that affect both species consistently shows greater decreases in
P. falciparum
prevalence compared with
P. vivax
, a finding consistent with established epidemiological data. Estimates from regions of low
P.vivax
prevalence require additional explanatory factors such as misdiagnosis of
P. vivax
infections as
P. falciparum
, variation in hypnozoite relapse rate and protection from infection via Duffy-negativity prevalence.
Conclusion:
This work introduces a key tool for understanding and predicting
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
prevalence trends in co-endemic regions and demonstrates the reduced impact of malaria interventions on
P. vivax
. We note that inherent biological parasite differences must be considered to accurately model malaria dynamics in co-endemic regions and recommend our model for use during sub-national tailoring of interventions. Our findings also highlight the need for greater understanding of the level and impact of misdiagnosis on parasite-specific prevalence estimates and the impact of Duffy-negativity on parasite transmission as next steps for improved model application.
Fig. 1
Modelled co-endemic prevalences capture global prevalence trends through time. Arrows show estimated
P. falciparum
and
vivax
prevalence trends (2000–2019), colored by k-means clustering (k = 6). The black line with ribbon shows the equilibrium solution with 95% credible intervals, accounting for regional Duffy-negativity and hypnozoite relapse rate
References:
Charles G, Winskill P, Topazian H, Challenger J, Fitzjohn R, Sheppard R, Brewer T, McCain K, Haile L.
malariasimulation: An individual based model for malaria
. R package version 2.0.0. 2024.
White MT, Walker P, Karl S, Hetzel MW, Freeman T, Waltmann A, Laman M, Robinson LJ, Ghani A, Mueller I. Mathematical modelling of the impact of expanding levels of malaria control interventions on
Plasmodium vivax
. Nature communications. 2018;9(1):3300.
Hay SI, Snow RW. The Malaria Atlas Project: developing global maps of malaria risk. PLoS medicine. 2006;3(12): e473.
T1-P19 The prevalence of
Plasmodium vivax
and G6PD deficiency in the Mandoto district in Madagascar and the Shashemene district in Ethiopia, results of a large observational study in preparation of the PvSTATEM cluster-randomized study

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P19
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) malaria contributes significantly to the burden of malaria in Northeast Africa and Madagascar. Insights in the epidemiology of
P. vivax
is crucial for malaria control efforts. Radical cure of
Plasmodium vivax
involves the use of 8-aminoquinolines such as primaquine and tafenoquine. There is a need to determine the epidemiology of G6PD deficiency in areas affected by
P. vivax
, as the use of 8-aminoquinolines in G6PD deficient patients can result in hemolysis.
Materials and methods:
In the PvSTATEM study, which will start in March 2025, individuals, randomized by clusters, will be tested for the presence of serological markers of a recent
P. vivax
infection, followed by a targeted drug treatment intervention aimed at killing both
P. vivax
blood stage infections and hypnozoites. In preparation of this study, we conducted a large observational study assessing the prevalence of PCR detected
P. vivax
malaria in 72 clusters (villages) in the Mandoto district in Madagascar and the Shashemene district in Ethiopia. Demographic data, malaria treatment history and GPS locations of study participants were obtained. In addition, the G6PD enzyme activity of participants was obtained using the SD biosensor point-of-care test.
Results:
Between November 2023 and November 2024, over 7,553 participants were recruited in Ethiopia and 3,562 participants were recruited in Madagascar. In Ethiopia, 57 out of 2024 (2.8%) samples tested positive for
Plasmodium vivax
. In Madagascar, 202 out of 3562 (5.7%) samples tested positive for
Plasmodium vivax
. In ongoing analysis that will be completed at the time of the ICPvR conference, we link the prevalence of
P. vivax
to geographic localization, and demographic factors such as age, occupation and gender.
G6PD enzyme activity was determined in 1,518 participants. Enzyme activity was found to be deficient (≤ 3.9 U/g Hb) in 4 out of 790 (0.5%) individuals in Ethiopia and 57 out of 728 (7.8%) individuals in Madagascar.
Conclusion:
This study provides up-to-date insights into the prevalence of
P. vivax
in two heavily affected districts in Madagascar and Ethiopia, and links these results to population demographics. In addition, our study contributes to the limited available data on G6PD enzyme activity in both Madagascar and Ethiopia. Finally, our presentation will provide insights into the way we will have selected clusters for the PvSTATEM cluster-randomized trial.
T1-P20 Genomic dynamics of patient derived parasites: Comparative genomic hybridization of
Plasmodium vivax
causing severe disease
Sampreeti Tahbildar

, Pon A Boopathi

, Mohamed Aiyaz

, Dhanpat K Kochar

, Ashis Das

2

Correspondence:

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P20
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
), a widespread malaria-causing parasite, was once considered benign, but recent studies reveal its potential for severe symptoms similar to
Plasmodium falciparum
(
P. falciparum
), including hepatic dysfunction, cerebral malaria, acute respiratory distress syndrome etc. [1]. The parasite’s genome shows significant structural variations, including copy number variations (CNVs), which may play a crucial role in the parasites’ pathogenesis. Recently, PvDBP gene amplification has been reported as a mechanism used by
P. vivax
to evade humoral immunity targeting PvDBP [2]. This study uses customized microarray based comparative genomic hybridization (CGH tiling array) to detect some novel CNVs not previously documented in
P. vivax
genome between patient isolates showing cerebral malaria (CM) and uncomplicated malaria.
Materials and methods:
This study utilizes a custom
P. vivax
2 × 400 K comparative genomic hybridization (CGH) tiling microarray with an average probe spacing of 56 bp, providing comprehensive coverage of the entire genome, including intergenic and intronic regions. Blood samples were collected from patients infected with
P. vivax
following informed consent and ethical approval.
P. vivax
infection was confirmed via microscopy and rapid diagnostic tests. Multiplex PCR confirmed the absence of
P. falciparum
co-infection. DNA was extracted, purified, and labeled with Cy3 (control/uncomplicated malaria) and Cy5 dyes (test/CM), then hybridized on the microarray. Data analysis involved image normalization using Agilent Feature Extraction Software, with aberrations identified through the ADM-2 algorithm.
Results:
Utilizing probes based on the
P. vivax
Sal-1 reference genome, we detected significant copy number variations (CNVs) across 14 chromosomes, affecting 2,138 genes. We have identified CNVs ranging from 100 bp to 1429 kb in the cerebral malaria isolates compared to the uncomplicated malaria isolates. Regions showing CNVs included genes linked to surface antigens such as 6-cysteine proteins, tryptophan-rich antigens (TRags), serine-repeat antigen (SERA), apical membrane antigen (AMA), and drug resistance markers. The most extensive CNV was identified on chromosome 12, spanning approximately 1450 kb. CNVs were also observed in intergenic regions, potentially impacting gene regulation. These findings suggest that CNVs in
P. vivax
may play a crucial role in pathogenesis, highlighting the complex genetic landscape of the parasite in different malaria manifestations.
Conclusion:
This study identified significant copy number variations (CNVs) in
P. vivax
field isolates associated with cerebral malaria, affecting key genes like surface antigens, drug resistance markers, and exported proteins. While the direct impact of these variations on disease severity remains unclear, the findings highlight distinct genetic differences between isolates from severe and uncomplicated malaria cases, including variations in intergenic regions. These differences may influence gene regulation or reflect inherent parasite diversity. Investigating these genomic variations could reveal mechanisms of immune evasion and survival employed by
P. vivax
, might also offer insights into the emergence of drug resistance, and ultimately guiding the development of effective malaria control strategies.
Acknowledgements:
We acknowledge Birla Institute of Technology and Science, Pilani, India and S.P. Medical College, Bikaner, India for all the infrastructural facilities during this study. We thank all the patients for their voluntary consent (Ethical clearance Ref No: No.F.(Acad) SPMC/2003/2395, No. F29(Acad)SPMC/2020/3151).
References:
Kochar DK, Das A, Kochar SK, Saxena V, Sirohi P, Garg S, et al. Severe
Plasmodium vivax
malaria: a report on serial cases from Bikaner in northwestern India. Am J Trop Med Hyg. 2009;80(2):194–8.
Popovici J, Roesch C, Carias LL, Khim N, Kim S, Vantaux A, et al. Amplification of Duffy binding protein-encoding gene allows
Plasmodium vivax
to evade host anti-DBP humoral immunity. Nat Commun. 2020;11(1):953.

Varijakshi Gutthedhar

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P21
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) poses considerable obstacles to malaria elimination in India due to its persistence and spread through asymptomatic carriers harboring gametocytes, which serve as reservoirs for transmission. Targeting these gametocytes is crucial for interrupting the parasite’s life cycle; however, the lack of effective in vitro cultivation methods limits study to clinical isolates. Genetic diversity of
P. vivax
across endemic malaria regions may lead to variations in gene expression and immune responses, impacting local transmission dynamics [1]. This study conducts transcriptional profiling of gametocyte-specific genes from clinical isolates in south Karnataka to identify regional expression patterns and enhance understanding of these dynamics.
Materials and methods:
Human blood samples infected with
P. vivax
were collected from the adult population of south Karnataka from Kasturba Hospital, Manipal and Dr. T.M.A. Pai Hospital, Udupi, with ethics approval (IEC No.: 440/2020) during rainy and dry seasons. Total RNA was extracted using the TRIzol method, and RT-qPCR was performed to quantify the expression of key gametocyte-specific genes, including
PVP01_0616100
(ookinete surface protein P25),
PVP01_0415800
(6-cysteine protein P230),
PVP01_1208100
(6-cysteine protein P48/45), and
PVP01_0814300
(male gamete fusion protein HAP2). Transition-related gametocyte genes analyzed were
PVP01_1008400
(7-helix-1 protein),
PVP01_1322800
(ABC transporter G family member 2) [2],
PVP01_0909500
(heat shock protein J2), and
PVP01_0904300
and
PVP01_1223200
(both CPW-WPC family proteins).
PVP01_0313500
(
seryl-tRNA synthetase
) was used as the endogenous control for normalization to gametocyte stage V focusing on its role in transmission.
Results:
Transcriptional profiling of clinical isolates identified the most highly expressed genes associated with key gametocyte development and transition-related processes. Among the gametocyte development genes, both
PVP01_0616100
(ookinete surface protein P25) and
PVP01_0415800
(6-cysteine protein P230) exhibited highest levels of expression. In contrast, the transition-related
PVP01_1322800
(ABC transporter G family member 2) gene was notably expressed at elevated levels. Remarkably, the expression profiles of these genes remained stable across samples collected in both seasons, underscoring their robustness and indicating that seasonal variations do not significantly influence their expression.
Conclusion:
This research reveals that
PVP01_0616100
and
PVP01_0415800
, known gametocyte-specific genes, are highly expressed in clinical isolates of
P. vivax
from south Karnataka, affirming their roles in gametocyte development and transmission. Additionally, the high expression of
PVP01_1322800
among transition related genes underscores its significance in the parasite's adaptation to host immune pressures and drug resistance. Notably, the expression levels of these genes remain stable across samples collected in both seasons, suggesting that seasonal conditions do not significantly impact their expression. Clinical samples remain invaluable for studying in vivo transcriptional activity, establishing a baseline for future gene expression comparisons across India, enhancing our understanding of transmission dynamics.
Acknowledgements:
Financial support from Intramural grant (MAHE/DREG/PHD/IMF/2019) and Dr. TMA Pai Fellowship provided by Manipal Academy of Higher Education; ICMR Senior Research Fellowship (128/2022-ECD-II) awarded to Varijakshi, the infrastructure and instrumentation facilities provided by the Manipal School of Life Sciences, Manipal Academy of Higher Education, Manipal, are gratefully acknowledged.

Plasmodium vivax
geographical isolates and implications on erythrocyte invasion mechanisms. PLoS Negl Trop Dis. 2024;18(1).

Plasmodium falciparum
: a review of current knowledge and future directions. 3 Biotech. 2023;13(10):344.
T1-P22 Genomic and Proteomic Analysis of Subspecies of

: Decoding the Mechanism of Refractoriness

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P22
Background:
Anopheles culicifacies
, a major vector of malaria in South Asia, comprises several sibling species with distinct biological and behavioural characteristics. Notably, some sibling species exhibit a refractory nature to
Plasmodium vivax
, presenting unique challenges and opportunities in malaria control and elimination efforts. Understanding the genetic, ecological, and physiological factors underlying this refractoriness is crucial for designing targeted interventions. This study explores vector-parasite interactions, shedding light on the mechanisms of refractoriness and their implications for malaria transmission dynamics and control strategies.
Materials and methods:
Mosquitoes will be collected from field locations and adapted to controlled laboratory conditions. Molecular techniques, including D3 domain ribosomal DNA (rDNA) PCR and BCE-PCR, will be used to identify sibling species, focusing on subspecies A and B. Proteins from these subspecies will be extracted and compared using two-dimensional sodium dodecyl sulfate–polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (2D SDS-PAGE) to detect differential protein expression. Identified proteins will undergo further analysis to investigate their roles in conferring refractoriness to
Plasmodium vivax
. This integrative approach combines molecular identification and proteomic analysis to unravel mechanisms of refractoriness.
Results:
DNA analysis revealed significant differences between subspecies groups A, D, and B, C, E. Proteins unique to subspecies B were identified, and characterization of these proteins is ongoing. These findings suggest that unique proteins in subspecies B may explain its inability to carry the malaria parasite.
Conclusion:
Subspecies B likely possesses distinctive genetic and protein traits that render it resistant to malaria parasites. Understanding these traits provides valuable insights for developing targeted mosquito control strategies and reducing malaria transmission.

Malaria Journal

T1-P23
Background:
It is an important step for designing a malaria vaccine, identifying highly immunogenic antigen which can be immunogenic in different eco-epidemiological areas. In October 2023 WHO approved a vaccine R21/Matrix-M which comprises PfCSP central repeat region and C-terminal region. Similarly, efforts are being made to design a vaccine with PvCSP to treat the malaria caused by
Plasmodium vivax
(
Pv
)
.
The
Plasmodium vivax
sporozoite surface protein circumsporozoite protein (PvCSP), whose role in sporozoite development, motility and cell invasion, has made it the leading pre erythrocytic malaria vaccine candidate. Here we performed a serological survey against recombinant PvCSP with
Pv
positive sera samples collected from different malaria endemic regions of India to have an insight to the immune status of people living in these epidemiological areas.
Materials and methods:
Recombinant expression of PvCSP and biochemical characterization: Recombinant PvCSP protein was expressed in E. coli expression system in soluble form and specifically recognized by monoclonal antibodies and characterized biochemically.
Blood collection: Approximately 3-5 ml of human blood samples were collected intravenously. Serum was separated by using centrifuge at 5000 rpm, 4 °C and stored at -20 °C.
ELISA: Indirect ELISA was performed by coating 1 μg PvCSP antigen in 96 well plate. Human serum sample were used as primary antibody in 1:50 dilution and HRP labelled anti-human IgG antibody were used as secondary antibody in 1:5000 dilution. Then developed by using TMB substrate and absorption were recorded at 450 nm.
Results:
PvCSP gene was successfully cloned in pEt28a plasmid and then transformed in DH5α and BL21-DE3 E. coli cells. Soluble form of PvCSP protein were then expressed and confirmed with anti-PvCSP antibodies. ELISA was performed against recombinant PvCSP with
Pv
positive,
Plasmodium falciparum
(
Pf
) positive and uninfected serum samples. Sero reactivity was higher in
Pv
positive sera samples in comparison to the
Pf
positive and uninfected samples. The difference in seroprevalence and factors associated with antibody responses at each site was statistically analyzed. Antibody response was more in mid aged person (20–50) in comparison to the children’s and older aged persons.
Conclusion:
Antibody response has shown that PvCSP is highly immunogenic in nature and the recombinant PvCSP was specifically recognized by the antibodies which are present in serum. The data shows that there is no cross reactivity against PvCSP antigens in the
Pf
positive and uninfected samples. Results of this study will support the malaria vaccine program against the
Plasmodium vivax
and give a broad view to the immune status of the people living in the malaria endemic regions.
T1-P24 Cytosolic iron-sulfur cluster assembly pathway from
Plasmodium vivax
Sayantani Chatterjee
1
, Vishal Saxena
1
, Nidhi Bub
1
, Subhiksha Dhanabala
1
, Sanjay Kochar
2
, Shilpi Garg
1
1
Department of Biological Sciences, Birla Institute of Technology & Science (BITS), Pilani, Pilani, Rajasthan, India;
2
Department of Medicine, S.P. Medical College, P. B. M. and Associated Group of Hospitals, Bikaner, Rajasthan, India
Correspondence:
Shilpi Garg (shilpi@pilani.bits-pilani.ac.in)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T1-P24
Background:
Iron-sulfur [Fe-S] cluster biogenesis pathway is one of the important housekeeping mechanisms present in all domains of life. In nature, four types of major [Fe-S] cluster biogenesis pathways are known, i.e., ISC (iron-sulfur cluster), SUF (sulfur utilization factor), NIF (nitrogen fixation), and CIA (cytosolic iron-sulfur cluster assembly) systems. The components of these pathways are known to form both 2Fe-2S and 4Fe-4S clusters and transfer them to target apo-proteins. In
Plasmodium
, there is substantial evidence of ISC and SUF pathways, however there is only one report on the CIA pathway detailing the Dre2 protein. Our current study attempts to elucidate the role of some other major components of the cytoplasmic Fe-S cluster biogenesis machinery.
Materials and methods:
In the present work, functional delineation of some components of the target pathway were done based on transcriptomic data analysis. We predicted the localisation of the protein by using different online available softwares like Mitoprot, PlasmoAP, NLS Mapper, SignalP, etc. Amplification of the target genes was done from clinical isolates and sequenced. The amplicons were cloned and the expressed protein was used to raise antibodies. Immuno-localization studies were performed to confirm the protein translation during different stages of the parasite life cycle.
Results:
There are about 8–9 major proteins known to function in the cytoplasmic Fe–S cluster biogenesis (CIA Pathway) from humans and yeast. We have identified all homologues of these proteins in the
Plasmodium
database few of which are designated as hypothetical or putative. Among these, characterization of 4 proteins including CIA1, CIA 2 was performed. The obtained sequences of
P. vivax
field isolates showed high conservation with both
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
database sequences. The transcriptome of the target proteins indicated a stage specific differential expression. Further the expressed proteins were validated through western blotting and co-localized in the cytoplasm/nucleus of the parasite.
Conclusion:
This study gives insights into the cytosolic iron-sulfur cluster assembly functional in
P. vivax
, that will help draw new therapeutic approaches in our fight for malaria elimination.
Theme 2: Curing malaria: clinical trials, therapeutics, & vaccines
T2-O1 The safety, and feasibility of a revised case management package for patients with
P. vivax
malaria in Indonesia and Papua New Guinea (PNG)
Jeanne Rini Poespoprodjo
1
, Maria Ome-Kaius
2
1
Papuan Community Health and Development Foundation, Timika, Papua, Indonesia;
2
PNG Institute of Medical Research, Papua New Guinea.
Correspondence:
Jeanne Rini Poespoprodjo (didot2266@yahoo.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-O1
Background:
Outside of sub-Saharan Africa,
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) malaria is becoming the predominant cause of malaria and a key barrier to elimination. Primaquine is the most widely available drug with anti-relapse activity against dormant stages of
P. vivax,
but recent metanalyses highlight the need for high dose primaquine regimens (7 mg/kg total), which increase the risk of severe haemolysis in patients with glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency. The SCOPE study was conducted to determine the safety, feasibility and cost-effectiveness of a revised case management package for patients with
P. vivax
malaria in Indonesia and Papua New Guinea (PNG).
Materials and methods:
The revised case management package includes: (i) point-of-care quantitative testing of G6PD activity; (ii) treatment with high dose (7 mg/kg total) primaquine over 7 days (PQ7) for G6PD normal patients, 14 days (PQ14) for intermediate patients, or 8 weeks (PQ8W; 0.75 mg/kg/week) for deficient patients; (iii) patients’ education prior to treatment; (iv) clinical review on day 3 and day 7 and (v) enhanced community pharmacovigilance. The study consists of two stages. Safety review of the primaquine treatment (Stage 1) was conducted in two clinics in Indonesia and two in PNG, enrolling consenting non-pregnant patients, > 6 months old with uncomplicated
vivax
malaria who had a haemoglobin concentration > 8 g/dl. Feasibility and cost effectiveness assessment of the intervention (Stage 2) will be carried out in 6 clinics in Indonesia and 4 clinics in PNG in 2025.
Results:
Preliminary results demonstrate that high dose primaquine treatment in G6PD normal patient was relatively well tolerated. The full results of the study will be presented including the risk of adverse events of special interest (severe gastrointestinal symptoms, haemolysis, and methaemoglobinaemia). The feasibility of the intervention will be gauged by the accuracy of G6PD testing, correct administration of PQ dose, and the proportion of patients who underwent clinical reviews on day 3 and 7. A qualitative evaluation of potential barriers in the scale-up of the novel case management will also be presented.
Conclusion:
Stage 1 of the SCOPE study will define the feasibility of an important novel intervention package designed to improve the safety and effectiveness of high dose short course primaquine radical cure for
P. vivax
malaria.
Acknowledgements:
This work was conducted on behalf of the Short COurse PrimaquinE for the radical cure of
P. vivax
(SCOPE Study) and funded by UNITAID. We acknowledge Medicines for Malaria Venture (MMV) and the Menzies School of Health Research as a partner and thank all collaborating institutions and field teams across Indonesia and Papua New Guinea for their essential support and contributions.
TrialR egistration:
This study was registered on clinicaltrials.gov:
NCT05879224
.
T2-O2 Clinical performance of the STANDARD™ G6PD Test for identifying cases of severe anemia in malaria case management
Arunansu Talukdar
1
, Santasabuj Das
2
, Sandip Mukherjee

, Supriya Sharma

, Naseem Ahmed

, Maria Kahn

, Greg Bizilj

, Benedikt Ley

, Ric N Price

, Nicole Advani

, Emily Gerth-Guyette

, Pooja Bansil
4
, Stephanie Zobrist
4
, Gonzalo J Domingo, Abhijit Sharma
8
, Sampa Pal
4
1
Medical College Hospital, Kolkata, India;
2
National Institute of Cholera and Enteric Disease (NICED-ICMR), Kolkata, India;
3
National Institute of Malaria Research (NIMR-ICMR), Delhi, India;
4
Diagnostics, PATH, Seattle, Washington, United States;
5
Global and Tropical Health Division, Menzies School of Health Research and Charles Darwin University, Darwin, NT, Australia;
6
Centre for Tropical Medicine and Global Health, Nuffield Department of Medicine, University of Oxford, United Kingdom;
7

8

Stephanie Zobrist (szobrist@path.org)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-O2
Background:
The STANDARD G6PD Test (SD Biosensor, Republic of Korea) is a point-of-care test for glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency that simultaneously provides a numerical value of G6PD enzyme activity (U/g Hb) and hemoglobin concentration (g/dL). This test aids in the detection of G6PD deficiency, a contraindication in the use of primaquine and tafenoquine, the only antimalarial drugs targeting the dormant liver stages (hypnozoites) of
Plasmodium vivax
and
Plasmodium ovale
malaria infections. Severe anemia, combined with malaria, is also a reason to withhold treatment with these hemolytic drugs. As such, the hemoglobin value provided by the STANDARD Test may inform treatment.
Materials and methods:
The STANDARD Test was evaluated among a febrile population enrolled at outpatient clinics in Kolkata, India, an area with high incidence of
vivax
malaria. The STANDARD Test was conducted on capillary specimens, alongside a comparator point-of-care hemoglobin test: the HemoCue 201+ (HemoCue AB, Sweden). Venous blood was collected, and a reference Complete Blood Count (CBC) was performed using an automated hematology analyzer. The capillary hemoglobin results of the STANDARD and HemoCue tests were compared against the reference venous CBC result through linear regression and Bland Altman plots. Test performance for identifying severely anemic cases in terms of sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value (PPV), and negative predictive value (NPV) was assessed. Analyses are presented for participants with complete results available for all tests. Implications for primaquine and tafenoquine eligibility among participants was considered based on the reference assay, HemoCue, and STANDARD hemoglobin test results.
Results:
In total, 951 participants were enrolled, 466 with available results for all tests. Nearly one-quarter (24%) had moderate or severe anemia. R-squared values were 0.83 and 0.84 for the STANDARD and HemoCue tests, respectively. No cases of severe anemia were misclassified by the STANDARD Test. For identifying severe anemia, the STANDARD Test showed higher sensitivity (100.0% [86.3%–100.0%]) than the HemoCue (92.0% [74.0%–99.0%]), though PPV was lower (51.0% vs 82.1%). If STANDARD Test results were used to exclude severely anemic patients (Hb ≤ 7 g/dL) from primaquine/tafenoquine, all cases of severe anemia by CBC (n = 25) would have been correctly identified, and an additional 24 patients with moderate anemia by CBC would have been excluded. If HemoCue results were used, 23/25 severely anemic cases by CBC would have been correctly identified, and 5 additional patients with moderate or no/mild anemia by CBC would have been excluded.
Conclusion:
A high proportion of participants presented with either moderate or severe anemia. When compared against the reference CBC assay, the STANDARD Test showed similar performance and patterns of misclassification as the HemoCue. No true cases of severe anemia were misclassified by the STANDARD Test, but it tended to overestimate moderate and severe anemia. In contrast, the HemoCue misclassified two severely anemic cases as moderate, but had higher overall PPV for the identification of severe anemia. The STANDARD G6PD Test can reliably identify patients likely to have severe anemia. This is of particular value in remote settings where alternative approaches to anemia assessment may not exist.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-O3
Background:
Vaccine candidates targeting
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) Duffy-binding protein region II (PvDBPII), a blood-stage antigen, were recently tested for efficacy in controlled human malaria infection trials in adults. These trials showed that delayed boosting with a protein-in-adjuvant vaccine partially inhibited parasite growth. The level of in vivo growth inhibition correlated with antibody responses; however, the mechanisms of antibody-mediated parasite growth inhibition are not well defined. To determine the breadth and the mechanisms of protective antibody responses that humans generate in response to PvDBPII-based vaccines, we isolated and characterised a large panel of monoclonal antibodies (mAbs) from humans vaccinated against PvDBPII.
Materials and methods:
PvDBPII-specific single cell sorted B cells were isolated from blood from adults vaccinated with either viral-vectored (ChAd63 and MVA) PvDBPII vaccines (
NCT04009096
) or protein in Matrix-M adjuvant vaccines (
NCT04201431
). Recombinant IgG mAbs were produced in mammalian cells. Antibody variable gene sequences were analysed using IMGT V-QUEST. ELISA and high throughput surface plasmon resonance (HT-SPR) were used to determine binding characteristics of each mAb, their ability to inhibit receptor binding and to identify mAb communities with competitive epitope binding interactions. The functional activity of each mAb was assessed with an in vitro parasite growth inhibition activity (GIA) assay using transgenic
P. knowlesi
expressing PvDBP.
Results:
182 anti-PvDBPII mAbs were isolated from eight vaccinees, two who received viral-vectored and six who received protein-in-adjuvant vaccines. Antibody variable gene sequence analysis showed diverse gene usage, with about half of the mAbs using unique gene combinations. All mAbs recognised conformational epitopes. The mAbs were sorted into six communities based on their pairwise competitive binding interactions. Two communities contained mAbs that inhibited binding of PvDBPII to its receptor—Duffy antigen receptor for chemokines (DARC). Assessment of in vitro GIA of each mAb showed that the majority were GIA positive, with GIA positive mAbs found in all six epitope communities. Most of the mAbs which inhibited DARC binding retained GIA against
P. knowlesi
expressing a vaccine heterologous strain of PvDBP. However, the six most potent mAbs (from the same epitope community) did not inhibit DARC binding and lost GIA when tested against a heterologous strain of PvDBP.
Conclusion:
We have isolated a large panel of vaccine-induced human anti-PvDBPII mAbs with the aim to characterise the antigenic landscape of the leading blood-stage
P. vivax
vaccine candidate. We used HT-SPR to sort mAbs into epitope communities with similar binding and functional characteristics. We have identified potent parasite growth inhibitory mAbs from different epitope communities, however the most potent mAbs are strain-specific.
T2-P1 Pv3Rs: an R package to estimate the probability that a
Plasmodium vivax
recurrence is a recrudescence, relapse or reinfection
Aimee R. Taylor
1
, Yong See Foo
2
, Michael T White
1
1
Department of Global Health, Institut Pasteur, Université Paris Cité, Paris, France;
2
School of Mathematics and Statistics, University of Melbourne, Parkville, Victoria, Australia
Correspondence:
Aimee R. Taylor (ataylor@pasteur.fr)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P1
Background:
A
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) recurrence can be caused by a failure to treat a preceding blood-stage infection (recrudescence), by the activation of hypnozoites (relapse), or by a new mosquito inoculation (reinfection). Accurate estimates of the cause of recurrence are important in clinical trials; they can lead to improved evaluation of the efficacy of radical cure, and improved monitoring of drug resistance. To estimate the probability that a
P. vivax
recurrence is a relapse, recrudescence or reinfection we have developed an R package, Pv3Rs [
https://github.com/aimeertaylor/Pv3Rs
], based on a statistical genetic model.
Materials and methods:
The model builds on a previously presented Bayesian model [1]. For each study participant one-by-one, it leverages the relatedness between parasites in recurrent and past episodes to infer the cause of
P. vivax
recurrence. Different causes are characterised by different patterns of genetic relatedness because the relationships between parasites in recurrent and past episodes differ with different causes. The model assumes that recrudescing parasites are clones of parasites in the preceding blood-stage infection; that relapsing parasites are delayed draws from one or more previous inoculations and are thus strangers, siblings and/or clones of parasites in previous blood-stage infections; and that reinfecting parasites are strangers.
Results:
The updated model is faster and more powerful. It scales linearly with the number of markers, generating probability estimates from genetic data on the many markers typical of amplicon sequencing data. It can directly estimate recurrent state probabilities for participants who experience more than two recurrences. In general, the estimates generated by the new model are comparable to those generated under the original model.
Conclusion:
We have built a user-friendly tool for
P. vivax
recurrent state genetic inference applicable to a variety of types of genetic data. We will describe the updates to the underlying model, demonstrate how to use the software, outline current limitations and describe future developments.
P. vivax
recurrent states are not always identifiable using genetic data alone because clones are compatible with both recrudescence and relapse while strangers are compatible with both reinfection and relapse. As such, we recommend plugging estimates based on time-to-event data (or other complementary information) into the prior of Pv3Rs’ statistical genetic model.
Acknowledgements:
Pv3Rs’ development is funded by the European Union (project number 101110393). Views and opinions expressed are however those of the author only and do not necessarily reflect those of the European Union or European Research Executive Agency (REA). Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.
References:
Taylor AR, Watson JA, Chu CS, Puaprasert K, Duanguppama J, Day NPJ, et al. Resolving the cause of recurrent
Plasmodium vivax
malaria probabilistically. Nat Commun. 2019;10(1):5

Malaria Journal
2025,

T2-P2
Background:
Effective control of
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) requires targeting the hidden liver-stage reservoir of hypnozoites.
P. vivax
serological testing and treatment (PvSeroTAT) [1] represents a novel intervention targeting hypnozoite carriers with radical cure. The forthcoming
Plasmodium vivax
Serological Testing and Treatment in Ethiopia and Madagascar (PvSTATEM) cluster-randomised trial, due to start in March 2025, seeks to evaluate the impact of PvSeroTAT with primaquine. In each site, 36 villages will undergo initial screening: 12 of these will be randomised to intervention (two rounds of PvSeroTAT, 6 months apart) and 12 to standard of care. The primary endpoint is mean cluster PCR-prevalence 6 months after the second PvSeroTAT round.
Materials and methods:
We use an existing individual-based model of
P. vivax
transmission [2, 3] to simulate the PvSTATEM study. We evaluate the primary endpoint on simulated data by calculating the odds ratio (OR) of PCR-prevalence in the intervention arm versus the control arm. This modelling framework allows us to (1) characterise factors influencing the impact of PvSeroTAT, and (2) optimise the design of the PvSTATEM trial.
To address the first aim, we simulate the trial protocol in a range of verisimilar transmission settings (varying e.g.
P. vivax
transmission intensity and seasonality, cluster size, intervention coverage and timing, primaquine dosage and adherence). To investigate the second aim, we simulate each of the villages screened for participation in the PvSTATEM trial by calibrating the transmission model to data from census and baseline observational surveys (completed in October 2024).
Results:
Simulations do not differ significantly when primaquine dosage is of 3.5 mg/kg (default modelling assumption) or 7 mg/kg. We estimate that the proposed cluster-randomised trial is likely to yield a significant result across a broad range of transmission settings, with particular sensitivity to cluster size, intervention coverage and
P. vivax
transmission intensity. Running the entire trial in villages of 500 people, with 0.5 infectious bites per person-year prior to the intervention, assuming an intervention coverage of 70%, we expect a significant trial outcome with an estimated OR of 0.56 (95% credible interval [CrI]: 0.42–0.71) and a p-value of 0.001 (95% CrI: 0.000–0.011). However, the significance of the trial outcome may be compromised should villages be smaller, transmission intensity lower and intervention coverage lower.
Conclusion:
Modelling results have so far informed the choice of the primaquine dosage of 3.5 mg/kg for the intervention arm. To maximise the chances of observing a significant trial outcome, the PvSTATEM study team will select which of the screened clusters to retain in the trial based on the size, transmission intensity and willingness to participate of each cluster. Estimates of the number of necessary G6PD tests and drug courses will allow the PvSTATEM study team to prepare sufficient consumables.
References:
Longley RJ, White MT, Takashima E, Brewster J, Morita M, Harbers M, et al. Development and validation of serological markers for detecting recent
Plasmodium vivax
infection. Nat Med. 2020;26(5):741–9.
White MT, Walker P, Karl S, Hetzel MW, Freeman T, Waltmann A, et al. Mathematical modelling of the impact of expanding levels of malaria control interventions on
Plasmodium vivax.

Plasmodium vivax
control and elimination in Brazil: A modelling study. von Seidlein L, editor. PLOS Med. 2021;18(4): e1003535.

Malaria Journal
2025,

T2-P3
Background:
The emergence of
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) resistance to the first-line treatment, chloroquine (CQ), threatens the success of malaria control and elimination programs around the world. Without robust molecular markers, it remains difficult to identify cases of chloroquine resistance and monitor its spread. In areas where a high prevalence of chloroquine resistance is present, such as in Papua New Guinea, national malaria control programs have changed their first-line treatment to ACTs. Until now the impact of this important and necessary shift to ACT treatments on CQ resistance has yet to be explored.
Materials and methods:
In this study, 63 non-
P. falciparum
mono-infection patient isolates diagnosed by RDT were collected on Day 0 (40 isolates in 2023, 23 isolates in 2024) to evaluate the current levels of CQ resistance of
P. vivax
parasites circulating in Papua New Guinea. An ex vivo drug susceptibility assay was used to define the CQ susceptibility phenotype of each isolate and isolates were preserved for in-depth omics characterization.
Results:
Of the 63 non-
P. falciparum
isolates collected, 41 isolates were identified as
P. vivax
mono-infections by qPCR, 4 isolates were determined to be mixed infections (3 isolates
P. vivax/P. malariae,
1 isolate
P. vivax/P. malariae/P. falciparum
), and 2 isolates were
P. falciparum
mono-infections. No
P. ovale
was detected by qPCR and the other 16 isolates were found to be negative for all
Plasmodium
species tested. The ex vivo drug susceptibility assay shows that CQ resistance is still present in Papua New Guinea, despite the switch to an ACT for first-line treatment in 2011.
Conclusion:
In conclusion,
P. vivax
CQ resistance is still present in Papua New Guinea despite changing the first-line treatment for
P. vivax
to artemether-lumefantrine in 2011. Our findings suggest that
P. vivax
has an inherently different mechanism for CQ resistance as compared to
P. falciparum
. Our study underlies the importance of continued monitoring of drug susceptibility, even after changes in treatment, as this data could have further implications for choosing partner drugs in ACT treatments.
T2-P4 Severe Hemolysis on the First Dose of an 8-Week Primaquine Regimen for Radical Cure of

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P4
Background:
Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency increases the risk of primaquine-induced hemolysis in
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) patients. While the WHO recommends weekly primaquine (0.75 mg/kg) for G6PD deficient individuals, data on its safety are limited. We report a case of severe hemolysis during weekly primaquine administration in a G6PD deficient child.
Case report:
During a feasibility study of G6PD testing and different primaquine regimen in Hanura Primary Health Center, Lampung province, Indonesia, September 2023, patients with fever or history of fever were screened for malaria (microscopy) and their G6PD activity level recorded with the Standard semi-quantitative G6PD test (SD Biosensor, Korea). A 5-year-old girl (36.9 °C), with
P. vivax
infection (asexual parasitaemia of 48/µL blood), had G6PD deficiency (activity 1.9 U/g Hb) and a hemoglobin concentration of 9.8 g/dL. She weighed 17 kg and was given 1 tablet of dihydroartemisinin-piperaquine daily for 3 days and prescribed weekly 15 mg tablet of primaquine (equivalent to 0.88 mg/kg/dose, the closest to the recommended dose) for 8 weeks. Since the clinic staff had no prior experience of treating patients with G6PD deficiency, the decision was made to admit the patient for 1 week at the Center’s inpatient facility for clinical and laboratory observation.
Conclusion:
Despite adherence to the WHO treatment guidelines, the patient developed severe hemolysis after the first primaquine dose. Her hemoglobin decreased to 8.3 g/dL on day 2 and 7.8 g/dL on day 3. On day 5 her hemoglobin had fallen to 5 g/dL, at which point the patient was referred to the hospital. Despite a decline of 4.8 g/dl, the patient was still active, with pallor and conjunctival anemia not evident until day 5. Her urine Hillmen score rose from 3 to 5, but this did not fully reflect the severity of hemolytic episode. Following two blood transfusions (200 cc on day 5 and 150 cc on day 7) the patient’s Hb increased to 11.7 g/dL, after which the patient was discharged. The patient completed the 8-week regimen. Genotyping later confirmed that the patient was homozygous for G6PD Chatham variant.
Acknowledgements:
This case report is part of the UNITAID-funded SCOPE study (Short Course PrimaquinE for the radical cure of P. vivax), conducted in partnership with Medicines for Malaria Venture (MMV). We thank the collaborating institutions and the Hanura field team for their contributions.
Informed Consent:
We have obtained informed consent from the patient’s parents before we did further analysis for this case report.
T2-P5 Mesenchymal stem cells (MSCs) attenuate cerebral malaria induced neurodegeneration in
Plasmodium berghei
ANKA infected C57BL/6 murine models
Indu Sharma
1,2
, Jyoti Das
1,2
1
Academy of Scientific and Innovative Research (AcSIR), Noida, India;
2
Division of Immunology, National Institute of Malaria Research, Dwarka, New Delhi, India
Correspondence:
Jyoti Das (drjyoti203@gmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P5
Background:
Cerebral malaria (CM) is a severe neurological complications of
Plasmodium
infection, resulting in 15–20% mortality due to multiorgan failure [1]. Antimalarial drugs effectively eliminate parasites, but they have minimal impact on neurological symptoms, thus neurological sequelae are observed in 25% of survivors [2]. Resistance against commonly used drugs is also being reported making the treatment quite challenging. Mesenchymal stem cells (MSCs) are often used as cell-based therapy in treating infectious and neurodegenerative disorders due to their immunomodulatory and regenerative properties, which include the secretion of growth factors [3].
Materials and methods:
Female C57Bl/6 mice were infected with
Plasmodium berghei
ANKA (Pb. ANKA) infected RBCs which were further divided into two groups, one which received no MSCs and the other which were infused with MSCs intravenously. Parasitemia was monitored regularly and at specific time points animals were euthanized, serum was collected and cells were isolated from spleen and brain for molecular and flow cytometric analysis. All the results were drawn in comparison to the control group which received no infection.
Results:
MSC infusion reduced parasitemia (Fig. 1.A), increased survival and decreased hemozoin accumulation in spleen as well as brain parenchyma. MSC-infused mice also showed reduced parasite-mediated brain encephalopathy and neurodegeneration, with fewer neurons having condensed nuclei and bright eosinophilic cytoplasm along with overlaying vacuolation compared to the infected group. The therapeutic mechanism of MSC may also be attributed to reduced inflammation as it downregulates pro-inflammatory cytokines (IL-6, IL-1β, TNF-α, IFN-γ), decreases the expression of CXCL10 and attenuate endothelial dysfunction by downregulating the expression of ICAM, VCAM and iNOS in brain vasculature. MSCs improved brain pathology by protecting the Blood Brain Barrier, resulting in lesser Evans blue infiltrating brain parenchyma of MSC-infused mice (Fig. 1.D). Also, MSCs were able to reduce the pro-inflammatory cytokines in peripheral circulation thus protecting the mice from disease pathology.
Conclusion:
In general, our findings offer insight into how the infusion of MSCs reduces the severity of experimental cerebral malaria by modulating the excessive pro-inflammatory response and protecting the animals from neuropathology of the disease. Thus, MSCs could potentially be used as an adjunct therapy for addressing the neurological complications of CM.
Fig. 1
Effect of MSCs on Cerebral Malaria pathology. MSC infusion reduce parasitaemia (A) and Splenomegaly (B, C) and brain encephalopathy and maintains blood brain barrier in
Pb
ANKA infected mice (D, E)

Birbeck GL, Molyneux ME, Kaplan PW, et al. Blantyre Malaria Project Epilepsy Study (BMPES) of neurological outcomes in retinopathy-positive paediatric cerebral malaria survivors: a prospective cohort study. Lancet Neurol. 2010;9(12):1173–1181

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P6
Background:
Vivax
malaria continues to exert large health and financial burden on individuals, families and health systems; understanding the associated financial costs, treatment-seeking behaviour and productivity losses is crucial for optimising effective control and elimination strategies. Despite subsidised malaria care in Indonesia and other malaria endemic countries, households still bear substantial out-of-pocket expenses when seeking care. Households have shown to have similar financial burden for both falciparum and vivax malaria previously. Our study quantified the household economic burden of vivax malaria in Indonesia.
Materials and methods:
Household costs of illness were estimated using data from an implementation study in Indonesia, using purposive sampling from three sites in Papua (a high burden setting) and two in Sumatra (where
vivax
malaria is seasonal). Direct costs included medical fees, travel, and food. Productivity losses were also calculated from time unable to perform usual activities due to illness or caregiving and from patients and carers monthly income and spending. Sensitivity analyses were conducted to assess variations in productivity loss estimation methods. Catastrophic health expenditure among the study populations was identified by patients with direct medical expenditure which exceeded predefined thresholds of 10 and 25% of monthly income.
Results:
A total of 130 patients with confirmed vivax malaria were sampled from the five study sites (60 in Sumatra, 70 in Papua). Patients required a mean 7 days away from usual activities, with caregivers needing 1–2 days off usual activities, depending on the setting. The mean total costs for all cases were US$31 (SD = 55), with productivity losses accounting for 78% of total costs (US$24). Direct medical costs mostly consisted of patients visiting other health clinics prior to attending the health clinic where vivax malaria was treated. In total 86 (66.2%) patients with malaria experienced direct health costs exceeding 10% of their monthly income, and 65 (50%) had health spending > 25% of their monthly income, indicative of catastrophic health expenditure.
Conclusion:
Our study provides important insights into the economic burden of
Plasmodium vivax
infections and highlights the substantial direct costs and productivity losses for patients. Household out-of-pocket costs were driven by productivity losses, emphasising the need for targeted interventions to alleviate the financial strain of affected households. A high number of households suffered catastrophic health expenditure. Ensuring that malaria patients have easy and affordable access to effective antimalarial treatment is critical as endemic countries move towards elimination. Eliminating malaria in these settings will benefit households financially and reduce productivity losses.
Acknowledgement:
This work was conducted on behalf of the Short Course Primaquine for the radical cure of P. vivax (SCOPE Study) and funded by UNITAID. We acknowledge Medicines for Malaria Venture (MMV) as a partner and thank all collaborating institutions and field teams across Indonesia for their essential support and contributions.
Trial registration:
This study was registered on clinicaltrials.gov:
NCT05879224
.
T2-P7 Investigating the epigenetic landscape of severe malaria: a genome-wide methylation analysis of
Plasmodium vivax
clinical isolates
Priyanka Roy
1
, Arnav Bharti
2
, Tanmaya Mahapatra
2
, Sanjay Kumar Kochar
3
, Dhanpat Kumar Kochar
3
, Ashis Das
1
1
Molecular Parasitology and Systems Biology Lab, Department of Biological Sciences, Birla Institute of Technology and Science (BITS), Pilani campus, Rajasthan, India;
2
Department of Computer Science and Information Systems, Birla Institute of Technology and Science (BITS), Pilani campus, Rajasthan, India;
3
Department of Medicine, S.P. Medical College, Bikaner, Rajasthan, India
Correspondence:
Ashis Das (adas@pilani.bits-pilani.ac.in)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P7
Background:
Globally, malaria remains one of the most widespread infectious diseases. According to the World Malaria Report 2023 [1], half of the world’s population is at risk of malaria caused by
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
). The parasite's complex life cycle requires tight regulation of gene expression, which is controlled at both transcriptional and post-transcriptional levels [2]. Further, as a survival strategy, in response to the variable conditions encountered in host environments, DNA base modifications may play a pivotal role in regulating gene expression during the different parasite stages [3]. Epigenetic modulation links to almost every critical biological process and may impact mechanisms of parasite’s drug resistance.
Materials and methods:
This study employs Oxford Nanopore DNA Sequencing to identify the epigenetic signatures i.e. 5-Methylcytosine-5mC, 5-hydroxymethylcytosine-5hmC (Fig. 1) by using computational tools. To gain more insights into these methylation signatures, we also investigated the differential methylation regions (DMRs) in promoter, gene body and enhancer at a single nucleotide level resolution in clinical samples of
P. vivax
showing both uncomplicated and complicated manifestation. Additionally, gene set enrichment analysis was performed on regions significantly enriched for DMRs, and these findings were correlated with RNA-sequencing datasets from same samples which have been experimentally validated in our lab.
Results:
We generated the first genome-wide DNA methylation maps (5 mC & 5 hmC) at single CpG resolution in clinical isolates of
Plasmodium vivax
between uncomplicated and complicated cases, covering all three asexual parasite stages. Our findings reveal that the epigenetic landscape is significantly altered in severe disease states, with most changes occurring in regulatory regions such as promoters and enhancers. Notably, we identified differentially methylated regions (DMRs) in severe malaria cases and compared these with transcriptome expression profiles, highlighting dysregulation in key pathways related to immune evasion, pathogenesis, and drug resistance. Additionally, gene ontology analysis of these genes revealed enrichment in various biological pathways, including the regulation of gene expression, DNA-templated transcription, RNA biosynthesis, and cellular metabolic processes. Taken together, our findings establish a clear link between DNA methylation alterations and gene expression dysregulation. This suggests that such epigenetic changes may facilitate parasite survival under the hostile environmental conditions of severe disease.
Conclusion:
Substantial genomic alternations in DNA methylation are observed in clinical samples with different disease states from malaria. Changes in gene expression correlates to differential methylation, suggesting a functional role for DNA methylation in transcriptional regulation during severe disease states. Strikingly, the differential methylation status of certain key genes has shown significant epigenetic changes involved in immune evasion and pathogenesis processes. DMRs containing drug resistance marker genes may affect efficacy of drug treatment of parasites. Multiple genes and pathways that are dysregulated have been identified and offer future potential targets for biomarker development, novel therapies, and potent leads for future studies.
Fig. 1 DNA methylation (5mC/5hmC) on gene promoters/enhancers can cause transcriptional repression/activation

T2-P8 Preliminary clinical performance and usability data from Brazil on a new point-of-care test for glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency
Rebecca K. Green
1
, Michelle Oliveira Silva
2
, Marcelo Brito
3
, Laila R. A. Barbosa
3
, Rosiane Machado
2
, Mikka Nyarko
1
, Sampa Pal
1
, Emily Gerth-Guyette
1
, Stephanie Zobrist
1
, Gonzalo J. Domingo
1
, Marcus V. G. Lacerda
3
, Dhélio Batista Pereira
2
1
PATH, Seattle, Washington, United States;
2
Centro de Pesquisa em Medicina Tropical de Rondônia, Porto Velho, Rondônia, Brazil;
3
Fundação de Medicina Tropical Doutor Heitor Vieira Dourado, Manaus, Amazonas, Brazil
Correspondence:
Rebecca K. Green (rgreen@path.org)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P8
Background:
Current treatment guidelines for radical cure of
Plasmodium vivax
malaria recommend the use of 8-aminoquinolines, which can result in potentially life-threatening complications for people deficient in the glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) enzyme [1]. Accordingly, testing for G6PD deficiency is recommended prior to administration of such drugs [1].
The Wondfo G6PD/Hb Test (Guangzhou Wondfo Biotech Co., Ltd., China) is a new quantitative point-of-care (POC) G6PD test that can help decentralize testing and expand safe access to treatment methods. The test uses an optical signal to detect G6PD activity and hemoglobin concentration in human capillary and venous whole blood. The specimen is added to the prepared test cuvette containing lyophilized reagent and buffer, then G6PD catalyzes the enzymatic reaction. The instrument measures fluorescence and absorbency to calculate G6PD activity normalized to hemoglobin concentration. Fig. 1 shows a prepared test cuvette being inserted into the analyzer for testing.
This study aims to evaluate the performance of the Wondfo G6PD/Hb Test in a clinical setting and assess usability among intended users.
Fig. 1
Inserting a prepared test cuvette into the Wondfo G6PD/Hb Analyzer to measure G6PD activity
Methods:
A prospective diagnostic accuracy study is ongoing in Porto Velho, Rondônia and Manaus, Amazonas, Brazil. This research is approved by WCG IRB (Washington, USA; Ref. 20231229) and the National Commission for Ethics in Research (Brasilia, Brazil; Ref. 6.627.319). Participants are recruited at clinics and through an enriched sample of individuals with known G6PD status. At the point of care, the Wondfo test, HemoCue 201+, and standard of care malaria microscopy are conducted on fresh capillary blood. Venous blood is collected and transferred to a laboratory, where the Wondfo and HemoCue tests are repeated, alongside reference G6PD (spectrophotometer) and hemoglobin (complete blood count) testing. A usability study is also being conducted with intended end-users of the POC G6PD test. Participants receive training on the test prior to the observed completion of the test procedure followed by label comprehension and results interpretation questionnaires.
Results:
As of December 31st, 2024, 1,282 participants (45.9% female) have been enrolled in Brazil. In Manaus, 674 participants (44.4% female), have been enrolled including 82 malaria positive, 37 deficient (< 30% G6PD activity) and 23 intermediate (30–70% G6PD activity, females only) per reference testing. In Porto Velho, 608 participants (47.5% female) have been enrolled, including 60 malaria positive, 20 deficient and 11 intermediate per reference testing. Thirty participants from Porto Velho and Manaus (average age 44.3 years; N = 11 female) have completed the usability evaluation. Most participants (N = 28, 93%) had previous experience with G6PD testing (primarily the STANDARD G6PD Test), and 55% rated the test procedure as somewhat or very easy to complete. Test completion time ranged from 6–15 min (median time 8.5 min), and 216/235 (92%) example results were interpreted correctly (with intermediate results the most frequently misinterpreted).
Conclusions:
Recruitment will conclude in 2025. Once complete, data from Brazil will be analyzed to determine if the performance is sufficiently accurate and reliable to inform clinical decision-making and the test is appropriate for use by frontline health workers. This data will provide evidence of clinical performance and usability for a promising new POC G6PD test to support its regulatory approval, ensuring a stable supply of affordable, accessible, and accurate POC G6PD tests.
References
:
WHO guidelines for malaria, 16 October 2023. Geneva: World Health Organization; 2023. Report No.: WHO/UCN/GMP/2023.01 Rev.1. License: CC BY-NC-SA 3.0 IGO.
T2-P9 DNA-Dependent protease promotes parasite survival during replication stress induced through DNA damaging agents
Risha Shameem
, Abhisheka Bansal
School of Life Sciences, JNU, New Delhi, India
Correspondence:
Abhisheka Bansal (abhisheka@jnu.ac.in)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P9
Background:
Proteases in malaria parasite are critical for asexual replication within the red blood cells as they are involved in egress, invasion, and haemoglobin degradation.
Pf
WLM is a novel protease belonging to the WLM family. Yeast Wss1 and Human SPRTN are the DNA-dependent proteases which are involved in DNA protein crosslink repair. Here we have functionally characterized
Pf
WLM in
Plasmodium falciparum
. Recent reports of emerging resistance from South-East Asia against artemisinin combination therapy, the front-line anti-malarial regimen is of grave concern. Novel essential targets are required to be identified and validated for drug development against malaria. Combination therapies targeting WLM could enhance drug development in the context of emerging drug resistance.
Materials and methods:
Generation of
Pf
WLM conditionally down-regulated Transgenic parasite through CRISPR cas9. The GlmS ribozyme system was used to conditionally knock down the parasite. Western blot and IFA were used for stage-specific expression. Parasite growth assay in the absence and presence of DNA damaging agents was performed. Purification of recombinant
Pf
WLM and its DNA binding assay were carried out by affinity chromatography.
Results:
Pf
WLM is a DNA-dependent protease as it undergoes auto cleavage in the presence of DNA. It binds to DNA validated through EMSA.
Pf
WLM is expressed in asexual stages with maximum expression in the trophozoite stage. Conditional knockdown of
Pf
WLM does not affect the proliferation of the mutant parasite however, interestingly, when the parasite without
Pf
WLM is subjected to hydroxyurea and formaldehyde, a drastic decrease in viability was seen compared to the wild-type parasite.
Conclusion:
Pf
WLM may be used as a valuable target for drug development to control malaria parasite growth within the red blood cells. Combination treatments focused on WLM have the potential to advance drug development in response to the challenge of emerging drug resistance.

Malaria Journal
2025,
