T2-P10
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) is the predominant cause of malaria across the Greater Mekong Sub region challenging malaria elimination [1].
P. vivax
recurrences can arise from reactivation of the parasite’s dormant liver stage (hypnozoites) weeks to months after an initial infection. The only drugs that kill hypnozoites can cause severe haemolysis in Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficient individuals [2]. The STANDARD G6PD test (Biosensor) is a new point-of-care diagnostic to identify patients at risk [3].
Materials and methods:
To explore the countrywide implementation of the Biosensor in Cambodia, a mixed methods study was conducted in three high-burden provinces. Quantitative data were gathered from routine surveillance records (January 2021–March 2023). Interviews and focus group discussions were conducted among 142 participants, followed by thematic analysis. Ethical approval was obtained as part of the EFFORT clinical trial (
NCT04411836
) from the Cambodian National Ethics Board (# 118) and the Institutional Review Board of the Menzies School of Health Research (HREC 2020-3694).
Results:
G6PD testing at health centres was well adopted with 93.9% (790/841)
P. vivax
patients presenting at the health centre receiving G6PD testing. The main challenge to implementing G6PD testing was referral to a health centre following malaria diagnosis by community health workers (CHW); only 49.2% (437/888) of patients reached health centres for G6PD testing. Influencing factors included road conditions and long distances to health centres compounded by the cost of seeking further care and patients’ perceptions of vivax malaria and its treatment. The use of the Biosensor by CHWs could overcome these challenges by bringing testing into the community. Health centre workers’ and CHWs’ confidence in CHWs conducting the test could be built; however, there was more hesitancy among policymakers for whom more confidence building was required given concerns about CHWs’ capacity and risk of haemolysis associated with treatment in the case of misdiagnosis.
Conclusion:
Our findings suggest that bringing treatment closer to patients, through CHWs using the Biosensor, would improve access to radical cure for vivax malaria, but the successful implementation of the device at community level will require confidence-building and strategies to address stakeholders’ concerns.

T2-P11 Exploring mechanistic insight of newly synthesized nano-formulated chalcones for antimalarial activity
Shweta Sinha
1
, Rakesh Sehgal
1,2
, Uma Nahar Saikia
3
1
Department of Medical Parasitology, Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh, India;
2
Department of Microbiology, Aarupadai Veedu Medical College & Hospital, Puducherry, India;
3
Department of Histopathology, Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh, India
Correspondence:
Rakesh Sehgal (sehgalpgi@gmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P11
Background:
Malaria caused by
Plasmodium
species, is arguably one of the main medical concerns worldwide. In present situations artemisinin combination therapy is the most recommended treatment for
falciparum
malaria in most part of the world. However, rapid extension of drug-resistant parasite has increased the need for development of new drugs, formulations, more combinations, and vaccines. In this context, chalcones with potent antimalarial activity [1], has been nano-formulated to increase their efficacy by employing targeted-based approach and further these nano formulations were explored for their antimalarial mechanism.
Materials and methods:
This involves synthesis and characterization of nano-formulated chalcones. The chalcones derivatives by Claisen–Schmidt condensation, was encapsulated using nano formulation techniques, i.e. nanoparticles and these nano formulations was further characterized with different analytical techniques [2]. For the in vivo investigation, the most potent synthesised Nano conjugates that passed the in vitro antimalarial drug sensitivity test were selected. Peters’ 4-day suppressive test against
P. berghei
infection was used to measure antimalarial activity. For detail features, additional criteria like the survival assay, morphological and histopathological changes, and resetting frequency were performed. Nitric oxide, reduced glutathione, and lipid peroxidation were assessed in the serum and liver or spleen tissue homogenates in order to investigate the mechanism of action of oxidative stress in each group of animals. For added clarity, RT-qPCR was used to measure the expression of the cytochrome c oxidase gene in each treatment group. ANOVA and post-hoc tests were used to examine the data. Significant values were defined as p < 0.05.
Results:
The synthesized nano formulated chalcones were nearly spherical, well dispersed and stable particles which shows its biocompatibility. All treatment groups showed a significant decrease in percentage suppression as compared to the infected control groups. The CQ-treated and nano formulated chalcones groups showed a significant (p < 0.05) decrease in rosette frequency. When presenting to each of the designated groups, the specific histological characteristics of the liver and spleen sections will be covered. Increased oxidative biochemistry and gene expression levels has validated the involvement of oxidative pathways in the present study. The generated reactive oxygen species causes intracellular stress responses and oxidative damage to various cellular organelle present inside the parasites such as mitochondria, electron transport chain (affects ATP production) etc., that finally responsible for the distortion, and apoptosis of parasites.
Conclusion:
Conjugation of drugs, (in this case chalcones), onto metal nanoparticles protect them against the body’s immune system, thereby extending their blood circulation time. Therefore, in the present study the developed metal-based nanoparticles functionalized with antimalarial chalcones has meet the biomedical applications by proving the conditions like stability and tendency. Additionally, these nano formulated chalcones provides a basis for designing more such conjugates that may enhance the antimalarial activity. The difference in the mode of action of the nanoparticles and chalcones additionally make it more potent therapeutic lead, that would help in delaying the development of resistance in malaria parasite.

We are thankful to Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR), New Delhi (Ref No. NAN-BMS-45/25/2020), for providing financial support in form of Research associate fellowship to Dr. Shweta Sinha.

Sinha S, Kaur A, Sehgal R. Synthesis and characterization of silver nanoparticle conjugated-chalcones and their evaluation for antimalarial, cytotoxicity and haemolytic potential at in vitro level. Discov Appl Sci. 2024; 6:273.
T2-P12 Implementing G6PD testing for
Plasmodium vivax
malaria in Punjab, North India: a pilot study
Rahi U

, Mewara A
1
, Kaura T
1
, Singh N

, Grover G S

2

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P12
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) is an important public health concern and remains a challenge for malaria elimination in Punjab. One of the major concerns is the ability of
P. vivax
to form dormant liver stages (hypnozoites) that can cause a relapse months or years after the primary infection. For the radical cure of liver stages of
P. vivax
, two 8-aminoquinolone compounds, primaquine and tafenoquine can be used by virtue of their hypnozoitocidal activity. However, primaquine may cause acute hemolytic anaemia in G6PD deficient individuals. In the state of Punjab, majority of malaria cases are due to
P. vivax
and as G6PD testing is vital for managing
P. vivax
malaria.
Materials and methods:
A pilot study was conducted to assess the reliability and ease of implementation of G6PD screening in the district hospitals of Punjab, in field settings as well as in malaria lab units. The ease of usage amongst the end-user laboratory technicians was also assessed by a post-usage survey to assess the operational feasibility of introducing the POCTs kits (BinaxNOW® G6PD; STANDARD™ G6PD test) in the state labs.
Results:
A total of 80 patients diagnosed with
P. vivax
malaria were screened for G6PDd, of which 62 were tested by both conventional meth haemoglobin reduction test (MRT) and commercial rapid diagnostic point-of-care test (POCT) kit for qualitative analysis of G6PD deficiency (BinaxNOW® G6PD); two of which were found to be G6PD deficient by both the tests. Further, for quantitative analysis of G6PDd, STANDARD™ G6PD test has been introduced in parallel with BinaxNOW® G6PD and tested on 18 patients in an ongoing evaluation, of which none were found G6PD deficient yet. Amongst the laboratory technicians, there was wide acceptability of the G6PD testing, and all found the POCT RDT kits easy to use.
Conclusion:
These results demonstrate intervention feasibility of POCT for G6PD deficiency as these tests can be performed at the bed side of the patients, with minimum infrastructure facility in rural areas as well as field settings before initiating the radical treatment with primaquine.
T2-P13 Survey of Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase deficiency, anaemia and malaria parasitaemia among febrile patients in Indonesia

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P13
Background:
Primaquine is the only available drug to eradicate the liver stage of
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) in Indonesia, but its widespread use is hindered by the risk of severe drug-induced haemolysis in patients with glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency. Measuring G6PD activity and haemoglobin (Hb) concentration before primaquine treatment can inform case management and reduce the risk of adverse patient outcomes. This study aimed to determine how G6PD activity, anaemia and malaria parasitaemia varied with age and sex in different endemic settings in Indonesia.
Materials and methods:
We conducted cross-sectional surveys of febrile participants presenting to six health facilities in three Indonesian provinces: Central Papua (high malaria endemicity) and North and South Sumatera (low to moderate seasonal malaria endemicity respectively). Each health centre enrolled 200 consecutive febrile patients. Participants were tested for malaria using microscopic examination or malaria rapid diagnostic test (RDT). G6PD and Hb concentration were assessed using the G6PD Standard (SD Biosensor, ROK) semi quantitative point-of-care G6PD assay. 100% G6PD activity was defined as the adjusted male median. G6PD activity was categorised as normal (> 70% activity), intermediate (30–70%), or deficient (< 30%). Hb was categorised as normal (> 10 g/dL), anaemic (7–10 g/dL), or severe anaemia (< 7 g/dL). Hb concentrations between sites were compared using the Kruskal Wallis test with Mann Whitney U post hoc tests and the prevalence of malaria using chi-squared tests.
Results:
Malaria prevalence was 47.5% (380/800) in Papua, 34.0% (68/200) in North Sumatera, and 15.0% (30/200) in South Sumatera. In Papua 42.9% (163/380) cases were due to
P. vivax
compared to 100.0% (68/68) in North Sumatera and 96.7% (29/30) in South Sumatera. Males were at higher risk of malaria (46.8%, 293/626) compared to females (32.2%, 185/574); p < 0.001, OR = 1.85 [95%CI 1.46–2.34] and greatest in young adults aged 15–24 years old (54.2%, 109/201). Overall, 9.0% (108/1200) of patients had intermediate activity and 1.8% (21/1200) were G6PD deficient, with the highest prevalence of G6PD deficiency found in South Sumatera (4.0%, 8/200). Anaemia prevalence was 14.6% (175/1200), with 20% (25/175) being severe. Papua’s patients had lower median Hb (12.0 g/dL; Range: 4.1–23.4) than in North Sumatera (13.4 g/dL; Range: 6.7–18.6; p < 0.001) and South Sumatera (12.8 g/dL; Range: 6.4–18.4; p < 0.001). Females with malaria had a greater risk of anaemia (24.3%, 45/185) compared to females without malaria (13.1%, 51/389), p = 0.001; OR = 2.13 [95%CI 1.36–3.33].
Conclusion:
This cross-sectional survey across six primary health centres in three malaria endemic provinces in Indonesia highlights the continued importance of malaria diagnosis in febrile patients. Measurement of G6PD activity and baseline Hb concentration is feasible in primary health care settings, which may help to minimize the risk of haemolysis following primaquine radical cure.
Acknowledgements:
This work was conducted on behalf of the Short Course Primaquine for the radical cure of P. vivax (SCOPE Study) and funded by UNITAID. We acknowledge Medicines for Malaria Venture (MMV) as a partner and thank all collaborating institutions and field teams across Indonesia for their essential support and contributions.
Trial registration:
This study was registered on clinicaltrials.gov:
NCT05879224
.
T2-P14 Designing chimeric Circumsporozoite Protein (CSP) construct: a promising vaccine candidate against
Plasmodium vivax
and
Plasmodium falciparum
Kumar J.
1,2
, Kumar V.
1,2
, Chakraborti S.
1,3
, Pandey K.
1,2
1
ICMR-National Institute of Malaria Research, New Delhi, 110077;
2
Academy of Scientific and Innovative Research (AcSIR), UP, 201002;
3
Birla Institute of Technology & Science, Pilani, Hyderabad Campus, Telangana, 500078
Corresponding:
Pandey K. (pandey.kailash70@gmail.com); Chakraborti S. (soumyabiochem@gmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P14
Introduction:
Plasmodium vivax
is a major contributor to global malaria cases, posing unique challenges, such as relapse due to hypnozoites and increasing drug resistance. Although artemisinin-based combination therapies (ACTs) are used to treat
P. vivax
malaria, the lack of an effective vaccine remains a significant hurdle. This study focuses on developing a chimeric Circumsporozoite Protein (CSP) construct to advance vaccine research targeting multi-
Plasmodium
species.
Materials and methods:
A chimeric CSP construct was engineered using immunogenic sequences to enhance antigenicity and structural stability. The designed construct consists of 324 amino acids, with a molecular weight of 34.9 kDa and a theoretical isoelectric point (pI) of 9.28. Structural modelling and refinement were performed using I-TASSER and Galaxy web server (C-score = − 2.83, TM-score = 0.39 ± 0.13, RMSD = 13.1 ± 4.1 Å). Linear antibody epitopes were predicted using ElliPro. Immune simulations using C-ImmSim predicted strong immune responses, identifying epitopes interacting with MHC class I and II alleles. Cytokine analysis revealed IL-2 and other interleukins indicating effective immune activation. The construct was expressed in a heterologous system, and its expression was confirmed via western blotting with anti-His antibodies under pre- and post-refolding conditions. Additionally, indirect ELISA was performed using sera from individuals infected with
P. falciparum
(Pf) and
P. vivax
(Pv) to assess cross-reactivity.
Results:
The CSP chimeric construct demonstrated no similarity to human proteins, mitigating potential risks of autoimmunity. Structural analyses revealed significant antigenic regions suitable for eliciting robust immune responses. C-ImmSim simulations confirmed a strong immunogenic profile, with epitopes recognized across diverse HLA alleles and cytokine production consistent with effective immune activation. Western blot analysis confirmed successful protein expression at 37 °C, with stronger recognition post-refolding, indicating proper folding and structural integrity. Indirect ELISA showed that the chimeric construct elicited a response against both
Pf
and
Pv
sera, demonstrating its cross-reactive potential across
Plasmodium
species.
Conclusion:
The CSP chimeric construct showed promise as a vaccine candidate, addressing the challenges of
P. vivax
while exhibiting cross-reactivity with
P. falciparum
, enhancing its utility in regions co-endemic for both species. The absence of similarity to human proteins reduces the risk of adverse immune reactions. The successful expression, refolding, and serological reactivity highlight the construct's suitability for further immunogenicity studies. Future in vivo experiments are needed to validate its protective efficacy and potential to mitigate the burden of malaria caused by
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum.
T2-P15 Antimalarial delivery with ferritin-based protein cage: A step towards developing smart therapeutics against malaria
Chiging Tupe

, Shruti Bhatt

, Subrata Dasgupta

, Kailash C. Pandey

Correspondence:
Kailash C. Pandey (pandey.kailash70@gmail.com); Soumyananda Chakraborti (soumyabiochem@gmail.com, soumyananda.chakraborti@hyderabad.bits-pilani.ac.in)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P15
Background:
Malaria remains a significant global health challenge, with high mortality rates, particularly in Sub-Saharan Africa and Southeast Asia, exacerbated by drug resistance (1). This study explores the potential of ferritin, a protein cage known for its biocompatibility and drug delivery capabilities (2,3), as a novel vehicle for antimalarial therapeutics. The primary aim is to investigate the binding interactions between various antimalarial compounds and horse spleen ferritin, assessing their loading efficiency and therapeutic efficacy against
Plasmodium
. This research seeks to enhance drug bioavailability and targeting, paving the way for innovative malaria treatments.
Materials and methods:
The study employed a combination of experimental and computational methods to investigate the binding and efficacy of antimalarial drugs encapsulated in horse spleen ferritin. Initially, twelve antimalarial compounds were screened for their binding affinity to ferritin using fluorescence quenching techniques. Computational docking studies were conducted to identify binding sites and assess the stability of the drug-ferritin complexes through molecular dynamics simulations. The encapsulation efficiency was evaluated using anion exchanged chromatography, and transmission electron microscopy (TEM) was utilized to visualize the structural changes in ferritin upon drug loading. The antiparasitic efficacy of the chloroquine-loaded ferritin nanoconjugate was tested against Plasmodium falciparum in vitro, measuring the half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) to determine the effectiveness of the drug delivery system. This multifaceted approach provided insights into the binding interactions, structural integrity, and therapeutic potential of ferritin as a drug delivery vehicle for antimalarials.
Results:
The study successfully encapsulated twelve antimalarial compounds within horse spleen ferritin, identifying six that effectively bound to the protein cage, with chloroquine and sulfadoxine analogues showing a strong preference for ferritin (Fig. 1). Binding interactions were validated through experimental fluorescence quenching and computational docking, revealing minimal structural changes in ferritin upon drug binding. Molecular dynamics simulations confirmed the stability of the drugs at the binding sites. The efficacy of the chloroquine-loaded ferritin nanoconjugate was tested on Plasmodium falciparum in vitro, demonstrating significant antiparasitic activity with an IC50 of approximately 42.5 nM which was comparable to IC50 of chloroquine i.e., 35 nM (Fig. 2). The results indicate that ferritin-based carriers enhance the bioavailability and targeting capabilities of antimalarials, supporting their potential as effective drug delivery systems in malaria treatment.
Fig. 1
Stern–Volmer analysis and log(
F
0–
F
)/
F
versus log[
Q
] plots (inset) describing the quenching mechanism, binding constant and number of binding sites within HsFtn for six antimalarials including a) amodiaquine, b) mefloquine, c) primaquine, d) quinine, e) sulfadoxine and f) chloroquine, at three different temperatures (298 K, 303 K and 313 K)
Fig. 2
Antimalarial encapsulation efficiency of ferritin determined by anion exchange chromatography (AEC); chromatogram clearly shows encapsulation of a) chloroquine and b) amodiaquine by HsFtn. Absorbance at 280 nm was used to monitor antimalarial encapsulation events. Transmission electron micrographs also confirm antimalarial encapsulation as c) apo-ferritin (HsFtn) appears dark under negative staining in TEM in contrast d) antimalarial loaded ferritin shows whitish appearance of cavity due to dye exclusion. e) Anti-parasitic effect of antimalarial (CQ) conjugated HsFtn in comparison with apo-HsFtn. (f) Microscopic view of the antimalarial activity observed by the chloroquine ferritin cage conjugate (50 nM), lower concentration of same combination showed presence of parasites. Negative control (NC) without any drug and HsFtn (Buffer) showed normal growth of parasites, whereas Chloroquine alone used as a Positive control (PC) displayed inhibition of parasites
Conclusions:
The study concludes that ferritin-based protein cages are promising vehicles for antimalarial drug delivery, demonstrating effective encapsulation and release of antimalarials like CLO. The ferritin-drug conjugate showed significant efficacy against Plasmodium falciparum, with an IC50 of approximately 42.5 nM. While the results indicate comparable antiparasitic effects to individual drugs, variations in IC50 values suggest the need for further research to optimize drug delivery systems and understand the mechanisms of parasite inhibition. Overall, this approach could enhance therapeutic outcomes in malaria treatment, paving the way for advanced smart therapeutics.

Background:

To the best of our knowledge, only a few investigations have previously been carried out using pharmacological assays to endorse the flora of India. Therefore, the screening of these plants is urgently needed. Various ethnobotanical surveys have already been conducted in southern parts of India.

T2-P17 The impact of recurrent vivax malaria and primaquine on haematological recovery
Megha Rajasekhar, Katelyn Brown
Centre for Epidemiology and Biostatistics, Melbourne School of Population and Global Health, The University of Melbourne, Melbourne, Victoria, Australia
Correspondence:
Megha Rajasekhar (megha.rajasekhar@unimelb.edu.au)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P17
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) malaria causes recurrent episodes of malaria with associated parasite induced haemolysis. Primaquine remains the mainstay of treatment to prevent relapses but can result in drug induced haemolysis. The longer-term haematological risks and benefits of primaquine have not been characterised. We aimed to investigate the relationship between vivax recurrences, the risk of anaemia and the impact of primaquine on haemoglobin changes over 12 months after treatment.
Materials and methods:
A systematic review identified clinical efficacy studies published between January 2000 and July 2024 of patients with uncomplicated
P. vivax
that included a primaquine treatment arm with at least 90 days follow-up. Individual patient data from patients with G6PD ≥ 30% were pooled using standardised methodology. Negative binomial regression models were used to estimate the rate of anaemia (haemoglobin < 11 g/dL) after treatment with or without primaquine by total mg/kg dose (low dose 2 to < 5 mg/kg and high dose ≥ 5 mg/kg). Cox regression was used to assess the association between primaquine use and dose and rate of first anaemia episode. Linear mixed effects models were used to model haemoglobin changes over time and in response to recurrences. Models were adjusted for age, sex, baseline parasite density, schizontocidal drug used and baseline haemoglobin, with random effects by study site.
Results:
Data were available from 8 studies, with 4417 patients: 1083 (24.5%) treated without primaquine, 608 (13.8%) following low total dose primaquine and 2726 (61.7%) following high total dose primaquine. Mean haemoglobin concentrations were 0.4 g/dL [95%CI 0.3–0.5] higher at day 90 in patients treated with primaquine compared with those treated without primaquine, with the difference falling to 0.2 g/dL [0.1–0.3] at day 180. Compared with patients not treated with primaquine, those treated with low dose primaquine had a similar annual rate of anaemia (adjusted rate ratio: 0.94 [95%CI 0.72–1.21]), whereas patients treated with high dose primaquine had a significantly lower rate of anaemia (ARR:0.65 [0.53–0.79]). By Cox regression, the rate of first anaemia episodes was also lower after high dose primaquine compared to no primaquine (Adjusted hazard ratio: 0.39 [95%CI 0.30–0.51]) but not after low dose primaquine compared to no primaquine (AHR:0.83 [0.49–1.40]). There were fewer recurrences after primaquine treatment and patients with ≥ 2 recurrences had a lower mean haemoglobin on day 90 (0.8 g/dL lower [1.1–0.5]) compared to those with no recurrences, with similar patterns observed on day 180 for symptomatic recurrences.
Conclusion:
Haemoglobin concentrations are lower in patients who have multiple recurrent episodes of malaria. In patients with G6PD activity ≥ 30%, primaquine reduces the number of recurrences and is associated with higher haemoglobin concentrations compared to those not treated with primaquine, however the benefit was only observed in patients treated with a high total dose.
Acknowledgements:
This work was conducted as part of, and is presented on behalf of the WorldWide Antimalarial Resistance Network (WWARN) Vivax Haematology Study Group.
T2-P18 Correlation of the platelet indices with severity and outcome in cases of
vivax
malaria
Kochar S. K., Garhwal A.
1
, Meena B. L.
Sardar Patel Medical College, Bikaner
Correspondence:
Kochar S. K. (drskkochar@gmail.com, drskkochar @rediffmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P18
Background:
Identifying the expected progression of malaria to severe malaria is an important tool for referring the patient to higher center equipped with the treatment of severe malaria. Platelet indices can be detected in the complete blood count analyzer (CBC) available at most of the primary health care (PHC) facilities and if there is any co-relation of platelet indices with severity of malaria, patient may be referred to higher center well in time. This study investigates platelet indices in patients with acute malaria caused by
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
), assessing their association with warning signs of severe and complicated malaria.
Materials and methods:
We included 103 patients of vivax malaria treated in the Department of Medicine, Sardar Patel Medical College, Bikaner during 2023–24. After confirming the diagnosis of
P. vivax
infection and excluding mixed infection with
P. falciparum
, we evaluated CBC and platelet indices. Disease severity was assessed as per WHO criteria for severe malaria, based on classic signs of severe malaria, including severe anemia, bilirubin levels, aminotransferases, and creatinine at admission. Patients with prolonged symptoms or identified as having a primary infection were considered at higher risk for severe malaria progression.
Results:
Among the 103 patients, 30 (29.12%) had severe malaria and 73 (70.87%) had non-severe malaria, with a predominance of males (70.08%). The majority (61.17%) were aged 21–49 years. Significant correlations were found between severe and non-severe malaria regarding mean platelet volume (MPV) (p > 0.05) and platelet distribution width (PDW) (p > 0.002). Multi-organ dysfunction syndrome (MODS) was observed in 33.33% of severe cases. No significant correlations were identified with other severe symptoms such as jaundice (80%), anemia (40%), or cerebral malaria (10%). Patients with symptoms lasting over 3 days, indicative of warning signs for severe disease, exhibited the highest MPV and PDW. Adjusted analyses indicated that these warning signs were independently associated with elevated MPV and PDW.
Conclusion:
Platelet indices, particularly MPV and PDW, may serve as potential markers for assessing the severity of
P. vivax
malaria. Future studies employing robust methodologies for prognostic evaluation are warranted to validate the clinical utility of these indices.
T2-P19 Transmission-blocking antibodies and plant extracts: novel interventions against
Plasmodium
development in mosquito vectors
Bharti Goyal
1,2
, Priyanka Chaudhary
1
, Kailash Pandey
1,2
1
Parasite-Host Biology Group, ICMR National Institute of Malaria Research, New Delhi, India;
2
Academy of Scientific and Innovative Research (AcSIR), Ghaziabad, UP, India
Correspondence:
Kailash Pandey (kailash.pandey@icmr.gov.in)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T2-P18
Background:
Malaria transmission depends on the successful development of
Plasmodium
parasites within the mosquito vector, specifically during their sexual and sporogonic stages. The sexual stage involves gametocytes that are ingested by mosquitoes during a blood meal, while the sporogonic stage includes processes such as exflagellation, ookinete formation, and sporozoite development. These stages are critical for parasite survival and transmission to the human host. Interruption of these processes is a vital strategy to curb malaria transmission. This study investigates innovative approaches to disrupt
Plasmodium
development in mosquitoes, focusing on the use of specific antibodies and natural plant extracts with transmission-blocking potential.
Materials and methods:
The study explored a dual strategy to block
Plasmodium
development in mosquitoes. Antibodies were generated against key surface proteins of
Plasmodium
gametocytes, ookinetes, and sporozoites. These antibodies were evaluated for their efficacy in blocking transmission to Anopheles mosquitoes using field strains of
Plasmodium falciparum
. Simultaneously, two natural extracts from medicinal plants, recognized for their antiplasmodial properties, were tested for their ability to interfere with parasite transmission in anopheles.
Results:
Preliminary findings indicated that four of the antibodies significantly suppressed parasite development in the mosquito midgut. The plant extracts demonstrated potent transmission-blocking activity by inhibiting exflagellation, ookinete and oocyst formation.
Conclusion:
Our study highlights the potential of combining immunological and natural strategies to disrupt
Plasmodium
development within mosquito vectors, effectively blocking malaria transmission. Antibodies targeting parasite surface proteins and natural extracts with antiplasmodial properties demonstrated significant transmission-blocking activity. This approach represents a promising avenue for integrated interventions in malaria control. However, further refinement is needed to improve efficacy, scalability, and field applicability. Future research should focus on optimizing dosages, delivery methods, and sustainability to facilitate implementation in malaria-endemic regions, ultimately contributing to global efforts in malaria eradication.
Theme 3: Targeting vivax: decoding host-parasite interactions
T3-I1 Whole genome analysis of
Plasmodium vivax
Indian field isolates
Suman Tamang
1,2
, Vineeta Singh
1,2
1
ICMR-National Institute of Malaria Research (NIMR), Delhi, India;
2

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-I1
Whole genome sequencing (WGS) technologies help in tracking disease outbursts and drug resistance spread throughout the world. To understand the unique biology of
Plasmodium vivax,
the genome analysis of the most widespread species of
Plasmodium
by using WGS technologies is proving reliable to large extent. Though there are advancements with WGS tools in genome mapping of
Plasmodium
species, yet; there is lack of WGS data from India. With this basis WGS analysis was carried out on five
P.vivax
field isolates to understand the diverse and existing genomic makeup of
P.vivax
Indian isolates. Blood samples were collected from malaria-positive patients and confirmed with PCR assay for monoinfections. The samples were prepared and Illumina sequencing platform was used to perform WGS. The data were analysed for sample coverage, depth and mutations present in clinical samples. The cleaned paired-end reads were aligned to the
P. vivax
reference genome (
PvP01 v1
) using BWA-MEM.
The whole genome sequencing of samples generated paired-end reads between 71.30–50.73 million. The coverage of these samples ranged from 90–99% for the four samples. Total combined variants, SNPs and indels were found to be 97989, 63427, 35882 respectively. Analysing the data of the four samples by Circos plots revealed variant density across 14 chromosomes with more variants towards the telomeric ends (Fig. 1). The WGS analysis demonstrated the existence of heterogeneous
P vivax
populations.
This study found greater genetic diversity in Indian
P. vivax
field isolates which will enable a deeper understanding of
vivax
genetic makeup and also will serve as reference strains to further studies in clinical samples.

SNP density plot (circos-galaxy)

T3-I2 extracellular vesicles as intercellular communicators in
Plasmodium vivax
intra-splenic infections
Alberto Ayllon-Hermida
1,2
, Hernando A. del Portillo
1,2,3
, Carmen Fernandez-Becerra
1,2,4
1
Barcelona Institute for Global Health (ISGlobal), Barcelona, Spain;
2
Institut d’Investigació Germans Trias i Pujol, Institut, Badalona, Spain;
3
Catalan Institution for Research and Advanced Studies (ICREA);
4
CIBERINFEC, ISCIII-CIBER de Enfermedades Infecciosas, Instituto de Salud Carlos III, Madrid, Spain
Correspondence:
Hernando A. del Portillo (hernandoa.delportillo@isglobal.org); Carmen Fernandez-Becerra (carmen.fernandez@isglobal.org)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-I2
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
is increasingly recognized as a cause of asymptomatic chronic infection, with up to 90% of cases detectable only by PCR. Notably, the spleen has recently been identified as a cryptic erythrocytic reservoir during natural infections, harbouring a substantial parasite biomass [1]. Furthermore, specific parasite genes appear to be regulated in a spleen-dependent manner [2], and circulating extracellular vesicles (EVs) from infected individuals contain parasite proteins that promote cytoadherence of
P. vivax
-infected reticulocytes to human spleen fibroblasts (hSFs) [3], suggesting a mechanism for parasite persistence in splenic tissue. This study aims to functionally characterize spleen-dependent genes of
P. vivax
and elucidate the contribution of EVs to the molecular mechanisms underlying parasite–spleen interactions.
Materials and methods:
Due to the inability to culture
P. vivax
in vitro, we employed CRISPR-Cas9 gene editing to knock-in spleen-dependent
P. vivax
genes into
P. falciparum
. A stable clonal transgenic line expressing a hypothetical spleen-dependent gene was generated and validated by genomic PCR, RT-PCR, western blotting, and confocal microscopy to confirm expression and membrane localization. EVs were isolated from plasma of
P. vivax
-infected patients using size exclusion chromatography or direct immunocapture. Their effects on parasite adhesion to hSFs were assessed in vitro. To investigate host cell responses, single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) of EV-stimulated hSFs was conducted to resolve cell-type-specific transcriptional changes and identify upregulated adhesion molecules. Selected adhesins were further validated by RT-qPCR.
Results:
We successfully generated a
P. falciparum
3D7 transgenic line expressing a hypothetical
P. vivax
spleen-dependent gene, now termed
P. vivax
Spleen-Dependent Protein 1 (PvSDP1), with confirmed surface localization. Functional adhesion assays demonstrated that only EVs derived from
P. vivax
-infected patients (PvEVs) enhanced binding of the PvSDP1-transgenic line to hSFs; this effect was partially inhibited by antibodies targeting PvSDP1 [4]. Importantly, scRNA-seq analysis of PvEV-stimulated hSFs revealed the upregulation of specific host adhesin genes, highlighting a potential role of EVs in splenic niche formation. Expression of several of these adhesins, including CD36, ICAM1, FN1, AMIGO2, CD44, and THBS1, was independently validated by RT-qPCR.
Conclusions:
Collectively, our findings suggest that spleen-regulated parasite genes such as PvSDP1 may act as ligands for host adhesins expressed by spleen fibroblasts (Fig. 1). Moreover, they offer new insights into how
P. vivax
leverages circulating EVs to communicate with the human spleen, promoting the formation of cryptic intrasplenic infections, a major obstacle to malaria elimination.

T3-O1 SLC4A1/Band3 is a strain-transcendent invasion host factor for
Plasmodium vivax
Usheer Kanjee
1
, Sheena Dass
1
, Christof Grüring

, Mudit Chaand

, Manish Kumar

, Joao Paulo

, Jon Goldberg

, Angela Mascarenhas

, Ligia Pereira

, Mahmoud Mikdar

, Merryn Fraser

, Rimi Chakrabart

, Edwin Gomes
3
, John Doench
5
, Pradipsinh K Rathod
4
, Manoj T. Duraisingh
1
1
Department of Immunology and Infectious Diseases, Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health, Boston, MA, USA;
2
Department of Cell Biology, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA;
3
Goa Medical College and Hospital, Bambolim, Goa, India;
4
Departments of Chemistry and Global Health, University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA;
5
Genetic Perturbation Platform, Broad Institute, Cambridge, MA, USA.
&
Swiss Tropical and Public Health Institute, Basel, Switzerland
Correspondence:
Manoj T. Duraisingh (mduraisi@hsph.harvard.edu)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-O1
Background:
During the blood-stage infection,
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) merozoites selectively invade reticulocytes, the youngest red blood cells (RBCs), via parasite-produced invasion ligand proteins binding RBC host receptors. Only two ligand/receptor interactions have been genetically validated: PvDBP/DARC [1] and PvRBP2b/TfR1 [2]. Identification of other essential interactions is critical to prioritize blood-stage vaccine targets. SLC4A1/Band3 has been implicated as a candidate host receptor through: (i) anti-SLC4A1 antibody inhibition studies [3]; (ii) reduced
P. vivax
invasion in RBCs carrying the Southeast Asian Ovalocytosis polymorphism in SLC4A1. Here we use RBC genetics and
P. vivax ex-vivo
short-term culture to investigate the functional role of SLC4A1 during parasite invasion.
Materials and methods:
In order to determine the functional role of SLC4A1 in
P. vivax
invasion, we generated RNAi knockdowns (KD) and CRISPR/Cas9 knockouts (KO) of SLC4A1 in either primary CD34+-derived hematopoietic stem cells (cRBCs) or immortalized erythroid cells (EJ-RBCs). The effect of the SLC4A1 KD/KO on surface membrane proteins was characterized both via flow cytometry and tandem mass spectrometry-based surface proteomics. SLC4A1 KD/KO cell size was measured via a MoxiGo coulter counter, and osmotic stability was measured using an established flow-cytometry based assay. Functional studies to measure
P. vivax
invasion into reticulocytes or SLC4A1 KD/KO cells, with or without addition of host-targeted antibodies, were performed using short-term cultured Indian
P. vivax
clinical isolates and enumerated via slide microscopy.
Results:
Deletion of SLC4A1 resulted in ~ 30% reduced cell volume and reduced osmotic stability compared to EJ-WT-RBCs, but < twofold differences in surface membrane protein levels. In functional assays, we observed > 80% reduction in
P. vivax
invasion in EJ-∆SLC4A1-RBCs, suggesting a critical role for SLC4A1. Next, we generated SLC4A1-KDs using three shRNAs (B3-KD1, B3-KD2, B3-KD3) where we observed a 60–75% reduction in SLC4A1 levels without significantly affecting cell volume, osmotic stability, or levels of surface membrane proteins. We observed between 50% (B3-KD1)—80% (B3-KD3)
P. vivax
invasion inhibition. In a biochemical approach, we observed up to 70% invasion inhibition with an anti-SLC4A1 antibody. To investigate the role of SLC4A1 with other receptors during invasion, we performed synthetic lethal studies combining B3-KD1 cells with host-targeted inhibitors (α-DARC/MGSA, α-TfR1/OKT-9, α-CD98/UMF98 and sCR1) at ~ 50% inhibitory dose. We observed strong enhancement of invasion inhibition up to > 80% in all Indian
P. vivax
isolates tested.
Conclusion:
SLC4A1 has previously been implicated as having a role in
P. vivax
invasion. Here we demonstrate, using a combination of genetic and biochemical approaches, that SLC4A1 has a critical, strain-transcendent role in
P. vivax
invasion. Future studies will aim to identify and validate the invasion ligand binding SLC4A1. Our observation of additive inhibition effects via targeting two host receptors may provide an attractive approach for blood stage vaccine development efforts. In the future, we plan to identify combinations of anti-invasion ligand antibodies leading to potent inhibition of
P. vivax
invasion.
References:
Adams JH, Hudson DE, Torii M, Ward GE, Wellems TE, Aikawa M, Miller LH: The Duffy receptor family of
Plasmodium knowlesi
is located within the micronemes of invasive malaria merozoites. Cell

Malaria Journal

T3-O2
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
)
,
was long considered to be a benign parasite [1]. However, reports over the last two decades has shown the presence of disease severity due to mono-infections by the parasite in the absence of any other infectious or disease causes, from different geographical locations [2, 3]. We report here a part of global transcriptomic studies comparing the parasite transcriptome causing uncomplicated disease with severe
Plasmodium vivax
manifestations, namely hepatic dysfunction and cerebral malaria focusing on the
vir
genes, some of which have been postulated to be involved in cytoadherence.
Materials and methods:
Venous blood samples were collected from
P. vivax
infected patients with informed consent at S.P. Medical College, Bikaner, India, as per hospital guidelines. Microscopy, rapid diagnostic tests and PCR for malaria parasites were performed to confirm infection only with
P. vivax.
Blood samples from 23 patients with malaria caused by
P. vivax
were obtained
.
The patients were diagnosed as either complicated (n = 17: 12 hepatic dysfunction and 5 cerebral malaria) or uncomplicated malaria (n = 6). All patients with severe malaria underwent tests to rule out viral hepatitis, dengue, typhoid, leptospirosis, infectious mononucleosis, and HIV. Total RNA isolated from histopaque separated erythrocytes was used for hybridization on custom microarrays and the data analyzed. Results for some genes were validated using real time quantitative PCR. As part of current studies DNA methylation analysis of the parasite genome (Nanopore sequencing) is being analyzed with respect to the upregulated
vir
genes in hepatic dysfunction cases.
Results:
The log2 fold change value of +1 was considered as upregulated and -1 was considered as downregulated.
pir
genes showing a 50% and above, upregulation or downregulation in patient samples have been considered. Among upregulated
pir
genes in severe malaria samples, 7
pir
genes belonging to the
vir
subfamily C, G and E;
pvpir
subfamily H and unclustered are common among cerebral malaria and hepatic dysfunction cases. In cerebral malaria 17
pir
genes belonging to the
vir
subfamily B, C, and E;
pvpir
subfamily A and H; not clustered and unclassified are unique. In hepatic dysfunction 21
pir
genes belonging to the
vir
subfamily B, C, E, G, and I;
pvpir
subfamily A and H; not clustered are unique. Real time qPCR experimentation confirms upregulation of two genes (PVX_086865 in HDYS & PVX_097530 in CM) and downregulation of two genes (PVX_017645 in HDYS & PVX_104180 in CM) from array data.
Conclusion:
The difference in the transcriptome profile of
pir
genes in
P. vivax
cerebral malaria and hepatic dysfunction cases is shown. Upregulation of these
vir/pvpir
genes might be playing a crucial role in pathogenesis. One of the possible roles of
vir
genes is that they could be exhibiting cytoadherence with host cell receptors as in reported studies, The single
vir
gene documented to have shown binding in the reported study, (PVX-108770, vir_C,) has shown 50% and above upregulation in the patient samples with hepatic dysfunction in our data. Attempts to correlate methylation status of genic regions for regulatory understanding is ongoing.

We thank all the patients for their voluntary consent. We also acknowledge BITS Pilani, Pilani campus for providing the required infrastructural facilities during this study, and SP Medical College for the ethical approval to collect samples (No.F. (Acad)SPMC/2003/2395) and permission to use these samples through IERB approval No. F29(Acad)SPMC/2020/3151. Project grant no. BT/PR7520/BRB/10/481/2006.

to investigate the role of

Sophia M. DonVito

Malaria Journal
2025,

T3-O3
Background:
Selection and binding of suitable host red blood cells by
Plasmodium
merozoites is essential for erythrocytic replication and is mediated by several protein families shared across
Plasmodium
species. These include the reticulocyte binding-like (RBL)/RBL-homologue (Rh) and Duffy/erythrocyte binding-like (DBL/EBL) proteins. Understanding their role in invasion can highlight determinants of infection risk and disease severity within individuals and populations—such as preferences for specific cell subsets, blood types, or the risk of zoonotic transmission—as well as informing rational vaccine design. However, the lack of continuous and genetically modifiable culture system has hindered the study of these proteins in
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
).
Materials and methods:
The closely related zoonotic parasite,
P. knowlesi
, is a particularly useful model for studying
P. vivax
, as it can be readily cultured in human erythrocytes and is highly amenable to CRISPR/Cas9 genome editing for orthologue replacement of functionally similar proteins. Here, we use a
P. knowlesi
transgenic line expressing
P. vivax
DBP to investigate how human Duffy antigen receptor variation affects parasite binding to red blood cells. Additionally, we have developed a model whereby
P. knowlesi
can be conditionally induced to rely upon PvRBP2b, a
P. vivax
RBL, for erythrocytic invasion. PvRBP2b has been shown to bind the reticulocyte marker CD71/TfR1 and is hypothesised to mediate reticulocyte restriction. This system now allows PvRBP2b to be studied in its native, full-length form and for its role during invasion to be assessed in live culture.
Results:
Using peptides representative of the major human DARC alleles found globally, both unmodified
P. knowlesi
and
P. vivax
DBP orthologue replacement lines demonstrated preferential binding to one allele, with a significantly stronger effect observed in
P. knowlesi
, providing insight and informing optimised DBP-based vaccine design for both species. Building on this, our recent generation of a novel PvRBP2b P. knowlesi line now permits study of invasion proteins which may not fully complement a
P. knowlesi
orthologue—for example, if complementation can only occur under specific conditions. Unlike in short-term
P. vivax
culture, this model allows RBLs of interest to be studied individually, as well as to be tagged and modified. With this, we can investigate the role of PvRBP2b in greater detail, including during invasion into reticulocytes versus normocytes. We can also now use parasite-derived full-length RBP2b for characterisation, such as in the validation of human monoclonal antibodies.
Conclusion:
Orthologue replacement of
P. knowlesi
invasion ligands with proteins from closely related non-Laveranian parasites, including
P. vivax
, continues to be a powerful tool, especially while most
Plasmodium
species are unable to be maintained in culture or genetically modified. These methods improve understanding of protein function during invasion and help to identify and improve vaccine antigens and validate inhibitory antibodies. The addition of PvRBP2b to this repertoire is a key step in understanding its role in
P. vivax
invasion biology, especially reticulocyte restriction.

Malaria Journal
2025,

T3-O4
Background:
Despite the existence of treatment and cure, malaria remains a public health problem, causing 249 million cases and 608 thousand deaths in 2022, 94% occurring in African, attributed to
Plasmodium falciparum
. However, in the Americas,
Plasmodium vivax
represents 72% of cases, with more than 80% occurring in Brazil [1]. Due to vector resistance to pyrethroid insecticides, strategies to block
Plasmodium
transmission (TB) in the vector are being developed. In this study, atovaquone (ATQ) in a treated sugar solution was evaluated for TB of
P. vivax
in
Anopheles darlingi
, the main Amazonian vector [2], using a direct membrane feeding assay (DMFA).
Materials and methods:
The treated sugar feeding assay consisted of a pre-infection stage involving the preparation of the solution and its offering to
An. darlingi
females, followed by DMFA. The treated sugar solution was prepared by diluting ATQ in a 10% sucrose solution, resulting in a final concentration of 100 μM [3]. This solution was applied to cotton and offered for 24 h [3] to 150 females, while the control group received only 10% sucrose. After 24 h, feeding is suspended for 12 h, and then the membrane feeding test is performed using blood from a patient with
P. vivax
. On the 7th and 14th days after infection, midgut and salivary gland dissections were performed to verify and count oocysts and sporozoites, respectively. The intensity of infection was determined by the Mann–Whitney test, and the prevalence of infection and engorgement was determined by the chi-square test. Blood samples were collected with the patient's consent and ethical authorization.
Results:
The ATQ offered to females obtained a significant difference in the prevalence of infection (control = 96.49%; ATQ 25.86%, p < 0.0001) and eliminated oocysts in the midguts (medians of the intensity of infection: Control = 31 oocysts/mosquito; ATQ = 0 oocysts/mosquito). The prevalence of sporozoites was also affected by ATQ (control = 93.7%; ATQ = 30.43%, p < 0.0001), as well as the intensity of sporozoites (medians: Control = 8960 sporozoites/mosquito; ATQ = 0 sporozoites/mosquito, p < 0.0001). Atovaquone did not affect the survival of females in the 14 days of follow-up (Control = 72%, ATQ = 73.1%, p = 0.8546). However, engorgement with treatment 24 h before infection reached a significant difference in relation to the control (Control = 64.44%, ATQ = 43.78%, p < 0.0001).
Conclusion:
Our results confirm the potential of ATQ for TB of
P. vivax
in
An. darlingi
, providing positive evidence of the prospect of TB of the parasite in the vector, and may encourage the search for compounds that have structural and chemical similarities to be also evaluated.
Acknowledgements
:
We thank all volunteers from Porto Velho-RO, Brazil, who provided blood samples for DMFA assays. This work was supported by the Brazilian Ministry of Health/DECIT/CNPq N° 23/2019 (grant number 442653/2019-0) and the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation (INV-003970).

WHO. WHO Guidelines for Malaria—14 March 2023. Geneva; 2023.

Anopheles
of the subgenus
Nyssorhynchus
. Rev Inst Med Trop Sao Paulo. 2000; 42:87–94.

Anopheles
and
Plasmodium
resistance to malaria control strategies. Plos Pathogens. 2022; 18: e1010609–e1010609.

Background:
Research on invasion mechanisms of
P. cynomolgi
remains limited, however, functional protein characterization provides an alternative approach. Similarities between
P. cynomolgi
and
P. vivax
make it a valuable model for
P. vivax
studies. Blocking the PcyDBP-DARC interaction inhibits invasion, suggesting PcyDBP has a critical role. This study aims to identify its receptor, elucidate the invasion mechanism, and predict key residues involved in host-cell binding, advancing our understanding of
P. cynomolgi
as a model for
P. vivax
.
Materials and methods:
We expressed recombinant proteins from
P. vivax
,
P. knowlesi
, and
P. cynomolgi
ligands, along with human and monkey DARC receptors, using mammalian cells and purified by His-tag and Fc-fusion-tag. Interaction assays were performed, including ELISA, immunoprecipitation, and biolayer interferometry. Species-specific DBP-RII sequences were analyzed for tertiary structures and amino-acid residue variations.
Results:
Non-
Laverania Plasmodium
species including
P. vivax, P. knowlesi
, and
P. cynomolgi
contained 12 conserved cysteine residues in DARC interaction domain
.
DBP ligands and DARC receptors successfully expressed by HEK293-6E cells. ELISA test confirms PvDBP-RII interacts with DARC. Co-IP results revealed PvDBP-RII interact with human DARC. PkDBPα-RII and PcyDBP1-RII binds to both receptors. PcyDBP2-RII didn’t bind to any DARC receptors. Biolayer Interferometry analysis revealed PvDBP-RII binds most strongly to human DARC (K
D
= 8.2 ± 0.54 nM). PcyDBP1-RII binds slightly weaker 9.8 ± 0.65 nM with human DARC, while PkDBPα-RII has lowest binding-affinity with human receptor K
D
= 25 ± 4.6 nM. The interaction between monkey DARC and PcyDBP1-RII shows highest affinity (K
D
= 3.32 ± 0.94 nM). In contrast,
P. vivax
(Kd = 18.69 ± 1.09 nM) and PkDBPα-RII (K
D
= 22.89 ± 2.04 nM) shows lower binding- affinity. Structural analysis identified key-residues in DARC at position D24 aspartic acid interacts with Y363F and K367S on PvDBP-RII, shows specific binding-interface. In PkDBPα-RII, Y363 and K367 are replaced by F363 and S367, indicates amino acid variations could impact binding- specificity. Interaction between
R274
(DBP-RII) and
E23
(DARC) conserved across all DBP-RII, signifying an essential role in binding stability.
P. vivax
DBP showed higher binding-affinity to human DARC, however,
P. cynomolgi
DBP1 prefers to interact with monkey DARC. Interestingly,
P. knowlesi
show distinct interaction properties and no preference for host DARC type.
Conclusion:
The DBP-DARC interactions have known to be critical factor for invasion of
P. vivax, P
.
knowlesi
, and
P. cynomolgi
. The Co-IP results showed that PvDBP-RII specifically interact with human DARC, not monkey DARC, and PcyDBP1-RII strongly bound to human DARC, while PkDBPα-RII interacts with both receptors. 3D structural analysis revealed mutations at Y363F and K367S in PkDBPα-RII affect affinity, highlighting species-specific adaptations.
D24
on DARC is a key residue for PvDBP-RII binding and could influence complex stability. The conserved
R274-E23
interaction serves as an anchoring mechanism that maintains DBP-RII-DARC binding across species. These results indicate specific DBP amino acid residues influence binding-affinity, host-selection and interaction-preference. Based on this result, we highlight several important residues in DBP roles could design target vaccines against
P. vivax
and related species.

Malaria Journal
2025,

T3-P1
Background:
Select species of
Plasmodium
can undergo developmental dormancy after infecting hepatocytes (hypnozoite formation), hindering radical cure of relapsing malaria. The mechanisms of hypnozoite dormancy and reactivation remain a mystery, with both intrinsic (parasite genetics) and extrinsic (host cellular/organismal environment) factors likely to contribute. An understanding of these mechanisms can lead to molecular targets for curative therapy.
Materials and methods:
In this study, we utilized
Plasmodium cynomolgi
infected primary macaque hepatocytes cultures to investigate the developmental dynamics of the parasite. Using a multi-omics approach we explored the transcriptional (single-cell RNA sequencing) and chromatin signatures (bulk ChIP sequencing and ATAC sequencing) of the parasitized hepatocyte cultures.
Results:
Using single cell transcriptomics, we identified a developmentally stalled parasite sub-population with enhanced expression of genes encoding RNA-binding proteins, which was validated by RNA-FISH. These factors could potentially enable hypnozoite dormancy via translational repression of mRNAs involved in parasite maturation. Comparative ChIP-seq and ATAC-seq profiling evidenced heterochromatin-mediated silencing of these genes prior to liver stage schizogony, which could support hypnozoite reactivation. To identify host factors contributing to hypnozoite dormancy, we compared the transcriptional profiles of host hepatocytes harbouring hypnozoites and liver schizonts. Altered expression of genes involved in cytoskeleton organization, metabolism and general/mitochondrial detoxification were prominent differentiating features of hypnozoite-harbouring host cells. Host cell transcriptional heterogeneity in early infection setting was additionally identified to be a determinant of parasite infection preference. Selective host Wnt and TGFβ signalling states were found to be linked with this transcriptional heterogeneity.
Conclusion:
Overall, our study identifies epigenetically-regulated RNA-binding factors as specific markers of hypnozoites, hinting at role of translational repression in hypnozoite dormancy. Furthermore, it provides new insights into host cell signalling pathways and metabolic states possibly influencing parasite development in the liver.
T3-P2 Identifying novel drug targets in Type II Fatty Acid synthesis and Lipid biosynthesis pathway to combat Malaria
Akanksha Singh
1
, Shilpi Garg
1
, Samarth Dwivedi
2
, Atish T Paul
2
, Sanjay K Kochar
3
, Vishal Saxena
1
1
Department of Biological Sciences, Birla Institute of Technology and Science, Pilani, Pilani Campus, Rajasthan, India;
2
Department of Pharmacy, Birla Institute of Technology and Science, Pilani, Pilani Campus, Rajasthan, India;
3
Department of Medicine, Sardar Patel Medical College, P. B. M. and Associated Group of Hospitals, Bikaner, Rajasthan, India

Vishal Saxena (vishalsaxena@pilani.bits-pilani.ac.in)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-P2
Background:
Malaria parasite
Plasmodium
is dependent on the host Fatty Acids and Lipids for its membrane biogenesis and energy acquisition.
Plasmodium
acquires these either by scavenging them directly from the host; and/or by de novo synthesis using apicoplast based Type II FAS pathway [1]. The key players for these mechanisms have not been characterized from
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
). Though
P. vivax
is the most widespread human malaria parasite from decades, it hasn’t been studied in detail due to various reasons. With the antigenic variability shown by each parasite, there is a requirement to understand the parasite biology and identify novel targets.
Materials and methods:
Sequences of proteins functional in the Type II FAS pathway in different
Plasmodium
species, Apicomplexans and prokaryotes were retrieved from the PlasmoDB, ToxoDB and NCBI databases. To characterize the properties and functions of above proteins and two additional proteins, AcpS and LIPIN, functional upstream and downstream of the Type II FAS pathway,
in-silico
analysis was performed using online available tools such as Conserved Domain search (CDD-NCBI), Clustal Omega, Phylogenetic Analysis (Mega), Homology Modeling (using Modeller 9.9 and SWISS-MODEL) and Docking of in-house designed anti-malarials. For homology modeling, the template models were retrieved from PDB database. Docking of in-house library derived compounds was done using Schrodinger Maestro. Further, to understand the expression profile of Type II FAS pathway proteins, we performed real-time expression analysis using mRNA isolated from
P. vivax
infected field isolates and performed a comparative analysis with the transcriptome data available from PlasmoDB.
Results:
FAS related enzyme sequences were analysed and found to be highly conserved between
Plasmodium
species, apicomplexans and prokaryotes. The functional domain analysis of
P. vivax
Type II FAS and lipid biosynthesis pathway enzymes revealed around 60 to 99 percent identity between
P. vivax
and other primate parasite enzymes while around 68 to 88 percent identity between
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
enzymes. Phylogenetic analysis revealed grouping of all
P. vivax
enzymes in primate parasite clade. The transcriptome analysis revealed expression of FAS enzymes during mid to late stages of parasite life cycle. The homology models were energy minimized, and validated. The Overall Quality Factor of ERRAT Score was in the range of 95 to 98 for all the selected enzyme models with 98% of the residues in the most favoured region in Ramachandran Plot. Approximately 550 compounds were docked from in-house library and top 5 compounds with the best docking score were selected for further studies.
Conclusion:
This study concludes that the FAS II pathway and lipid biosynthesis enzymes are conserved across
Plasmodium
species. The functional domains and motifs of all the enzymes are conserved which indicates that the Type II FAS pathway must be similarly operational in all the
Plasmodium
species. Based on the molecular docking studies of in-house prepared inhibitor ligands on various Type II FAS pathway enzymes as well as AcpS and Lipin, inhibitor molecules were identified showing best interactions with the key amino acids in the active sites of the enzymes. These molecules may be tested for parasite inhibition.
Acknowledgements:
1. BITS Pilani for fellowship and infrastructural facilities. 2. Sardar Patel Medical College and P. B. M. Hospital staff for clinical support during sample collection.
References:
Shunmugam S, Arnold C-S, Dass S, Katris NJ, Botte´CY. The flexibility of Apicomplexa parasites in lipid metabolism. PLoS Pathog. 2022; 18:3.

interactions
Christeen Davis

, Renugah Naidu

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-P3
Background:
Parasites of the genus
Plasmodium
are the causative agents of malaria, resulting in around half a million deaths yearly.
Plasmodium
parasites infect erythrocytes during their asexual development. During the intra-erythrocytic phase, parasites remodel the morphological and biophysical characteristics of the host for survival. Monitoring these changes is essential to identify the pathways necessary for development and the underlying biology. Here, we utilized label-free optical diffraction tomography (ODT) and image reconstruction techniques to understand such cell alterations induced by human malaria parasites
P. falciparum
and
P. vivax
within their host erythrocytes such as normocytes and reticulocytes within peripheral and umbilical cord blood.
Materials and methods:
Reticulocytes and normocytes were enriched from the leucodepleted erythrocyte fraction of cord blood and peripheral blood samples using magnet-activated cell sorting (MACS) technique [1]. Parasites were cultured within each cell type by maintaining 2% hematocrit and 1% parasitemia. Parasite cultures were maintained at 37 °C provided with 5% CO
2
.
P. falciparum
(3D7 strain) and
P. vivax
(clinical isolates) were cultured within a complete RPMI-1640 media (supplemented with Glutamax, 30 mM HEPES, 200 µM hypoxanthine, 11 mM glucose, and 20% heat-inactivated human AB serum).
For optical imaging, cells were prepared onto an imaging disc (RI:1.337). Individual RBCs were analyzed using the commercial ODT system (HT-2H, Tomocube Inc., Republic of Korea). 3D RI tomograms were reconstructed from measured multiple 2D images.
Results:
3D RI mapping highlights the biophysical and biochemical aspects of healthy and
Plasmodium
-infected erythroid cells based on differential refractive indices of intracellular contents. ODT data highlighted several differences, such as cell size, cellular volume, hemoglobin (Hb) content between the healthy immature and mature erythrocytes.
P. falciparum
and
P. vivax
infections alter these parameters. Host erythrocyte modification patterns are different between
P. falciparum
and
P. vivax
. The cell size of mature
P. falciparum
schizonts decreased by 20%, whereas that of
P. vivax
enlarged by 10% compared to healthy host cells. Hemoglobin reduction and hemozoin distribution within the infected cells are also influenced by species. At the late-stage schizonts, about 60% hemoglobin reduction is estimated for
P. falciparum
, whereas about 29% is observed in
P. vivax
. Hemozoin content within the
P. falciparum
-infected erythrocytes was compactly accumulated within the cytosol but scattered within
P
.
vivax
-infected cells.
Conclusion:
An in-depth understanding of host cell modifications by parasite activity is important in designing malaria prevention strategies. ODT is an ideal, label-free method to study host-parasite interactions. Using this technique, we have characterized healthy and parasitized erythroid sub-populations in terms of surface area and sphericity index, intracellular hemoglobin, and hemozoin concentration using a label-free manner. Our observations highlight species-specific morphological and molecular alterations within the infected erythrocytes. Findings from our work indicate that
P. falciparum
consumes more haemoglobin within the host cells than
P. vivax
.
P. vivax
parasites possibly evolved to use amino acids from metabolite-rich reticulocytes with reduced dependency on the host hemoglobin degradation. Implications of such host tropism patterns need to be studied further.
References
:
Malleret B, Li A, Zhang R, Tan KSW, Suwanarusk R, Claser C, et al.
Plasmodium vivax
: restricted tropism and rapid remodeling of CD71-positive reticulocytes. Blood. 2015;125(8):1314–24.

Plasmodium
Diksha Sisodia

Malaria Journal
2025,

Background:
Malaria is a severe life-threatening disease caused by the Apicomplexan parasite
Plasmodium
and transmitted by the female
Anopheles
mosquito.
Plasmodium
complete its life cycle in two different hosts; thus, during its development, it encounters different host environmental conditions like changes in pH, salt concentration, different temperature etc. To combat these conditions, the parasite regulates various genes/ proteins necessary for its survival. In this study, we aim to identify protein(s) that play an important role in the survival of parasites and mosquito vectors during cold conditions.
Materials and methods:
Based on literature survey, a homologue of cold-induced/ shock protein was identified from both
Plasmodium falciparum and Plasmodium vivax
. Its functional role was further validated using various in silico tools such as Conserved Domain and targeting analysis and in vitro culture-based studies. Using the transcriptomic approach, we also studied the proteins differentially regulated during cold conditions in the parasite.
Result:
In-silico data analysis suggests the protein is evolutionarily conserved and shares the most conserved RNA binding residues and signature sequences as observed in prokaryotes. From the available target prediction tools, the protein was found to be dual-targeted to both the Apicoplast and Mitochondria. This protein was amplified from the clinical isolates, cloned and expressed in the prokaryotic system, and further, the expression was confirmed using the western blot. We also investigated the effect of the transformed protein on growth of BL21 (DE3)
E. coli
cells transformed with the cloned plasmid and subjecting them to low temperature, where a slight change in growth of cells was observed in case of transformed cells as compared to control. Transcriptomic data suggests this protein is upregulated during the Gametocyte stages.
Conclusion:
Cold shock protein helps the organism to adapt to temperature changes, which is crucial for its survival and transmission. This study highlights the significance of cold shock/ induced proteins in malaria parasite. Understanding these proteins may open up new preventive approaches and vector control strategies for malaria.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-P5
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) is a leading cause of malaria, with rising prevalence despite global control efforts. One reason for this is the absence of an in vitro culture system, which hampers our understanding of the biology of this tiny parasite. Researchers are now focusing on the protein level in order to gain a deeper understanding of merozoite invasion mechanisms. One of the most crucial molecules for merozoite invasion is the Duffy binding protein, which is known as PvDBP. The interaction between the PvDBP and the Duffy antigen receptor for chemokines (DARC), which makes it easier for merozoites to get inside, is very important. This study aimed to identify novel compounds capable of disrupting this interaction to inhibit merozoite invasion.
Materials and methods:
A computer-aided drug discovery approach was employed to screen 580,000 compounds against a pharmacophore model designed to mimic the binding characteristics of PvDBP to DARC. The initial stage of the process identified approximately 6000 potential compounds. A secondary screening step, based on molecular docking scores, further refined the pool to 2000 hits. The interactions between PvDBP and DARC in the presence of these compounds were then quantitatively assessed using an ELISA-based fluorescence polarization assay. This assay measured the extent to which the compounds inhibited the binding of PvDBP to a DARC-derived peptide, and thus identified the top 29 lead compounds. To confirm the efficacy of the identified compounds, three commercially available compounds were tested using a gene-edited
P. knowlesi
line that expressed
P. vivax
DBP.
Results:
The selected compounds (#1074, #852, #1755) showed significant inhibition of PvDBP-DARC interaction in vitro, in a concentration-dependent manner, as evidenced by a reduction in fluorescence polarization. Control experiments confirmed the specificity of these compounds for the DBP-DARC binding site. In the
P. knowlesi
line (PvDBP
OR
), the inhibitory activity was confirmed to successfully block merozoite invasion.
Conclusion:
This study found new ligands that can stop the interaction between PvDBP and DARC. These are useful methods for controlling
P. vivax
. These compounds could serve as positive controls in future research and have potential as candidates for a new vaccine strategy to prevent
P. vivax
invasion. The rising prevalence of
P. vivax
and the lack of effective control measures necessitate novel therapeutic strategies. This research establishes a robust foundation for further exploration of ligand-based inhibition strategies. Future studies will focus on optimizing these compounds and exploring their potential applications, thereby advancing our ability to model and target
P. vivax
invasion mechanisms with precision and reliability.

Background:
[Fe-S] cluster biogenesis is a multistep process that involves assembly of [Fe-S] cluster on protein scaffold and their transfer to target proteins. In
Plasmodium
three such pathways viz. SUF, ISC and CIA have been reported, however, the parasite’s need for different pathways and their crosstalk needs further investigation which demands characterization of the proteins involved in these pathways. Therefore, we aim to study a BolA-like protein suggested to be involved in the [Fe-S] cluster biogenesis pathway and its interactome from
Plasmodium
with an aim to evaluate them as potential drug targets.
Materials and methods:
The sequences of BolA-like protein from
Plasmodium falciparum and Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) were retrieved from PlasmoDB. The sequence was analyzed using various in-silico tools such as Conserved Domain search (CDD-NCBI), Clustal Omega, MEGA etc. Further, to perform molecular characterization of BolA-like protein, parasite DNA was isolated from clinical isolates and gene encoding BolA-like protein was amplified, cloned and expressed. Purified recombinant protein was analyzed using various biochemical assays and used for Immunolocalization studies.
Results:

P. falciparum
and
P. vivax
field isolates was found to be conserved with other
Plasmodium
species. A silent mutation was observed in
P. falciparum
sequences where Cytosine (C) was replaced by Adenine (A) nucleotide coding for proline. The interactome showed few proteins absent from human host which might be crucial to the parasite development and survival, thus can be used as a potential drug target.
Conclusion:
Our study concludes that the BolA-like protein is evolutionary conserved among different organisms. Data mining and in-silico analysis suggests this protein to be essential where the protein and its interactome can be utilized as potential drug targets.
T3-P7 Insights into Plasmodium vivax interaction with human red blood cells: PvTRAg26.3 interaction with Stomatin and lipid Rafts
Manish Tripathi
, Sumit Rathore

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-P7
Background:
Aligned with the global objective to eliminate malaria, continuous efforts are made in the field of malaria biology. However, the parasite is similarly advancing itself to be in competition. Our information about parasites is the tip of the iceberg, making this tug of war into the parasite side. Molecular mechanisms of host-parasite interaction during malaria remain elusive, especially in the case of
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
)
.
For a long time, the Duffy antigen was thought to be the sole receptor for
P. vivax
invasion, but recent discoveries have challenged this understanding. However, alternative pathways of invasion are now coming up
. P. vivax
has a specific gene family named
Pv-fam-a
that encodes forty tryptophan-rich antigens (PvTRAgs). Several PvTRAgs bind to host erythrocytes and reticulocytes, but identifying their host receptors or interacting partners requires further investigation.
Methods and results:
In his study, we identified erythrocyte interacting partner for one of the significant
P. vivax
tryptophan-rich antigens, i.e., PvTRAg26.3 (PVX_112660). PvTRAg26.3 is known to be eliciting cellular and humoral immune responses during
P. vivax
infection. We had identified PvTRAg26.3 interactions with erythrocytes using flow cytometry-based assays in our work. Further analysis with Immunopulldowns and LC–MS, confirms that Stomatin (STOM_HUMAN), which is part of lipid raft, as an interacting partner for PvTRAg26.3. This interaction was further supported by direct binding between PvTRAg26.3 and Stomatin during solid-phase binding and biolayer interferometry. Further, the ability pf PvTRAg26.3 to interact with RBC membrane lipid was checked using lipid strip assay. Result showed that PvTRAg26.3 have strong interaction with two of RBC membrane lipids- Phosphatidic acid and Phosphatidyl glycerol.
Conclusion:
This finding enhances our understanding of the interaction between parasite ligands and host protein in
Plasmodium vivax
, a critical factor in disease progression. This lipid-protein raft interaction with
P. vivax
interaction may pave a new way for developing Immunotherapeutics to combat this parasitic infection.

blood-stage vaccine antigens
Prasun Kundu

, Usheer Kanjee

, Deboki Naskar

, Manoj Duraisingh

Department of Parasitology, Institute of Biomedical Sciences, University of São Paulo, São Paulo, Brazil;

Global Health and Tropical Medicine, Associate Laboratory in Translation and Innovation Towards Global Health, LA-REAL, Institute of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine, NOVA University of Lisbon, Lisbon, Portugal

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-P8
Background:
Global malaria vaccine development efforts have primarily targeted
Plasmodium falciparum
(
P. falciparum
), culminating in the WHO’s approval of two vaccines based on
P. falciparum
Circumsporozoite Protein. By contrast,
P. vivax
vaccine development remains in its early stages, mainly focusing on
P. vivax
Duffy Binding Protein (PvDBP). However, PvDBP has well-characterised polymorphism which can lead to variant-specific immune responses and is known to undergo frequent gene amplification. Relying on it as the sole target therefore carries some risks. This underscores the need to identify additional targets that could either complement PvDBP or serve as alternative vaccine candidates.
Materials and methods:
Blood-stage vaccines (BSVs) for
P. vivax
are particularly promising for
P. vivax
as the parasite's reticulocyte-restriction leads to lower parasitemias and therefore a reduced blood-stage biomass for vaccine-induced immunity to overcome. We used a reverse vaccinology approach to identify and screen new potential BSV candidates. Candidates expressed predominantly in the invasive merozoite stage were identified by analyzing published RNAseq data. We then selected genes with an N-terminal signal peptide, indicating potential exposure on the merozoite surface and accessibility to the immune system, and with full-length ectodomains < 200 kDa, which is the maximum that can be routinely expressed in the HEK293 system. 240 proteins were screened for expression, and those that express well were screened in two ways: (a) a novel flow-based binding assay testing the ability of purified recombinant proteins to bind human reticulocytes or erythrocytes, and (b) growth inhibition assays (GIAs) using polyclonal antibodies generated against the expressed candidates in
P. knowlesi
and
P. cynomolgi
model systems. Promising candidates from this screen will further be tested on
P. vivax
ex vivo samples.
Results:
The full-length ectodomains of 240 proteins were screened for expression with 85 yielding sufficient protein expression for subsequent assays. 30 candidates showed binding activity in the reticulocyte/erythrocyte binding assay to one or both human RBC types. Among the strongest binders were members of the Tryptophan-Rich Antigen (TRAg) family, which is significantly expanded in
P. vivax
. Experimental determination of the structure of the TRAg domain suggests a lipid binding, which was validated in subsequent assays [1].
In parallel to the RBC binding assay, a low-volume (30 µl) high-throughput growth inhibition assay was developed to screen antibodies against these 85 targets across three parasite models (Pk/Pc/Pf). Notably, antibodies against one candidate, which showed strong erythrocyte and reticulocyte binding, also demonstrated over 80% GIA activity in all models, making it a promising
P. vivax
blood-stage vaccine candidate (Dass, Kundu et al., 2025, Under review).
Conclusion:
In conclusion, the study employed a large-scale systematic screening approach to identify promising
P. vivax
vaccine targets. Several candidates with strong RBC binding and/or GIA activity were identified and have strong potential for subsequent development as potential
P. vivax
vaccine targets. Further research based on these findings could significantly advance prevention efforts for this neglected disease.
References:
Kundu, P., Naskar, D., McKie, S.J. et al
.
The structure of a
Plasmodium vivax
Tryptophan Rich Antigen domain suggests a lipid binding function for a pan-
Plasmodium
multi-gene family.
Nat Commun
14, 5703 (2023).

2025,
