Background:
VIR/PvPIR proteins encoded by
vir/pvpir
genes, a multigene family of variant surface proteins in P. vivax [1], might be involved in cytoadherence leading to severe complications such as cerebral malaria, hepatic dysfunction etc [2]. Similar complications have been reported in
Plasmodium falciparum
reportedly due to cytoadherence mediated by var genes [3]. Microarray data validated by qPCR from our lab shows upregulation of some
vir/pvpir
genes from patients with cerebral malaria. So, a detailed in-silico analysis is being done using different tools to study these
vir/pvpir
genes and the interaction of the proteins encoded by these genes with different human endothelial cell surface receptors and followed by experimental validation.
Materials and methods:
We have selected few
vir/pvpir
genes which have been upregulated in parasites from patients with cerebral malaria. The data has been generated using microarray experimentation and validated using qPCR by us. A detailed in-silico analysis of the protein sequences of these genes have been done including protein conserved domain analysis by CDD-NCBI database [
https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Structure/cdd/wrpsb.cgi
], sequence similarity analysis by pBLAST tool [
https://blast.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Blast.cgi?PAGE=Proteins
], physiochemical properties prediction by ProtParam [
https://web.expasy.org/protparam/
] and Cyscon tool [
http://www.csbio.sjtu.edu.cn/bioinf/Cyscon/
], PEXEL or PEXEL like motif analysis by 3of5 tool [
https://3of5.dkfz.de/mga2/3of5/
], subcellular localization prediction by DeepLoc 2.1 tool [
https://services.healthtech.dtu.dk/services/DeepLoc-2.1/
], 2D structure prediction and analysis by PSIPRED [
http://bioinf.cs.ucl.ac.uk/psipred/
] and DeepTMHMM 1.0 tool [
https://services.healthtech.dtu.dk/services/DeepTMHMM-1.0/
], 3D model prediction and analysis by trRosetta tool [
https://yanglab.qd.sdu.edu.cn/trRosetta/
], 3D model validation by SAVESv6.1 [
https://saves.mbi.ucla.edu/
], energy minimization by Maestro Schrödinger suite [
https://www.schrodinger.com/platform/products/maestro/
], protein-protein docking between VIR/PvPIR proteins and human endothelial cell surface receptors using HADDOCK 2.4 tool ICPvR-2025 120 [
https://rascar.science.uu.nl/haddock2.4/
], and molecular dynamic simulation of the docked product by GROMACS tool [
https://www.gromacs.org/
].
Results:
The selected VIR/PvPIR proteins have very less sequence identity (<50%) compare to other multigene family from different species of Plasmodium involved in similar function. Conserved domains with unknown function were found in these proteins. The proteins were found to be hydrophilic in nature with a higher instability score. PEXEL ([KR][GAVLIMFWPSTCYNQ][LI][GAVLIMFWPSTCYNQ][DEQ]) or PEXEL like motif ([KR]. [LI].[DEQ]) which is required for a VIR/PvPIR protein to be transported either inside the cytoplasm or on the surface of infected RBCs are present in these proteins. The selected VIR/PvPIR proteins are mainly composed of helix and loop with a transmembrane region. The subcellular localization of these proteins is predicted to be on cell membrane. Since, there were no better homologous templates was found therefore an ab-initio models were created for these proteins. Possible active binding pockets have been predicted for site specific protein-protein docking for these proteins. The site-specific protein-protein docking and molecular dynamic simulation is in process.
Conclusion:
These in-silico studies will help us to recognize the possible host endothelial cell surface receptors (I- CAM-1, V-CAM or CD-36) that might bind to the selected VIR/PvPIR proteins, encoded by
vir/pvpir
genes upregulated in
Plasmodium vivax
cerebral malaria samples. The multiple active binding pockets of a particular VIR/PvPIR protein which may interact with the different endothelial cell surface receptors may suggest multiple roles for the same. The results of in-silico studies will be validated by experimental studies which may further strengthen our objective of study.

T3-P10 Identification of novel interactions between the conserved antigen
Plasmodium
TRAg and human erythrocytes
Lee W
, Jun H, Ernest M, Louis J, Fitriana F, Syahada J, Han E, Han J
Department of Medical Environmental Biology and Tropical Medicine, School of Medicine, Kangwon National University, Chuncheon 24341, Republic of Korea
Correspondence:
Han J. (han.han@kangwon.ac.kr)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T3-P10
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) is a significant malaria pathogen, responsible for numerous cases and substantial morbidity worldwide. A critical step in the infection process is the invasion of human erythrocytes by merozoites, which involves various molecular interactions between malaria ligands and host cell receptors. However, the limited ability to culture
P. vivax
in vitro poses a challenge for basic research. Therefore, we used
Plasmodium knowlesi
(
P. knowlesi
), a species phylogenetically like
P. vivax
, as a model to investigate the molecular interactions between tryptophan-rich antigens (TRAg) and human erythrocytes.
Materials and methods:
Based on bioinformatics analysis and previous studies on
P. vivax
TRAg, we identified
P. knowlesi
TRAg genes that exhibit high sequence homology and demonstrate binding activity to human erythrocytes. PkTRAg38.3 and PkTRAg40.1 were successfully expressed as functional recombinant proteins, and their binding abilities to human erythrocytes and reticulocytes were verified using flow cytometry and cell-based ELISA. Confirmation of PkTRAg localization in schizont-stage parasites was achieved using antigen-specific immune sera through immunofluorescence assay (IFA) analysis. The antigenicity of the IgG response to the target protein in
P. knowlesi
patient sera was evaluated using a protein microarray. Erythrocyte membrane proteins that interact with PkTRAg38.3 and PkTRAg40.1 were identified using co-affinity chromatography and MALDI-TOF/MS. Further analysis revealed specific binding sites for PkTRAg40.1 and its host partner proteins.
Results:
We conducted a characterization study using
P. knowlesi
TRAg to understand the interaction mechanism between
P. vivax
ligands and host erythrocyte proteins. PkTRAg was expressed as a soluble recombinant protein, and antigen-specific antibodies were generated by immunization. We examined the binding ability of PkTRAg38.3 and PkTRAg40.1 to human reticulocytes and erythrocytes. As a result, PkTRAg38.3 exhibited binding activity to both reticulocytes and erythrocytes, whereas PkTRAg40.1 showed no significant binding activity. However, PkTRAg40.1 exhibited significant binding activity in a cell-based ELISA using permeabilized erythrocytes. To evaluate antigenicity, a protein array using sera from 70
P. knowlesi
patients revealed low immunogenicity for all of them. The localization of PkTRAg in infected erythrocytes revealed distinct staining patterns. Finally, the interactions between PkTRAg38.3 and PkTRAg40.1 with human host erythrocyte proteins were examined. PkTRAg40.1 specifically bound to the erythrocyte cytoskeletal protein α-spectrin, particularly interacting with the α 6–11 and α 12–16 repeat regions of α-spectrin.
Conclusion:
PkTRAg38.3 binds directly to human reticulocytes and erythrocytes, whereas PkTRAg40.1 binds only to membrane-permeable erythrocytes. Both proteins elicit relatively low IgG antibody responses in
P. knowlesi
patients. The localization of PkTRAg38.3 is associated with the neck of the schizont parasite, while PkTRAg40.1 is located on the merozoite surface. Further studies are needed to clarify their co-localization regions. PkTRAg40.1 interacts with the host erythrocyte cytoskeletal protein α-spectrin, suggesting that PkTRAg38.3 acts as an invasion ligand by binding to host cell receptors, while PkTRAg40.1 is involved in protein trafficking and cytoskeletal remodeling within infected erythrocytes.

Malaria Journal
2025,

T3-P11
Background:
Heat Shock Proteins (HSPs) are critical molecular chaperones, categorized into various classes based on their molecular mass, which assist in protein folding and support organisms in adapting to extreme environmental conditions. Despite their importance, the role of HSPs in the stress response of mosquitoes remains insufficiently understood. This study investigates Heat Shock Protein 70 (HSP-70) in two primary Indian malaria vectors, Anopheles culicifacies and Anopheles stephensi, aiming to elucidate its role in stress response mechanisms and its potential application in vector control.
Materials and methods:
The investigation involved a comprehensive bioinformatics analysis of HSP-70 in An. culicifacies and An. stephensi using tools such as BLASTP, tBLASTn, and Pfam for sequence analysis, and phylogenetic and syntenic analyses to explore evolutionary relationships. Experimental validation included qPCR to examine HSP70 expression across developmental stages and under various stress conditions, alongside recombinant protein expression and characterization. Functional studies involved RNAi-mediated silencing of HSP70-1 in An. culicifacies larvae, coupled with selective inhibition of HSP70 to evaluate effects on developmental cycle of mosquito and
Plasmodium
development inside mosquito.
Results:
Seven distinct subcellular localizations of HSP70 isoforms were identified. Notably, HSP70-1 exhibited increased expression at the L3 larval stage, underscoring its role in larval development. Silencing HSP70-1 led to significant upregulation of other HSP70 isoforms indicating compensation of stress. In
P. berghei
-infected mosquitoes, HSP70 inhibition markedly impaired oocyst development, suggesting its critical role in parasite survival within the mosquito host.
Conclusion:
This study provides novel insights into the function of HSP70 in malaria vectors, particularly how its inhibition impacts mosquito development and malaria parasite progression. The findings suggest that targeting HSP70 could serve as a potential vector control strategy. By examining the interactions between HSP70 and malaria parasites, this research identifies exploitable vulnerabilities that may aid in reducing malaria transmission.
Theme 4: Immunology Unmasked: Natural Infections, Cryptic Reservoirs

Malaria Journal
2025,

T4-O1
Background:
Duffy Binding Protein region II (DBPII) is a leading
Plasmodium vivax
blood-stage vaccine candidate, but variations in its B-cell epitopes lead to strain-specific immunity [1]. The engineered vaccine DEKnull-2 has successfully elicited broadly inhibitory IgG antibodies that block
P.vivax
reticulocyte invasion [2]. In a 15-year follow-up study in the Brazilian Amazon, individuals with stable strain-transcending DBPII antibody response, termed elite-responder (ER), were identified [3]. We hypothesize that more efficient Tfh and B-cells interactions in ER drive a protective and stable DBPII antibody response. This study phenotypically characterized the Tfh and B cell subsets in ER to optimize DEKnull-2 as vaccine target.
Materials and Methods:
A 15-year follow-up study involved samples of the long-term
P.vivax
-exposed individuals from Brazilian Amazon region, including ER with stable strain-transcending DBPII antibody response, as well as non-Responder (NR) with persistent absence of an IgG response. Peripheral blood mononuclear cell (PBMC) samples were stimulated with DBPII and DEKnull-2 for 6 days at 37 °C in 5% CO₂. Following stimulation, live cells were stained with a panel of antibodies to identify T follicular helper (Tfh) cells and B cell subsets using flow cytometry.
Results:
There was no significant difference in the frequencies of total Tfh cells, activated B cells, and classical memory B cells between the ER and NR groups (p > 0.05). However, the frequencies of activated and Th2-like Tfh cell subsets, T-bet⁺ atypical B cells, and plasma cells tended to be higher in ER compared to NR for both DBPII and DEKnull-2.
Conclusions:
In summary, these results highlight the importance of Th2-like Tfh cell and Tbet + atypical B cell subsets associated with individuals with a stable, strain-transcending DBPII antibody response, providing insights for vaccine design. Furthermore, the findings reinforce the potential of DEKnull-2 as a component of a multi-stage, multivalent vaccine against
Plasmodium vivax
malaria.

T4-O3 An association of TLR polymorphism and cytokines production would help in vaccine designing for
Plasmodium vivax
Upninder Kaur
1
, Davinder Kaur
1
, Rakesh Sehgal
1
Department of Medical Parasitology, Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh, India;
2
Department of Microbiology, Aarupadai Veedu Medical College and Hospital, VMRF-DU, Pondicherry, India

Rakesh Sehgal (sehgalpgi@gmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,

T4-O3
Background:
Malaria is categorized into symptomatic or asymptomatic on the basis of clinical manifestations of the parasite and the patients of symptomatic category are further divided into complicated and uncomplicated cases according to WHO guidelines. Earlier studies on influence of genetic polymorphisms in TLRs on the changes in the dynamics of circulating cytokines are very rare. In the present study, innate and adaptive immune responses were elucidated to study their role in severity of disease and parasitic infection in
P. vivax
clinical isolates. For innate immune response polymorphisms in TLRs including (TLR 1 (I602S), TLR6 (S249P) and two promoter regions of TLR9 T-(1486)-C and T-(1237)-C) were studied. Adaptive immune response was checked by analyzing levels of pro-inflammatory and anti-inflammatory cytokines. Influence of genetic polymorphisms present in TLR genes on the production of pro-inflammatory and regulatory cytokines in individuals infected with
P. vivax
was also investigated.
Materials and methods:
Sixty-five microscopically positive
P. vivax
clinical isolates were collected and divided into complicated (n = 18) and uncomplicated (n = 47) according to WHO guidelines. 20 healthy controls (n = 20) were also included. DNA was extracted from all the collected samples using DNA extraction kit. Genetic polymorphisms in TLR1 (I602S), TLR6 (S249P) and two promoter regions of TLR9 T- (1486)-C and T-(1237)-C were detected through PCR–RFLP [1]. Serum samples were collected from all the enrolled patients and levels of circulating cytokines including IFN-Ƴ, TNF-α, IL-6 and IL-10 were measured by using ELISA. Association between TLR genotype and alterations in levels of different cytokines was detected by unpaired t-test and p < 0.01 was considered statistically significant. [2 & 3].
Results:
Among the studied TLRs genetic variation for TLR 1 (I602S), TLR6 (S249P) and promoter region of TLR9 T-(1237)-C were > 10% for complicated and uncomplicated patients while for promoter region of TLR 9 T-(1486)-C genetic variation was present in 27.7% (5/18) of complicated malaria cases and 36.5% (17/47) of uncomplicated malaria patients, thus showing association of this variation with infection but not with the disease severity.
An association between TLR 9 (1486) T/C variants and the cytokines TNF-α as well as IL-6 was absent. While in case of IL-10 and IFN-γ a significant decline was observed in genotype TLR 9 1486 T as compared to TLR 9 1486C, suggesting that polymorphism at TLR9 1486 alters the levels of IL-10 and IFN-γ.
Conclusion:
Significant interactions observed between TLR polymorphism and cytokines revealed the influence of SNPs in promoter region of TLR9 T-(1486)-C on the cytokines production. Cytokines play important role in disease pathogenesis as well as disease severity, so an association of TLR polymorphism and cytokines production would help in vaccine designing as well as designing tools for control and elimination of malaria.
References:
Leoratti FM, Farias L, Alves FP, Suarez-Mútis MC, Coura JR, Kalil J, Camargo EP, Moraes SL, Ramasawmy R. Variants in the toll-like receptor signaling pathway and clinical outcomes of malaria. The Journal of infectious diseases. 2008;198(5):772–80.
Costa AG, Ramasawmy R, Ibiapina HN, Sampaio VS, Xábregas LA, Brasil LW, Tarragô AM, Almeida AC, Kuehn A, Vitor-Silva S, Melo GC. Association of TLR variants with susceptibility to
Plasmodium vivax
malaria and parasitemia in the Amazon region of Brazil. PLoS One. 2017;12(8): e0183840.

Plasmodium vivax
malaria: TLR polymorphisms influence cytokine productions in malaria-vivax. Cytokine. 2018; 110:374

Malaria Journal
2025,

T4-O4
Background:
The leading
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) blood-stage vaccine candidate, Duffy binding protein region II (DBPII), has limitations, including high genetic polymorphism and strain-specific immunity. Thus, it seems to be essential to investigate the EBP2, a second
P. vivax
Erythrocyte Binding Protein [1]. EBP2 preferentially binds to young reticulocytes in the bone marrow, suggesting that may be involved in the extra-bloodstream parasite cycle [2]. A worldwide genetic diversity of EBP2, demonstrated that region II of EBP2 (EBP2-II) is less polymorphic than DBPII, including in the Brazilian Amazon [3]. Therefore, the immune response against EBP2 needs to be investigated.
Materials and methods:
The experimental approach involved a 15-year follow-up study carried out among long-term
P. vivax
exposure Amazon individuals (n = 363) and included cross-sectional surveys at periods of high and low malaria transmission. Anti-EBP2 and anti-DBPII IgG antibodies were measured using conventional ELISA assays. EBP2-specific cellular responses were evaluated by flow cytometry using commercial antibodies against different sub-sets of B/T cells, including T follicular helper (Tfh).
Results:
Regardless of the period of malaria transmission, the frequency and persistence of anti-EBP2 IgG antibodies were significantly higher than those of anti-DBPII IgG. Curves of antibody survival demonstrated that the average time in which 50% of responders lost their antibody response for DBPII was 72 months. In contrast, over 50% of responders retained detectable antibody levels against EBP2 for up to 180 months (Fig. 1). Preliminary data on EBP2-specific cellular response showed high frequencies of atypical B cell population.
Fig. 1
Survival curves of antibodies for EBP2 and DBPII. ****
p
< 0.0001, Log-rank (Mantel-Cox) test
Conclusions:
In these individuals with long-term exposure to malaria, the humoral immune response against EBP2 was stronger and more persistent than against DBPII. Studies are in progress to investigate the role of higher frequency of EBP2-specific atypical B cells. These findings reinforce EBP2-II as a promising vaccine candidate to compose a multi-target vaccine against
P. vivax.

Plasmodium vivax
erythrocyte invasion genes. PLoS Neglected Tropical Diseases. 2013; 7(12): e2569.

Background:
Our study aimed to test the hypothesis that asymptomatic individuals (Asym) infected with
P. vivax
from the Peruvian Amazon present different adaptive immune responses compared to
P. vivax
-infected symptomatic individuals (Sym) that are predictive of clinical immunity.
Materials and methods:
Serum/plasma samples from Asym (n = 27) and Sym (n = 35)
P. vivax
-infected subjects from the Peruvian Amazonian collected between 2018 and 2020 were evaluated [1]. Samples from healthy individuals from the same malaria-endemic communities (n = 9) were assessed as controls (no history of malaria in the past 3 years and no
Plasmodium
infection confirmed by microscopy and qPCR). IgM, IgG, and IgG1 and IgG3 subclass antibody (Ab) levels against a panel of 34
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
antigens were measured in a bead-based multiplex platform (Luminex®).
Results:
Comparison of antibody levels between study groups for each antigen revealed a higher IgG response in Asym
P. vivax
-infected individuals against antigen
P. vivax
Merozoite Surface Protein 7 (PVX_082645) (p ≤ 0.01) and
P. falciparum
Merozoite Surface Protein 2 (FC27 strain) (p ≤ 0.05), in comparison to Sym individuals. No significant differences were found for IgM and IgG1 levels between Asym and Sym individuals. Flexible Discriminant Analysis (FDA) evidenced it trends of higher IgG and IgG3 antibody responses in the asymptomatic group, that allowed a better clusterization for its discrimination.
Conclusion:
This preliminary study shows that Asym
P. vivax
-infected individuals from the Peruvian Amazon, were more prone to display a higher IgG and IgG3 antibody response in comparison to Sym P. vivax-infected individuals from the same endemic context.

T4-P1 Effect of
Plasmodium vivax
infection on cytokines and chemokines and their role in the degrees of thrombocytopenia
Marian Muskus, Maria Velasco, Maria Yasnot
University of Cordoba, Montería, Córdoba, Colombia
Correspondence:
Marian Muskus (mmmuskusmontiel@correo.unicordoba.edu.co)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T4-P1
Background:
Thrombocytopenia is a common manifestation in patients with
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) monoinfection confirmed by PCR. The pathophysiological mechanisms associated with the decrease in platelet count are still a focus of research. Studies have suggested that during
P. vivax
episodes there is an uncontrolled increase in proinflammatory molecules in the host, which has been associated with the presence of autoantibodies directed against blood cell components, highlighting phosphatidylserine (PS). Therefore, we believe that thrombocytopenia in vivax malaria is the result of an autoimmune response elicited by autoantibodies against PS.
Materials and methods:
This research was descriptive and analytical, cross-sectional. 163 samples (whole blood) were obtained from patients with
P. vivax
monoinfection in the municipality of Tierralta and were classified into four study groups. The project received approval from the Human Ethics Committee of the Health Sciences Faculty of the University of Cordoba. Plasma quantification of cytokines was performed using flow cytometry. Autoimmunity was assessed by enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA), using PS as antigen, and a flow cytometry assay was performed to demonstrate PS exposure on the platelet membrane, using FITC-labeled Annexin V.
Results:
The porinflammatory cytokines and chemokines, INF-γ, IL-6, MCP-1 and IP-10, IL-8 was shown to have significantly elevated concentrations (P < 0.005) in patients with vivax malaria and severe thrombocytopenia [1] (Fig. 1). The concentration of TGF-β1 was found to be significantly increased in patients with
P. vivax
infection but without thrombocytopenia or with a mild degree (P < 0.0001) [1] (Fig. 1). An increase in anti-PS antibodies was evident in patients with malaria and thrombocytopenia versus patients with malaria without thrombocytopenia (p < 0.05). Furthermore, a significant negative correlation was observed between them (r = − 0.19, P = 0.016). That is, the larger the relative units of these, the lower the platelet count. Finally, flow cytometry indicated that patients with thrombocytopenia had greater expression of phosphatidylserine in their membrane compared to the healthy control.
Fig. 1
Plasma concentration of proinflammatory cytokines (INFγ, IL-17a, IL-6) and anti-inflammatory cytokines (IL-10, IL-4, TGF-β1), in the different study groups: mild thrombocytopenia (TL), moderate thrombocytopenia (TMO), severe thrombocytopenia (TS), malaria without thrombocytopenia (MST)
Conclusion:
It is concluded that thrombocytopenia is caused by multifactorial mechanisms, such as phagocytosis enhanced by inflammation caused by INF-γ and IL-6, and by the development of autoantibodies against phosphatidylserine. Currently, there is little researchs on the possible pathophysiological mechanisms that trigger the trombocytopenia. For this reason, we believe that it is very important to divulge this research, since we were able to demonstrate the effect of
P. vivax
infection on plasma concentrations of cytokines, chemokines and autoantibodies in the different degrees of thrombocytopenia that can be triggered in a patient.
References:
Punnath K et al. Association between Inflammatory Cytokine Levels and Thrombocytopenia during
Plasmodium falciparum
and
P. vivax
Infections in South-Western Coastal Region of India. Malaria research and treatment. 2019; 2019: 4296523.
T4-P5 Mesenchymal stem cells restore erythropoiesis and lymphoiesis during malaria infection
Thakur Reva Sharan, Das Jyoti
ICMR-National Institute of Malaria Research, Immunology Division, New Delhi, India
Correspondence:
Thakur Reva Sharan (revathakur07@gmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T4-P5
Background:
Malaria is a major public health concern worldwide. The immunological mechanisms that mediate malaria protection yet to be discovered. MSCs are immune cells that can self-renew and differentiate into tissues of mesodermal origin. They also support hematopoiesis by releasing various molecules and transcription factors in development of progenitor cells (HSPCs) [1]. In malaria infection T cells exhaustion also has been reported [2]. Two key events are required to provide protection against malaria infection. First, avoid anemia, followed by a protective cellular immune response.
Materials and methods:
Balb/c mice were infected with 5 × 10
5
Plasmodium berghei
(Pb) parasitized erythrocytes via intraperitoneal injection. MSCs were isolated from Pb infected and normal mice. Experimental animals were divided into three groups. One group of animals was injected with MSCs (5 × 10
6
cells) from malaria-infected mice through the tail vein (iMSC), the second group of mice was given MSCs from normal mice (nMSCs) whereas the third group of mice received no cells. Third-day post injection, blood collected from tails was used to prepare smears to determine parasitic load. Day 7, mice were sacrificed for harvesting different organs. Splenocytes were used flowcytometry analysis and RNA were used for real time PCR. CD34
+
cells were analyzed in spleen and lymph node. Furthermore, hematopoietic progenitor cells were analyzed in cultured bone marrow (2 × 10
5
) cells. T cell responses during malaria infection were studied day post infection.
Results:
We previously established that mesenchymal stem cells (MSCs) influence the immune response and contribute to malaria resistance. Here, we demonstrate that MSCs reprogram haematopoiesis in primary (bone marrow) and secondary (spleen) lymphoid organs to protect the host from malaria. Adoptively transferred MSCs enhanced generation of erythroid cells and also boosted the expression of GATA-1. We found a little increase in CD34
+
hematopoietic stem and progenitor cells in lymphoid organs, including the spleen and lymph nodes. Infusion of MSCs increased T cell proliferation, resulting in more CD4
+
and CD8
+
T cells in the spleen. MSCs inhibited T cells exhumation by inhibition of PD-1 in recipient animals. Our findings suggest that MSCs regulate erythropoiesis and lymphopoiesis to protect the host against malaria infection.
Conclusion:
MSCs have been shown to protect mice from malaria infection by restoring CD34+ hematopoietic cells, CD4
+
and CD8
+
T lymphocytes, and lowering negative costimulatory molecules on T lymphocytes. Infected MSCs have stronger immunological stimulatory characteristics, leading to increased T cell proliferation compared to uninfected MSCs. MSCs promoted the reconstitution of erythroid and lymphoid cells, which could improve MSC engraftment Taken together, these findings suggested that MSCs act through multiple signals that are capable of combatting malaria infection.
References:
Ohneda K, Yamamoto M. Roles of hematopoietic transcription factors GATA-1 and GATA-2 th development of red blood cell lineage. Acta Haematol. 2002; 108: 237–245.
Trautmann L et al. Upregulation of PD-1 expression on HIV-specific CD8 + T cells leads to reversible immune dysfunction. Nat. Med. 2006; 12: 1198–1202.
Theme 5: Bridging the gap: advanced model systems for
Plasmodium vivax
T5-O1 Using
P. knowlesi
to unravel DBP biology and facilitate scalable assessment of PvDBP polymorphisms in parasite fitness and antibody susceptibility
Kostas Kousis
1
, Amy Ibrahim
1
, Mimi M Hou
2
, Melissa N Hart
1
, James A Charleston
1
, Franziska Mohring
1
, Thomas A. Rawlinson
2
, Doris Quinkert
2
, Angela M Minassian
2
, Simon J Draper
2
, Robert W. Moon
1
1
Department of Infection Biology, Faculty of Infectious and Tropical Diseases, London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine, London, UK;
2
Department of Biochemistry, University of Oxford, Oxford, UK
Correspondence:
Robert W. Moon (rob.moon@lshtm.ac.uk)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T5-O1
Background:
The binding of
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) Duffy binding protein (DBP) to the Duffy Antigen receptor for Chemokines (DARC) is essential for invasion of red blood cells (RBCs). The interaction underpins
P. vivax
epidemiology and is a prime candidate for blood stage vaccines. However, the inability to grow
P. vivax
in vivo has hampered efforts to understand its mechanistic role during invasion as well as efforts to design a vaccine that is able to counter polymorphic variants of PvDBP. The closely related zoonotic malaria parasite
P. knowlesi
shares the DBP-DARC invasion pathway and thus has provided a key tool for its study.
Materials and methods:
We have used genome-editing to replace the
P. knowlesi
orthologue (PkDBPα) with PvDBP and shown it can fully complement its essential role during invasion [1]. Using tagging and conditional knockout of DBP in
P. knowlesi
we have been able to follow the role of this protein during the complex multistep invasion process—taking advantage of the larger merozoites of
P. knowlesi
to study the process in exceptional detail with live-cell microscopy. In addition to studying the fine details of invasion, we have also used the high-efficiency genome-editing [2] enabled by
P. knowlesi
developed tools to scalable assess the effect of PvDBP polymorphisms, both on parasite fitness and susceptibility to vaccine induced PvDBP antibodies [3].
Results:
Using live-cell microscopy we have quantified secretion dynamics of DBP, and demonstrated that it forms a unique asymmetric localisation across the surface of the merozoite just prior to invasion. Conditional knock-out of DBP has no effect on the initial “cell selection” steps of invasion with unaffected gliding and erythrocyte deformation, but parasites can no longer form a tight junction or reorientate for internalisation. By generating and pooling
P. knowlesi
lines expressing 7 polymorphic variants of PvDBP we have developed a bar-seq based approach to asses fitness defects and antibody susceptibility. This allows us to screen vaccine derived monoclonals against all 7 PvDBP variants in a single well of a 96-well plate—helping to rapidly identify strain-transcending antibodies, and precisely determining breadth of activity for strain specific antibodies. Work to expand our library of PvDBP variants should enable us to capture all known PvDBP-RII variation within the field.
Conclusion:
The lack of in vitro culture for
P. vivax
, means that even for well-studied proteins like PvDBP we still lack comprehensive understanding of how they function within parasites, and a paucity of tools for rapidly and quantitively assessing inhibitory antibodies against them. Techniques developed here can be readily applied to a range of
P. vivax
biology as well as beginning to capture the complexity of
P. vivax
in field to aid rational development of multi-stage vaccines in the lab.
References:

T5-O2 A
Plasmodium knowlesi
A1H1 gene expression time course focusing on the schizont stage
Diaz-Delgado D, De Meulenaere K, Rosanas-Urgell A
Department of Biomedical Sciences, Institute of Tropical Medicine Antwerp, Antwerp, Belgium
Correspondence:
Diaz-Delgado D. (ddiaz@itg.be)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T5-O2
Background:
Among the different
P. vivax
laboratory models,
P. knowlesi
, originally a macaque malaria species, gained popularity in recent years due to the evolutionary closeness between the two species and adapted to grow in human red blood cells. The use of this model has proven valuable for preliminary studies on blood-stage vaccine candidates or drug testing for
P. vivax
, circumventing the unavailability of continuous
P. vivax
culture systems
1
. We have constructed a transcriptome time course of the
P. knowlesi
strain A1H1
2
with good resolution of the schizont stage in order to investigate expression of genes involved in invasion.
Materials and methods:
P. knowlesi
strain A1H1 was cultured in vitro using human red blood cells, and tightly synchronized in a 1 h time window. We collected parasites at five time points: 5 h post-invasion (hpi) (ring stage), 14 hpi (trophozoite stage), 20 hpi, 24 hpi and 27 hpi (schizont stages). Schizont stages were purified on a Nycodenz gradient. Human globin mRNA depletion was performed, after which samples were bulk mRNA sequenced. Read alignment and counting was performed with STAR. Transcriptional profiles of genes associated with invasion were investigated in depth and compared to expression patterns of
P. vivax
orthologs over time (Zhu et al., 2016
3
) after compensating for time-differences in the intraerythrocytic developmental cycle (IDC).
Results:
High-quality sequencing data was obtained for the 5 time points. 10–20 million read pairs were mapped per time-point and enriched in the expected GO terms (e.g. host cell surface binding at 27 hpi). We compared transcriptomes of
P. knowlesi
invasion-associated genes to the corresponding
P. vivax
orthologs (DBP, NBP/RBP, TRAg, MSP, AMA1, RON, etc.). Although they share similar transcriptional profiles over the IDC in both species, the
P. knowlesi
invasion-associated genes are often expressed later in the schizont stage. The same was observed for orthologs with known conserved functions in invasion, such as PvDBP-PkDBPα. Some of these time-expression differences may be due to less synchronous
P. vivax
patient isolates than the cultured
P. knowlesi
time course presented here; although biological differences in the time of expression of invasion genes cannot be excluded.
Conclusion:
We present a tightly synchronized transcriptome time course of the
P. knowlesi
A1H1 strain, which adds relevant data for its use as a model for studying the erythrocytic cell cycle in
P. vivax
. The generated data from five time-points over a 27 h life blood cycle covers the three main intra-erythrocytic stages, with focus on the schizont stage. This will allow to (i) gain insight into expression timing of
P. vivax
genes of interest, specially in those expressed during the schizont-stage (e.g. invasion genes), based on their
P. knowlesi
orthologs, (ii) identify co-expressed
P. knowlesi
genes as new candidates in
P. vivax
processes of interest, or (iii) find out which
P. vivax
genes are suitable to study in a
P. knowlesi
transgenic model based on a shared expression pattern over the IDC.

Malaria Journal

T5-O3
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
hypnozoite remains the most challenging aspect of malaria elimination due to detection difficulty, the potential for relapse, and the absence of accessible G6PD testing for safe hypnozoitocidal treatment [1]. This is mostly true in low- and moderate-endemicity areas where malaria elimination efforts often stagnate [2]. We aim to evaluate the efficacy of
P. vivax
serology as a screening tool to indicate high risk of hypnozoite carriage and guide treatment to prevent relapse.
Materials and methods:
We conducted a community-based open-label randomized controlled trial among children aged 6–15 years in a moderate-endemicity area. Participants were randomized 1:1 to intervention and control arms. The intervention was serological testing with treatment by directly observed high-dose primaquine regimen (1 mg/kgBW/day for 7 days) for the positive participants. We followed all participants monthly for 9 months, checking malaria signs and symptoms and collecting their blood smears. The incidence of
P. vivax
infection detected by microscopic reading was recorded.
Results:
We enrolled 1133 participants, and 772 were available for analysis. Among the untreated control arm, 6 out of 29 seropositive participants became positive during follow-up, compared to 7 out of 358 seronegative (RR = 7.51, p < 0.001); seropositive participants were 7.5 times more likely to experience recurrent parasitemia than seronegative. In comparison to the untreated controls, of the 34 participants who were seropositive and treated, none experienced recurrent parasitemia (OR = 19, p = 0.048); seropositive participants were 19 times more likely to experience recurrence without treatment. In the seronegative group, we found no difference in the incidence of vivax infections between arms (0.017 vs 0.020, p = 0.812). Overall, the incidence of vivax infections over 9 months of follow-up was 0.016 in the intervention arm vs. 0.033 in the control arm (p = 0.11).
Conclusions:
The results suggest that
P. vivax
serology test reliably detected people at highest risk of later experiencing vivax parasitemia during follow-up. Administering treatment to these seropositive participants prevented any recurrence. Therefore, the study proved that test and treatment based on
P. vivax
serology (PvSeroTAT) may be an effective supplementary strategy to accelerate malaria elimination efforts.
Trial registration:
ClinicalTrials.gov ID
NCT04223674
.
References:
Surjadjaja C, Surya A, Baird JK. Epidemiology of Plasmodium vivax in Indonesia. Am J Trop Med Hyg. 2016;95(6 Suppl):121–32.
Arisanti RR, Hawley W, Basri HH, editors. National Action Plan for Acceleration of Malaria Elimination 2020–2026 (Revision). Jakarta: Directorate General of Disease Prevention and Control, Ministry of Health of The Republic of Indonesia (ID); 2023.
Theme 6: Duffy enigma: epidemiology & invasion mechanism
T6-I1 ACKR1 function, Duffy negativity and
P. vivax
epidemiology in Africa
Isaac K. Quaye
1
, Grace K Ababio
1,2
1
Pan African Vivax and Ovale Network, Regent University College of Science and Technology, #1 Regent Ave, McCarthy Hill, Dansoman, Accra, Ghana;
2
Department of Medical Biochemistry, University of Ghana Medical School, Korle-Bu, Accra, Ghana
Correspondence:
Isaac K. Quaye (isaac.quaye@regent.edu.gh)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T6-I1
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
presents a challenge to malaria elimination because of its peculiar biological characteristics to stay hidden outside of the peripheral circulation in the spleen, bone marrow and the liver, from where new blood stages can be seeded [1]. The known pathway for invasion after seeding in the skin is either through the blood stream (80%) or through the lymphatic stream (20%) frequency, respectively. Entry into the tissues is facilitated by the sinusoids which is atypical and by binding to the atypical chemokine receptor 1 (ACKR1) [2]. This receptor binds CC and CXC chemokines, acts as a chemokine sink and reservoir, is absent in the red blood cells of sub-Saharan African populations but expressed in erythroid progenitors and young reticulocytes. It is linked to benign ethnic neutropenia (BEN) that commonly occurs in people of African descent [2]. The work examined ACKR1 function and
P. vivax
epidemiology vis-à-vis Duffy negativity in Africa.
Method:
We collated reports on
P. vivax
infections in Duffy negative individuals in sub-Saharan Africa, including those yet to be reported. These were through PubMed and google scholar searches.
Results:
The data obtained is presented in Fig. 1a and b as the raw data and fractional total per 1000 persons respectively.
Fig. 1
The data shows that wherever
P. vivax
has been found in sub-Saharan Africa, infection in Duffy negatives have been seen. It maybe that the Duffy null phenotype arose from a response mechanism broadly related to the exposure of African populations to pathogens within the environment, including malaria parasites. However,
P. vivax
has found a means to navigate around this restriction for survival. In the spleen and bone marrow where the largest biomass of
P. vivax
exists, ACKR1 is highly expressed in the sinusoids in nucleated erythroid cells (NEC) that are CD71+.
P. vivax
has preference for CD71+ cells and uses sinusoidal entry into tissues from the time of inoculation in the skin [3]. Considering that ACKR1 regulates the interaction between hematopoietic stem progenitor cells (HSPC) and NECs to modulate neutrophil cell output and phenotype, P. vivax appears to exploit the natural defense system for invasion.
Conclusion
: Further work in Duffy null phenotypes/haplotypes in Africa is necessary to understand the evolving invasion mechanisms of
P. vivax.

Background:
Plasmodium knowlesi
, a zoonotic malaria species which has a significant public health impact in Southeast Asia, is also a useful tool for studying
Plasmodium vivax
biology. Like
Plasmodium vivax
,
P. knowlesi
relies on the Duffy-Antigen Receptor for Chemokine (DARC), a human erythrocyte receptor found in Duffy-positive individuals, to invade human erythrocytes. In recent years,
Plasmodium
vivax infections have been reported in Duffy-negative individuals, and here, we investigate the possibility of duffy-negative erythrocyte invasion in
Plasmodium knowlesi.
Materials and methods:
We continually exposed the culture-adapted
P. knowlesi
A1-H.1 parasite line to duffy-negative erythrocytes at a ratio of 4:1 with duffy-positive erythrocytes. After 6 months, parasites were transferred into 100% duffy-negative erythrocytes until a stable in vitro line was established and cloned. We performed a genome assembly of the resultant duffy-negative adapted parasite line (Z1H1) through combining Oxford Nanopore long read data and Illumina short read sequence data and determine the genomic changes allowing for duffy-negative invasion.
Results:
Plasmodium knowlesi
is able to overcome its dependence on DARC and invade duffy-negative erythrocytes. The duffy-negative invasion pathway is independent of DARC-binding, as it is unaffected by α-DARC antibodies, and is due to a complex genomic recombination between two genes in the duffy-binding protein family (DBPα and DBPγ). The genomic recombination event results in a chimeric gene, DBPαγ. We confirm that DBPαγ is necessary for Duffy-negative invasion, as reversal of the DBPαγ locus to DBPα in Z1H1 duffy-negative adapted parasites restores the dependency of parasites on duffy-positive erythrocytes.
Conclusion:
This study sheds light on
P. knowlesi's
invasion plasticity, demonstrating duffy-negative invasion for the first time in this parasite species, which has wider implications for the control of this parasite species. Additionally, this study provides a novel tool for investigating the duffy-negative invasion pathway in
Plamsodium vivax
parasites, where a stable in vitro culture system for this parasite species does not exist. There is currently no defined mechanism for duffy-negative erythrocyte invasion in
P. vivax
parasites, using
Plasmodium knowlesi,
which is genetically closely related, we can investigate DARC-independent mechanisms of invasion, which will aid knowledge for
Plasmodium vivax
research.
Acknowledgements:
This work was supported by Specific League Funds 2021: Mahidol University, Intermediate Fellowship in Public Health and Tropical Medicine: Wellcome Trust, UK Medical Research Council. M.Z. received a PhD grant from Organization for Women in Science for Developing World (OWSD).

Background:
The human
ACKR1
gene encodes a transmembrane glycoprotein like the G-protein coupled receptors. It expresses the Duffy blood group antigens (Fy), which act as receptors for CC and CXC chemokines in innate and adaptive immune responses. The protein also binds the Duffy binding domain (DBP) of
P. vivax
and
P. knowlesi
to facilitate their entry into erythrocytes. Two forms of the protein derived from mutation at c.125G>A on the FY gene gives rise to two alleles FY*A and FY*B. When these are combined with the c1-67T>C mutation, the FY*N (Null) allele results (FY * 02N.01 or (FY*01N.01).
Materials and methods:
We assessed these alleles in addition to the c.136G>A that we previously found in Namibia, and from individuals from Ghana, and Zambia by PCR and Sanger sequencing. We also determined the status of infection for the four human
Plasmodium s
pecies
P. falciparum
,
P. vivax
,
P. ovale wallikeri
and
P. ovale curtisi
.
Results:
We observed that whereas the c1-67T>C was relatively fixed, variations occurred at the c.125 and c.136 loci depending on the country and type of
Plasmodium
parasite that had infected the individual.
Conclusion:
We conclude that these findings may reflect a broader consequence of the role of the ACKR1 gene in immunomodulation rather than strictly
Plasmodium
parasite interactions that requires careful attention.
T6-P1 Distribution of

Background:

Anopheles

T6-P2 Mixed method approach to investigate the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on malaria control in Indigenous communities
Susan Paredes Fernandez

, Paloma Diab García

, Hilde Bastiaens

, Stella Chenet

, Christopher Delgado-Ratto

3

Malaria Journal
2025,

Background:
In Peru, the abrupt interruption of the malaria control programs due to the COVID-19 pandemic led to increased cases in urban areas; however, the real burden and care situation in hard-to-reach indigenous communities remained unknown. Hereby, through a convergent-parallel mixed method study, we explored the prevalence and factors associated with malaria infections in Amazonian indigenous communities in Peru and their populations’ perceptions of COVID-19’s impact on malaria burden and care.
Materials and methods:
We conducted epidemiological surveys in 2021 and 2022 in 4 communities in the Amazonas region. The factors associated with malaria infections were determined using a generalized estimating equation. Also, semi-structured interviews were conducted with inhabitants and healthcare workers. They were audio recorded and transcribed, and thematic analysis was performed based on the procedures stipulated in The International Association for Medical Education Guide [1]. Using a convergent parallel approach, results from the quantitative and qualitative components were integrated through analytic assessment, resulting in a joint display.
Results:
Our main findings demonstrate that (i) there is a higher prevalence of malaria diagnosed with PCR in comparison with microscopy, (ii) perception of the prioritization of COVID-19 interventions over malaria control activities is reflected in the malaria prevalence increment between both years (17.6% for 2021 and 25.5% for 2022); (iii) population highlighted the limited access to healthcare facilities during the pandemic and the absence of health posts in their communities; (iv) population’s behavior leads to malaria infections, for instance, participants expressed that “
people don’t comply to the recommendations
”; and finally, (v) results suggest that as a potential consequence of fearing the new disease, inhabitants migrated to their farms, interfering with any possibility of follow-up within malaria control.
Conclusion:
Our study in hard-to-reach Indigenous communities demonstrated a high prevalence of sub-microscopic and asymptomatic infections; therefore, research to improve the diagnosis and treatment in such contexts is needed [1]. Moreover, the COVID-19 pandemic affected local health services and inhabitants, leaving their communities to avoid contagious, reflected in an increment of malaria infections. The present is a mixed-method study carried out in Indigenous communities in the Peruvian Amazon and provides key information to the national malaria elimination plan.
Reference:
Kiger ME, Varpio L. Thematic analysis of qualitative data: AMEE Guide No. 131. Med Teach. 2020;42(8):846–854.
Theme 7: Diagnostics unlocked: omics, single-cell & serological advancements
T7-I1 Unveiling the biology of
Plasmodium vivax
hypnozoites
Liliana Mancio-Silva
Institut Pasteur, Université Paris Cité, Inserm U1347, Unité Biologie de l’infection et transmission de
Plasmodium
, 75015 Paris, France
Correspondence:
Liliana Mancio-Silva (liliana.mancio@pasteur.fr)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T7-I1
The ability of
Plasmodium vivax
to form dormant hypnozoites in the liver remains a major challenge to malaria eradication. At the 9th International Conference on Plasmodium vivax Research, we presented recent work employing single-cell transcriptomics to dissect the biology of elusive hypnozoites and their host interactions [1]. We identified a 10-gene transcriptional signature that enables classification of hypnozoites. These dormant stages exhibit increased transcript levels of genes encoding for functions implicated in transcriptional and translational repression, as well as proteolytic activity, likely required to maintain viability over extended periods. Leveraging this gene signature, we further identified infected hepatocytes at single-cell resolution. Hepatocytes harboring hypnozoites showed distinct transcriptional profiles compared to other infected or uninfected cells, including significant lower transcript levels of genes encoding hepatic-specific functions and cytokine response pathways. The latter is consistent with the reduced sensitivity of hypnozoites exposed interferon-alfa and -beta treatment and may explain the survival of hypnozoites under immune pressure. These findings support a model in which both parasite and host cell exhibit altered states during dormancy.
We also presented complementary work on host circadian rhythms and their influence on liver-stage malaria infection [2]. Using
Plasmodium falciparum
parasites, we demonstrated that the timing of hepatocyte exposure to sporozoites impacts infection levels. Higher infection rates occurred when interferon-stimulated gene (ISG) expression was at its lowest point, suggesting time-dependent defense responses shape host susceptibility. Given the conserved response of ISG effectors, similar mechanisms likely affect
P. vivax
infection. Furthermore, by setting hepatocytes co-cultures to a defined circadian entrainment, we successfully extended their viability from 21 to over 80 days. This methodology opens new avenues for future studies on hypnozoite longevity, activation cues, and reactivation.
Finally, we highlighted progress toward maintaining a complete
P. vivax
life cycle in laboratory settings using a bone marrow-humanized mouse model [3]. This system supports parasite asexual replication, gametocytogenesis and mosquito transmission, representing a critical step toward sustainable
P. vivax
experimental platforms. Together, these efforts offer the first insights into the molecular composition of
P. vivax
hypnozoites, reveal host-parasite interactions underpinning dormancy, and provide essential tools for future research into relapse biology and radical cure development.
Acknowledgements:
Current research on
P. vivax
in our laboratory is supported by the European Research Council (Grant Agreement No. 101087911) and the Agence Nationale de la Recherche (Grant No. ANR-21-CE35-0006).

Mancio-Silva L et al
.

March S et al
.

https://www.science.org
.

.

Plasmodium
vivax
blood-stage infections and transmission. Nat Commun. 2022; 13(1). 10.1038/s41467-022-31864-6.
T7-O1 Towards the rapid diagnosis of malaria hypnozoite infection: In vitro cultured malaria hypnozoites leave a footprint of specific metabolites
Erica M. Pasini

, Hassan Hakimi

, Nattawat Chaiyawong

, Onny Klop

, Anne-Marie Zeeman

, Ivonne Nieuwenhuis

, Nicole van der Werff

, Kumiko Kihara

, Kenjiro Kami
4
, Clemens HM Kocken
1
, Osamu Kaneko

1
Department of Parasitology, Biomedical Primate Research Center, Rijswijk, The Netherlands;
2
Department of Protozoology, Institute of Tropical Medicine (NEKKEN), Nagasaki University, Nagasaki, Japan;
3

4

Erica M. Pasini (pasini@bprc.nl)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T7-O1
Background:
Plasmodium vivax
malaria control and elimination is complicated by the presence of dormant liver parasites, known as hypnozoites, that can reactivate long after infection giving rise to clinical and transmissible
vivax
malaria, without exposure to new infectious mosquito bites. Hypnozoite infection remains without symptoms and there are no diagnostic tools available to identify hypnozoite carriers.
Materials and methods:
Here we present a Proof-of-Concept study aimed at identifying diagnostic markers for malaria hypnozoite infection, by combining in vitro
Plasmodium cynomolgi
hypnozoite cultures (an accessible proxy to
P. vivax
with near identical biology) with sensitive metabolomics by capillary electrophoresis-mass spectrometry and liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry, which are the methods of choice to identify a variety of hydrophilic and hydrophobic metabolites.
P. cynomolgi
as a hypnozoite-forming parasite and
Plasmodium knowlesi
, a causative agent of the non-human primate and zoonotic malaria, as a non-hypnozoite forming control were compared throughout the study. Multiple biological and technical replicates were used to ensure reproducibility of results.
Results
:
The parasite liver stage cultures were successfully optimized for the metabolomics analysis resulting in the detection of between 567 and 653 metabolites in three biological replicates of the
P. cynomolgi-
and 513 metabolites in the
P. knowlesi
-infected in vitro monkey liver cell culture supernatants. Nineteen specific hypnozoite-related candidate metabolites have been detected in the supernatant of hypnozoite-enriched in vitro liver stage cultures. Pathway analysis was conducted to assess the relevance of these metabolite candidates, which confirmed their importance.
Conclusion:
The finding of specific hypnozoite-related metabolites suggests that, following in vivo validation of such metabolites in the
P. cynomolgi
/rhesus monkey model and subsequent in
P. vivax
-infected individuals, a rapid diagnostic test for hypnozoite infection may be developed. Such diagnostic tools are invaluable for precise mapping of the scale of the infection problem and for identifying individuals that would qualify for targeted drug treatment, to wipe out the hidden reservoirs of malaria parasites. Targeted treatment would have considerable benefits as it would prevent the exposure of individuals without hypnozoites to the considerable side-effects of drugs such as Primaquine, which has a relatively high toxicity in G6PD-deficient patients.
Acknowledgements:
We thank the GHIT Fund (Japan) for providing the funding for this research.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T7-O2
Background:
The Punjab state of India is one of the states which consistently reporting API ≤ 1 since 2012, and has qualified for malaria elimination under category 1 as per National Framework of Malaria Elimination 2016–30. In the low transmission setting of the state such as Punjab, the number of malaria cases is relatively low but the risk of reemergence remains because of regular influx of imported malaria. In these low endemicity areas, over time, the microscopists get lesser and lesser exposure to malaria positive cases and stand at the risk of losing the finer microscopy skills. The use of machine learning (ML) based methods can strengthen malaria microscopy, and may play a crucial role in boosting malaria elimination efforts.
Materials and methods:
In the present study YOLOv8 algorithm was trained to identify the various blood stages of
Plasmodium vivax
and
P. falciparum
based on their morphology using Giemsa-stained thin peripheral blood films (PBF) of malaria positive patients. Data set for training and validation of algorithm was generated from PBF of confirmed 24 malaria positive patients. All these samples were also confirmed positive for malaria by RDT, microscopy and
18S rRNA
gene nested polymerase chain reaction (nPCR).
Results:
From the dataset, a total of 617 images were annotated for training and validation by using YOLOv8 model. In these images, a total of 16,675 cells were annotated (14066 uninfected RBC, 2208 parasitized/infected RBC, 238 WBCs, and 163 platelets). For evaluation of the model, 180 images (with 1750 normal RBCs, 511 infected RBCs) were used. The correlation between the manual count and the automated count for the detection of uninfected RBCs was 0.999 while for the detection of infected RBCs was 0.988. The ML-based tool showed a sensitivity of 97.11%, specificity of 99.8% and accuracy of 95.39% for the detection of malarial parasite, and was able to differentiate between parasite species and stages.
Conclusion:
In conclusion, the developed ML algorithm, in conjunction with an efficient image capturing system, can be efficiently used for automated detection of malaria parasite in patient samples. It may play an important role in malaria diagnosis both in high and low malaria transmission regions. The innovative and cost-effective ML based approach can improve malaria diagnosis and will ultimately strengthen the global progress towards the malaria elimination.

Background:
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) is major challenge to the 2030 malaria elimination targets of countries in the Asia Pacific region. New tools to address the large reservoir of undetectable
P. vivax
infections are required. Validated
P. vivax
serological markers are a promising tool for identifying populations and individuals at high risk of relapsing infections, thereby guiding the administration of effective treatment. To ensure the timely integration of these new approaches into policy and practice it is essential to understand country-level priorities, health systems enablers, barriers and readiness to adopt and implement new tools effectively and sustainably.
Materials and methods:
The Vivax Serology Partnership (VISPA) is undertaking collaborative policy consultations with key stakeholders to inform National Malaria Control Programs on programmatic implementation and uptake of
P. vivax
serological testing and treatment (
Pv
SeroTAT). Interviews and focus groups are being undertaken with Ministry of Health and partners in countries such as Lao PDR and the Philippines, to explore the acceptability, feasibility and health systems readiness for
Pv
SeroTAT within two use case scenarios; (1) diagnosing relapse risk to guide radical cure and (2) population screening of recent
P. vivax
infections/hypnozoite carriage for programmatic applications.
Results:
In Lao PDR,
Pv
SeroTAT has been programmatically implemented by the Centre for Malaria Epidemiology and Parasitology (CMPE) to both control epidemic outbreaks in areas with previous
P. vivax
elimination as well as to attack residual hot-spots. In the Philippines,
Pv
serological markers are being used to understand the extent of residual
P. vivax
transmission in Palawan and Sultan Kudarat. Qualitative policy consultations will explore real or perceived barriers to serological guided treatment at both the health system and community level. Themes such as leadership and planning, community awareness and information, tool introduction and regulatory processes, funding mechanisms for sustainable implementation and workforce capacity, will also be explored. These learnings will support the pathway to implementation in these countries, whilst also enhancing knowledge and evidence that can be shared with other
vivax
endemic countries.
Conclusion:
The findings will be used to inform and co-develop roadmaps for country-specific use cases and adoption including possible test and treat approaches and critical evidence required to drive decision making and uptake of the
Pv
SeroTAT
.
T7-P1 Comparison of diagnostic performance between hemozoin-based magneto-optical detection (Hz-MOD) assays and rapid diagnostic tests (RDTs) for malaria detection: a systematic review and meta-analysis
Vishnu Teja Nallapati
, Manoj A.R., Kavitha Saravu
Department of Infectious Diseases, Kasturba Medical College, Manipal, Manipal Academy of Higher Education, Manipal, Karnataka, India
Correspondence:
Kavitha Saravu (kavitha.saravu@manipal.edu)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T7-P1
Background:
To effectively treat, control, and eliminate malaria, it is crucial to have access to accurate, affordable, and portable diagnostic tests for field use. However, the existing diagnostic tools have limitations due to low parasite density, the deletion of diagnostic markers (HRP2/3), and the requirement for a sophisticated laboratory. To address this problem, in recent years many researchers have developed hemozoin-based magneto-optical detection (Hz-MOD) assays for malaria diagnosis. Furthermore, various studies have assessed the diagnostic performance of Hz-MOD and rapid diagnostic tests (RDTs) for the detection of malaria [1, 2, 3]. However, no study has assessed the pooled diagnostic performance of these diagnostic modalities. Thus, in this study, we aim to compare the diagnostic performance of Hz-MOD assays and RDTs with microscopy and polymerase chain reaction (PCR) as reference standards.
Materials and methods:
PubMed, EMBASE, and Scopus were searched from January 1995 to March 2023 for studies that directly compared the diagnostic performance of Hz-MOD assays and RDTs with microscopy and PCR as reference standards. Out of the 622 records retrieved, 330 were duplicates. After excluding duplicates, 292 were screened for titles and abstracts. However, 204 studies did not fulfill the inclusion criteria. Afterwards, eighty-eight eligible studies were evaluated for full-text, and 83 were excluded. The remaining 5 studies were included in the final analysis and the results were analysed according to the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analysis for Diagnostic Test Accuracy (PRISMA-DTA) guidelines. Furthermore, this systematic review protocol was registered in PROSPERO (ID: CRD42023448780).
Results:
We identified and included 5 eligible studies with a total number of 2107 febrile patients. The sensitivity, specificity, and diagnostic odds ratio (DoR) of Hz-MOD tests compared to microscopy were 88.12% (95% CI 85.21–90.64%), 92.73% (95% CI 91.31–93.98%), and 94.59 (95% CI 68.89 to 129.88), respectively. When compared to PCR, the sensitivity, specificity, and DoR were 78.19% (95% CI 74.81–81.30%), 90.25% (95% CI 88.89–91.47%), and 33.19 (95% CI 25.75 to 42.80), respectively. When comparing RDTs to microscopy, the sensitivity, specificity, and DoR were 87.11% (95% CI 84.12–89.73%), 93.84% (95% CI 92.51–94.99%), and 102.98 (95% CI 74.79–141.81), respectively. When compared to PCR, the sensitivity, specificity, and DoR were 76.50% (95% CI 73.05–79.71%), 92.93% (95% CI 91.49 – 94.19%), and 42.79 (95% CI 32.66–56.06), respectively. The Hz-MOD assays demonstrated superior performance compared to RDTs, achieving an AUC of 0.966 versus 0.864 for RDTs compared to microscopy (Fig. 1).
Conclusion:
The Hz-MOD assays showed better-pooled sensitivities and specificities than RDTs (PfHRP2/pLDH). Moreover, Hz-MOD can be used for these diagnostic modalities and requires minimum equipment and time, making them great alternative screening tools for malaria. However, these tests cannot differentiate the species, and additional validation is required for the species identification.
Fig. 1
ROC curves for Hz-MOD and RDT compared with Microscopy as reference test
References:
Fernando D, Thota P, Semege S, Booso R, Bell D, de A. W. Gunasekera KT, et al. Evaluation of a haemozoin-based rapid diagnostic test for diagnosis of imported malaria during the phase of prevention of reestablishment in Sri Lanka. Malar J. 2022; 21:263.
Kumar R, Verma AK, Shrivas S, Thota P, Singh MP, Rajasubramaniam S, et al. First successful field evaluation of new, one-minute haemozoin-based malaria diagnostic device. EClinicalMedicine. 2020; 22:100347.
Valdivia HO, Thota P, Braga G, Ricopa L, Barazorda K, Salas C, et al. Field validation of a magneto-optical detection device (Gazelle) for portable point-of-care
Plasmodium vivax
diagnosis. PLOS ONE. 2021;16(6): e0253232.
T7-P2 Exploring novel proteins for diagnosis of
Plasmodium vivax
infection
Shradha Bhatnagar
1
, Sanjay Kumar Kochar
2
, Vishal Saxena
1
1
Molecular Parasitology and Vector Biology Laboratory, Department of Biological Sciences, Birla Institute of Technology and Sciences (BITS), Pilani, Pilani Campus Rajasthan, India;
2
Sardar Patel Medical College, P. B. M. and Associated Group of Hospitals, Bikaner, Rajasthan, India
Correspondence:
Vishal Saxena (vishalsaxena@pilani.bits-pilani.ac.in)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T7-P2
Background:
Malaria continues to pose a significant global health threat, demanding effective control and elimination strategies. Rapid diagnostic tests have revolutionized diagnosis but primarily focus on
Plasmodium falciparum
, neglecting other species. Current RDTs face limitations like gene polymorphism and false positives. Improved RDTs need to be more sensitive, robust, and affordable. This study aims to identify novel membrane parasite proteins with high species specificity, low polymorphism, abundant production, and strong antigenic properties. These proteins could form the basis for next-generation RDTs, improving malaria diagnosis and control efforts.
Materials and methods:
This study utilizes a multi-step
in-silico
approach to identify potential
Plasmodium vivax
biomarkers. An extensive literature survey was performed to identify probable candidate genes and proteins. The sequences of these genes and their respective protein were retrieved from databases. Bioinformatics analyses included sequence blast, multiple sequence alignments, transmembrane domain prediction, and B-cell epitope prediction. Gene amplifications and biochemical analyses aimed to identify proteins with high species specificity, low polymorphism, abundant production, and strong antigenicity. Synthetic peptides were designed from the shortlisted protein sequences and tested against field isolate for antibody titer.
Results:
Preliminary analysis of
Plasmodium vivax
gene and protein sequences from NCBI and PlasmoDB databases revealed about 5 potential biomarker candidates. These proteins were named Candidate proteins I–V. The in-silico analysis indicated their high specificity for
P. vivax
. Gene amplifications from 20 field isolates exhibited high conservation (~ 94–96%) among all isolates but minimal (~ 24–26%) cross-species conservation. Synthetic peptides were injected into the Swiss albino mice to test their antigenicity, and ELISA was performed to detect the same. Candidate protein III (CpIII) gave best antibody titre. CpIII peptides were also tested against the
P. vivax-infected field isolate samples, which showed the presence of antibodies against the
CpIII peptide.
Conclusion:
Identifying novel biomarkers for
Plasmodium vivax
is crucial for improving malaria diagnosis and control efforts. As highlighted in research on the proteomic analysis of urine from patients with
P. vivax
malaria (Fantin et al., 2022), there is a distinct difference in protein expression between
P. vivax
and
P. falciparum
. This distinction underscores the need for species-specific diagnostic tools, as current RDTs primarily focus on
P. falciparum
. Discovering and utilizing unique
P. vivax
biomarkers will enable the development of more accurate and reliable RDTs, ultimately contributing to more effective treatment and control of this prevalent malaria parasite.
Acknowledgments:
BITS Pilani for fellowship and infrastructural facilities S. P. Medical College and P. B. M. Hospital Staff for clinical support during sample collection.
T7-P3 Extracellular Vesicles: biomarkers in severe/uncomplicated malaria infection
Shreya Bhatnagar
1
, Shilpi Garg
1
, Sanjay Kochar
3
, Anupama Mittal
2
, Ashis Kumar Das
1
Molecular Parasitology and Systems Biology Laboratory, Department of Biological Sciences, Birla Institute of Technology and Sciences (BITS), Pilani, Pilani Campus Rajasthan, India;
2
Department of Pharmacy, Birla Institute of Technology and Sciences, Pilani, Rajasthan, India.
3
Sardar Patel Medical College, P. B. M. and Associated Group of Hospitals, Bikaner, Rajasthan, India
Correspondence:
Ashis Kumar Das (adas@pilani.bits-pilani.ac.in)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T7-P3
Background:
Malaria remains a significant global health concern, causing immense illness and death worldwide. Despite extensive efforts, shortcomings in early detection exacerbate infection severity, prompting the need for more precise diagnostic biomarkers. The intricate pathophysiology of the
Plasmodium
parasite remains incompletely understood. Recent studies have highlighted the pivotal role of extracellular vesicles (EVs) in mediating intercellular communication during malaria infection [1]. Secreted by various cell types, including infected red blood cells in bodily fluids, carrying crucial biomolecules and play a vital role in regulating the balance between host and parasite factors [2]. Identifying these molecules as biomarkers from patient plasma could enable the development of minimally invasive diagnostic tools for malaria. Species-specific biomarkers for
Plasmodium
infections are sparsely known. Commercially used biomarkers like HRP2 are exhibiting genomic instability in some parasite population [3]. Whereas the other known biomarker LDH, which is the only biomarker for
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
), shows cross-reactivity.
Materials and methods:
EVs were isolated from patient plasma samples by ultracentrifugation in combination with SEC-based column purification. Physical characterization was performed via particle analyzer/zetasizer, electron microscopy [SEM/TEM] analysis and protein identification, quantification was performed by Western blot analysis for known EV markers of lysed EVs. Followed by mass spectrometry-based label-free proteomic analysis of the patient samples (Fig. 1). Group of samples included a combination of severe and uncomplicated manifestations from
Plasmodium vivax
infected patients along with healthy individuals as control. The proteomic profiles obtained were compared with reference proteome [
Plasmodium vivax
Sal 1 strain]. Functional annotation was performed on the data followed by pathway enrichment analysis. Further statistical analysis of data was performed to filter out potential biomarker candidates.
Results:
Vesicular structure of EVs was observed by TEM analysis followed by particle analysis of samples, giving us peaks in the range [150–400 nm], expected to include both exosomes and microvesicles, broadly referred to as EVs.
Discovery phase mass spectrometric data from
P. vivax
infected plasma samples concluded an overall proteome coverage of 1239 human and 100 parasite proteins. Interestingly, changes in host proteins amongst severe versus non severe samples, were also observed, which could enhance diagnostic accuracy. Proteins unique to each species, present in over 70% of clinical isolates and highly abundant have been filtered, for further validation as ideal biomarker candidate. Functional annotation of proteins revealed pathways enriched in malaria patients, including essential mitochondrial membrane transport, heat shock proteins, parasite attachment and nuclear proteins etc.
Fig. 1
Detailed Methodology of Study (created with BioRender.com)
Conclusion:
Future work will involve expanding sample size and conducting targeted proteomics on a larger cohort of EV samples to validate diagnostic potential. Antibodies/designed peptides against selected candidate biomarkers can be assembled into a unique panel of diagnostic/prognostic markers for Malaria.
Acknowledgment:
The study’s proteomic targets are set for patenting, ensuring confidentiality Clinical data in the study has been collected, as per ethical approval DCG Registration No-ECR/27/SP/Inst/RJ/2013/RR-19[EC/SPMC/ECA/118]. The funding for this project is provided by BITS BioCyTiH Foundation. Clinical samples were provided by SP medical college, Bikaner, Rajasthan, after consent from every patient.
References:
Opadokun T, Rohrbach P. Extracellular vesicles in malaria: an agglomeration of two decades of research. Malaria Journal. 2021;20(1):442.
Dey S, Mohapatra S, Khokhar M, Hassan S, Pandey RK. Extracellular Vesicles in Malaria: Shedding Light on Pathogenic Depths. ACS Infectious Diseases. 2024;10(3):827–44.

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T7-P4
Background:
Single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) is a powerful technology for investigating cellular heterogeneity and is particularly valuable to study unicellular eukaryotes, such as
Plasmodium
, which often present as complex infections including mixed life stages and clones. The application of scRNA-seq to
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) has been limited to studying the liver and mosquito stages, and the late erythrocytic life stages which have only been studied in non-human primate models. This is primarily due to the various challenges associated with single-cell sequencing and working with
Plasmodium
natural infections in endemic settings.
Materials and methods:
After optimizing several sample preparation methods and validating a novel scRNA-seq technology, we applied this technology to collect
P. vivax
natural infection samples in Brazil. During validation using 4 mock natural infections and 2 in vitro culture samples, we successfully recovered 22,345
P. knowlesi
single-cell transcriptomes after doublet removal, covering all asexual blood stages and creating the most extensive
P. knowlesi
single-cell dataset to date. All samples resulted in reproducible circular UMAP projections with consistent cluster localization and high gene expression correlation, regardless of sample preparation method, which were further verified by biomarker expression and life stage annotation using the Malaria Cell Atlas
P. knowlesi
reference dataset.
Results:
We then applied the most promising sample preparation method, combining a MACS column and Plasmodipur filter, to collect and preserve 6
P. vivax
natural infection patient samples at our field site in Porto Velho, Rondônia, Brazil. These 6 samples were transferred to the laboratory where they were successfully processed and sequenced. After filtering and doublet removal, we recovered between 8000 and 15,000 cells per sample and our ongoing analysis is investigating the transcriptomic signatures of the blood stages.
Conclusion:
In conclusion, we show that it is possible to collect and successfully sequence
P. vivax
natural infection samples for single-cell sequencing in the field and present the first single-cell
P. vivax
natural infection data. These developments have the potential to fundamentally transform how we study
Plasmodium
natural infections and also can close the gap to complete a
P. vivax
cell atlas which can be used as a reference for this species and future
P. vivax
single-cell studies.
T7-P5 Identifying novel antigenic markers for improved detection of
Plasmodium vivax
malaria
Dixit B
, Pandey KC
ICMR-National Institute of Malaria Research, Dwarka, New Delhi-110077, India
Correspondence:
Pandey KC (bhuvandixit90@gmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T7-P5
Background:
Malaria is a significant global health problem with a very bleak impact on society both in terms of lifestyle and economics. In 2023 there were approximately 263 million cases and 597,000 malaria deaths worldwide. Globally,
Plasmodium falciparum
and
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
) account for the majority of cases of malaria. While
P. falciparum
is responsible for more deaths,
P. vivax
is the most widespread of all of the malaria species, can cause severe, even fatal infections and results in significant global morbidity and mortality. Accurate and prompt diagnosis and treatment of
P. vivax
malaria is vital for the control and elimination of the disease.
P. vivax
can be diagnosed using microscopy or rapid diagnostic tests (RDTs). However, compared to molecular diagnosis (real-time PCR assays), microscopy and RDTs although being cheap lacks sufficient sensitivity to detect infections with low parasite density and often fail to identify a substantial fraction of
P. vivax
infections in blood. Therefore, in this study we tried to identify novel antigenic markers which could overcome the problem of low sensitivity of
P. vivax
detection by RDTs which is based on lactate dehydrogenase. Hence, the objective was (i) To identify highly seroreactive secretory antigens in
P. vivax
malaria patients, (ii) Cloning, expression and purification of the immunoreactive
P. vivax
antigens, (iii) Raising polyclonal antibody against recombinant
P. vivax
antigens in mouse and (iv) To determine the sensitivity of the antibody generated in
P. vivax
positive patient serum samples.
Materials and methods:
• Selections of secretory
P. vivax
antigens based on high seroreactivity determined by independently reported ELISA and Protein Microarray studies. • Generation of synthetic construct of genes coding for selected
P. vivax
antigens. • Expression and purification of recombinant
P. vivax
antigens. • Raising polyclonal antibodies against purified recombinant
P. vivax
antigens in mouse. • To determine the sensitivity of the antibody generated against recombinant
P. vivax
antigens in
P. vivax
positive patient serum samples via ELISA.
Results:
• PVX_121935 and PVX_110935 are the top two highly reported seroreactive secretory proteins in independent studies of
P. vivax
positive patient samples. • Synthetic constructs of
PVX_12195
and
PVX_110935
were generated with pET-28a(+) vector backbone. • Recombinant PVX_121935 and PVX_110935 were expressed in
E. coli
and purified via Ni–NTA and size exclusion chromatography. • Polyclonal antibody is being raised in mouse against the purified recombinant PVX_121935 and PVX_110935.
Conclusion:
Prima facie, we hypothesize that the two seroreactive secretory proteins PVX_121935 and PVX_110935 might serve as novel serological markers with high sensitivity to detect
P. vivax
infections.
