Background:
Malaria is an environmentally sensitive disease, and variation in environmental conditions can lead to uneven distribution of risk across an area. Cluster-randomised controlled trials (cRCTs) for malaria interventions often span large areas with varied environmental conditions, which can affect cluster-level malaria risk. Randomisation of treatment allocation for clusters is undertaken to address and balance potentially confounding factors, which could otherwise bias trial result. To our knowledge, the impact of environmental risk factors on cRCTs, such as proximity to mosquito-breeding sites or elevation, have never been explicitly examined, or accounted for in malaria cRCTs. We hypothesize that environmentally-driven spatial heterogeneity of malaria risk could potentially bias trial outcomes, and may not be accounted for in traditional randomisation strategies. This study aims to develop methods to evaluate the impact of environmental and spatial heterogeneity of
Plasmodium vivax
on cluster balance in cRCTs. We use geostatistical simulations of baseline data from the PvSTATEM trial to explore if and how modified randomisation approaches might mitigate these impacts.
Materials and methods:
The study uses baseline data from the PvSTATEM trial in Ethiopia and Madagascar, which aims to test the effectiveness of a new intervention based on
P. vivax
serological testing and treatment (PvSeroTAT) with primaquine to target the asymptomatic reservoir of
P. vivax
infections. Cross-sectional baseline surveys have been completed. PCR and serological diagnostics, demographic information and household GPS coordinated were collected from potential clusters in each study site. Satellite-derived environmental and spatial data (e.g., land use, climate, topography) has been aggregated for both study sites. We used geostatistical Bayesian models to quantify
P. vivax
spatial heterogeneity, and the key environmental and spatial parameters which determine this heterogeneity. This information was applied to parametrise simulations of plausible spatial distributions of
P. vivax
in each study site. These simulations provide prevalence surfaces against which different traditional and novel strategies for randomisation can be evaluated.
Results:
The baseline surveys were completed in the study sites in Ethiopia and Madagascar between February and September 2024. 34 potential trial clusters in Ethiopia and 33 in Madagascar were surveyed. PCR diagnostics for 3570 participants from Madagascar, and 7551 from Ethiopia were collected and have been processed. The geostatistical simulations of
P. vivax
prevalence surfaces for each site have been developed and will be presented.
Conclusion:
This study addresses a critical gap in malaria cRCT design by accounting for environmental-spatial heterogeneity, a known but often overlooked factor in exposure risk. Environmental and spatial heterogeneity in cRCTs for malaria may lead to biases in the balance of disease outcome at the beginning of the trial or impact the trial outcomes. This study will develop methods to evaluate traditional and novel randomisation methods using geostatistical simulations of the spatial distribution of
P. vivax
in both study sites. We will investigate whether randomisation methods which account for environmental and spatial risk factors could enhance cluster balance and trial outcomes. We expect that the results may inform the development of methods which can improve study validity and ensure more accurate assessments of intervention efficacy. By exploring geostatistical methods and novel randomisation techniques, this work highlights a potential pathway to improve the validity of cRCT outcomes, particularly for diseases influenced by environmental factors across diverse settings.
T8-O2 Examining the causal effect of climatic variables on malaria cases in Tripura through the time series regression analysis: A case study for Dhalai district
Pandhare S
1
, Chougale P
1
, Gogoi U

, Borgohain A

, Pal-Bhowmick I

, Ananathkumar U

, Phuleria H

2

3
NESAC Shillong, Meghalaya, India
Correspondence:
Pandhare S. (pandhare.05@gmail.com)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T8-O2
Background:
Malaria incidences across India are steadily declining; however, certain regions, specifically the northeast, contribute a greater burden, driven by the interaction of ecological and climatic factors. The present study aims to investigate the causal effect of meteorological and climatic variables such as rainfall, humidity, elevation, normalized difference vegetation index (NDVI), population etc. on the malaria cases. Moreover, it is of interest to examine the seasonality in the malaria cases since malaria cases reach the peak during monsoon season.
Materials and methods:
We analyze the total positive malaria cases observed monthly between January 2014 and December 2023 at about 120 public health centers in Dhalai district of Tripura. We systematically estimate the trend and the seasonality using time series modeling techniques such as additive decomposition, Holt-Winters exponential smoothing and locally weighted regression models with seasonal covariates. The trend in the cases during monsoon seasons is investigated along with the careful analysis of the outbreaks. The forecasting performance of these methods is compared using crossvalidation. We then include the climatic variables as the exogenous covariates in the seasonal regression models. The statistical significance and the contribution of each of these variables is investigated. The role of these factors in the malaria outbreak in 2018 in Tripura is also examined.
Results:
The malaria cases are seen to follow the clear seasonal patterns. As is seen in Fig. 1, the cases tend to peak between July and September and start declining after October/November of every year. Holt-Winters smoothing and local regression with seasonal covariates provide the optimum forecasts of the cases as depicted in Fig. 2. Seasonal regression models are seen to yield the smallest forecast mean squared error (training FMSE = 231.61, testing FMSE = 304.21). An extensive association (regression) modeling reveals that the rainfall and the humidity are major contributing factors in the 2018 malaria outbreak. There is seen to be a significant positive causal association between rainfall and cases (p < 0.05). Temperature and NDVI are also observed to be the significant predictors of the malaria cases.
Fig. 1
Time series plot of the monthly cases aggregated for the years 2014–2023
Fig. 2
Actual and estimated cases: Holt-Winters Smoothing and local regression seasonal covariates
Conclusion:
The pre- monsoon and monsoon seasons cause a rise in malaria cases. Moreover, the climatic and meteorological variables have a significant association with malaria cases. The outbreak analysis reveals the significant contribution of climatic factors on malaria cases.
Acknowledgments:
We are grateful to Dhalai District, Tripura authorities for carrying out the study and providing the supporting data. We acknowledge Mr Varun Shende and Mr Jayanta Debnath and Mr Bishal Debnath for helping in data collation. We also acknowledge the Bill and Melinda Gates Foundation for availing the grants for the project.
T8-O3 Assessing the impact of climate variables on malaria incidence in coastal Karnataka, India
Gowthami Arumugam
, Prashanth Kotthapalli, Kavitha Saravu
Department Of Infectious Diseases, Kasturba Medical College, Manipal, Manipal Academy of Higher Education, Manipal, India
Correspondence:
Kavitha Saravu (kavitha.saravu@manipal.edu)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T8-O3
Background:
Malaria is regarded as a climate-dependent disease, as it is directly and indirectly influenced by climate change. The incidence, transmission rate, and duration of malaria can increase significantly, even with a small change in global climate [1]. Hence, understanding the association between climate variables and malaria incidence is essential for effective control measures, especially as the country is on a mission to eliminate malaria by 2030 [2]. The coastal region of Karnataka, an endemic zone for malaria, particularly
Plasmodium vivax
, exhibits seasonal transmission patterns influenced by climate factors [3]. In this study, we aim to assess how climate variables affect malaria incidence in the two malaria-endemic districts of Karnataka.
Materials and methods:
A retrospective study was conducted to determine the association between climate variables and malaria case occurrence in the two coastal districts of Karnataka, Dakshina Kannada (D.K) and Udupi from 2019 to 2023. Data on climate parameters such as monthly maximum and minimum temperature, rainfall, and relative humidity were obtained from the Indian Meteorological Department (IMD), Bangalore. Similarly, monthly malaria case data were obtained from the District Malaria Office of two districts. The collected data were entered into Microsoft Excel and analyzed using the statistical software GraphPad Prism. Descriptive statistics were used to summarize trends in malaria incidence and climatic factors. Correlation analysis was performed to assess relationships between climate variables and malaria incidence to identify significant associations and patterns throughout the study period. The data were visualized using bar graphs and trend lines to illustrate correlations and potential seasonal effects on malaria transmission.
Results:
The study’s findings reveal that
Plasmodium vivax
(
P. vivax
or
Pv
) was the predominant malaria species in both rural and urban settings, representing 86% (n = 4688) of the total cases across the two districts, while
Plasmodium falciparum
(
Pf
) accounted for 17% (n = 927). Seasonal variation in the data showed the highest malaria prevalence between June and September, with the lowest prevalence occurring in February. The data also indicated that as rainfall and relative humidity increased, there was a corresponding decrease in temperature. Statistical analysis demonstrated a strong positive correlation between rainfall and malaria cases in D.K. (p = 0.0025, 95% CI 0.35 to 1.22). In contrast, Udupi showed a weak positive correlation between rainfall and malaria cases (p = 0.25, 95% CI − 0.29 to 1.02). However, relative humidity was positively correlated with malaria cases in both D.K. (p = 0.003, 95% CI 0.31 to 1.21) and Udupi (p = 0.034, 95% CI 0.06 to 1.17), indicating that higher humidity levels are associated with an increase in malaria cases. In addition, correlation between mean temperature and malaria cases was negative in both districts.
Conclusion:
The study emphasizes
P. vivax
as the predominant malaria species in coastal Karnataka and highlights the crucial link between climate factors and malaria transmission. The findings underscore the necessity of incorporating climatic data into public health planning and intervention strategies to mitigate malaria outbreaks during peak seasons. Continuous monitoring and interventions along with effective management of environmental factors are crucial to reduce the burden of
P. vivax
in endemic regions.
References:
Hoshen M B, Morse A P, A weather driven model of malaria transmission. Malar J. 2004; 3:32.
Chalageri VH, Marinaik SB, Nath SN, Singhal R, Rawat S, Ravikumar K, Shariff M, Eapen A. Malaria control—lessons learned from trends of Malaria indices over three decades in Karnataka, India. Malar J. 2023; 22(1):353.

T8-O4 The impact of climate change on malaria incidence in Bonaberi and Tombel Districts, Cameroon
Sharlot Fosah Ennih

, Amelie D. Mbouna

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T8-O4
Background:
The accelerating pace of global warming has led to unprecedented changes in climate patterns worldwide, posing serious threats to public health, particularly through vector-borne diseases like malaria. Malaria transmission dynamics are directly influenced by temperature, precipitation, and humidity, all of which are impacted by climate change. The populations residing in wetland regions, where human activities are intensifying, face a heightened risk of malaria, as these areas support Anopheles mosquito breeding sites. Understanding the correlation between climate change and malaria incidence is essential for devising effective public health interventions and preventive measures, particularly in climate-sensitive regions. This study investigates malaria incidence trends and the projected impact of climate change on populations in Bonaberi and Tombel, two districts in Cameroon that present distinct climatic conditions and ecological landscapes.
Materials and methods:
Data on observed PR were obtained from the National Malaria Program, reflecting current malaria prevalence in Bonaberi and Tombel. Climate variables, including precipitation, were sourced from the Famine Early Warning Systems Network (FEWS NET), while temperature data were derived from the ECMWF ERA-Interim reanalysis, providing a comprehensive climate profile for these areas. To simulate the current and projected PR under different climate scenarios, we employed the VECTRI model, a malaria transmission model designed to incorporate climatic and ecological data. Model simulations used CORDEX projection data to examine two representative concentration pathway (RCP) scenarios: RCP2.6, reflecting a low-emission future, and RCP8.5, a high-emission scenario indicative of significant warming.
Results:
Our results indicate substantial variations in PR across the two regions. In Tombel, the observed PR peaked at 0.8, while Bonaberi exhibited a higher PR, reaching 0.9. These results highlight that Bonaberi, likely due to its wetland environment, experiences higher malaria incidence under current climate conditions. Future projections reveal that PR in both areas is expected to increase, particularly under the high-emission RCP8.5 scenario. In Bonaberi, PR could reach as high as 0.95, suggesting intensified malaria transmission risks if global emissions remain high. In Tombel, the PR projection peaks at 0.9 under both scenarios, indicating a consistent, climate-driven increase in malaria incidence.
Conclusion:
These findings suggest that climate change may exacerbate malaria transmission in Bonaberi and Tombel, with Bonaberi exhibiting higher vulnerability under both current and projected conditions. Increased temperatures and precipitation, particularly under RCP8.5, are likely to create favorable conditions for mosquito breeding and transmission cycles. These insights underscore the importance of incorporating climate projections into national malaria control programs, enabling more targeted interventions for high-risk areas. By integrating climate data with malaria transmission modeling, public health officials can better prepare for future malaria challenges, ultimately contributing to more resilient health systems in the face of climate change.
T8-O5 Considering the impacts of humidity on the thermal performance of
Anopheles stephensi
in a changing climate
Britny Johnson

, Michael Wimberly

, Ajeet Mohanty

6

Britny Johnson (blj54@cornell.edu)
Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T8-O5
Background:
Climate change is reshaping mosquito habitats, with rising temperature and extreme weather events transforming landscapes and influencing the distribution and behavior of disease vectors like
Anopheles stephensi
[1]. Mosquito fitness, population dynamics, and pathogen fitness are shaped by various environmental factors, with temperature and water availability being key drivers of distribution and abundance [2,3]. Yet our understanding of how humidity affects disease dynamics are largely unexplored. Here, we aim to explore the effects of relative humidity on the temperature relationship of mosquito fitness traits relevant for transmission in the
Anopheles stephensi
-
Plasmodium falciparum
system with potential applications for other mosquito-malaria systems.
Materials and methods:
To expand our understanding of how relative humidity affects the temperature-malaria transmission relationship we measured three key mosquito life history traits relevant for transmission (biting rate, lifespan, and eggs per female per day) in a longstanding colony of
An
.
stephensi
adults. 3–5-day old, mated females were distributed individually into mesh-covered cups and provided an oviposition site. They were maintained on a 12:12 light: dark cycle in Percival incubators in a full- factorial design across seven constant temperatures and five constants relative humidities. Females were checked every day for 60 days for mortality and egg production. Additionally, biting rate was assessed by offering females a bloodmeal once per day and recording if blood was imbibed. We then used Bayesian hierarchical modeling, assuming uninformative priors constrained to biologically reasonable values, to fit thermal performance curves to each relative humidity.
Results:
Adult lifespan and eggs per female per day varied significantly with temperature and relative humidity. For lifespan, there was no significant shift in the thermal minimum and an overall increase in the thermal optimum (~ 1.5 °C) and thermal maximum (~ 2.2 °C) (Fig. 1a). For eggs per female per day, there was a significant increase in the thermal minimum (~ 5 °C), thermal optimum (~ 5 °C), and thermal maximum (~ 3.5 °C) (Fig. 1b). Biting rate showed no significant shift in the thermal minimum, optimum, or maximum (Fig. 1c).
Conclusion:
Here we demonstrate that relative humidity is likely to alter malaria transmission for
An. stephensi
through changes in the temperature-trait relationship of fecundity and adult lifespan. The strongest effects are seen on lifespan (Fig. 1a) which is likely to have consequences for increased transmission risk in areas where relative humidity is high, and mosquitoes are surviving the extrinsic incubation period. Additionally, our results suggest egg production will increase in less humid environments compared to more humid environments (Fig. 1b). These observed effects could have implications for transmission during the dry season where mosquito populations are expected to be reduced.
Fig. 1
Thermal performance curves for (
a
) adult lifespan, (
b
) egg rates, and (
c
) biting rates, estimated using Bayesian methods. The central lines represent the median of the posterior distribution. Shaded areas indicate the 95% credible interval, reflecting the uncertainty in the estimated thermal performance across different relative humidities
Acknowledgements:
We thank Jared Skrotzki for maintaining the Cornell
Anopheles
colony and the National Institutes of Health R01 for funding. We also thank our NIMR, AMC, SMC, and One Health Trust collaborators and insect collectors from Surat and Ahmedabad, India, for their valuable contributions to this study.

Anopheles gambiae
and
Anopheles stephensi
. Ecology. 2022;103(8): e3685.

T8-O6 Specialized hybrid models combining trend and seasonality components with machine learning algorithms provide accurate forecasting of malaria incidence in India
Syed Shah Areeb Hussain

, Chander Prakash Yadav

, Ajeet Kumar Mohanty4, Suchi Tyagi

, N. M. Anoop Krishnan

7

Malaria Journal
2025,
24(Suppl 1):
T8-O6
Background:
Climate change may disrupt malaria seasonality which could hamper efforts to achieve malaria elimination by 2030. Early warning and short-term forecasting of malaria will, therefore, be vital for effective resource allocation. In this study, we develop specialized forecasting models for malaria based on environmental covariates, and evaluate their robustness in different parts of India with varying climatic conditions and malaria endemicity.
Materials and methods:
District wise monthly malaria incidence data for at least 10 years was obtained from six states of India—Odisha, Himachal Pradesh, Gujarat, Nagaland, Punjab and Goa. The most important predictors for malaria transmission among 25 climatic and ecological variables were identified for each of these states using a recursive feature elimination algorithm and Shapley Additive Explanations (SHAP). To compare their forecasting accuracy, three simple regression models (Linear, elastic net and Poisson regression), three time-series models (ARIMA, SARIMA and SARIMAX), three machine-learning models (Support vector machines, random forest, extreme gradient boosting), two deep-learning models (artificial neural networks and long-short term memory) and five specialized hybrid models combining machine learning, deep learning and time-series models were trained and then tested on data spanning from 2001 to 2021. Standardized root means square error (RMSE) and mean absolute error (MAE) values were used to evaluate the forecasting accuracy of each model.
Results:
Climatic and ecological factors influencing malaria vary by region, depending on the Koppen climate classification. Among simple regression models, linear regression model performed fairly well (RMSE 0.1–1.5), but failed to capture the peaks, indicating additional non-linear features in the data. Time-series models performed well when seasonality was included (RMSE 0.11–2.66), though adding exogenous variables did not always improve performance (RMSE 0.22–2.54), and confidence intervals of model forecasts were very large. Machine learning models provided comparable results to time-series models with greater consistency (RMSE 0.2–2.4), while deep learning models tended to overfit and failed to forecast accurately (RMSE 0.3–4.19). To combine time-series features with machine learning or deep learning models we built specialized hybrid models which produced highly accurate and precise forecasts, in particular the SVM-ARMA and XGB-ARMA models (RMSE 0–0.44), making them ideal for routine epidemiological analysis and resource allocation.
Conclusion:
Integrating time-series components into machine learning models harnesses the strengths of both approaches resulting in a substantial enhancement in accuracy and precision of forecasts. This technique has potential for wider use in planning malaria elimination, and routine epidemiological data analysis.
